MANOVA

SEM

Structural Equation Modelling. Measurement + structural model. Mediation, moderation. CFI, TLI, RMSEA, SRMR.

Topic 1: MANOVA

Multivariate Analysis of Variance — testing whether groups differ on a linear combination of two or more correlated continuous dependent variables simultaneously.

≥2 DVs≥2 GroupsWilks' ΛBox's MEffect Size η²
🎯

What is MANOVA?

▼

Multivariate analysis of variance (MANOVA) is simply an ANOVA with several dependent variables. MANOVA is a statistical method to test the hypothesis that one or more IV or factors, have an effect or influence on a set of two or more DVs. MANOVA tests for the difference in two or more vectors of means.

Instead of a univariate F value, we obtain a multivariate F value (e.g., Wilks' λ) based on a comparison of the error variance/covariance matrix and the effect variance/covariance matrix. The "covariance" is included because the two measures are probably correlated and we must take this correlation into account when performing the significance test.

DVs should be used together as a set (cross products), which should be biologically plausible and theoretically related (or with some degree of correlation) — decided at design stage. DVs should be "moderately correlated" because they measure separate aspects of the influence (variances) of the IV.

✅ Advantages over multiple ANOVAs

  1. Reduces experiment-wise Type I error — omnibus test protects inflated error probability
  2. Individual ANOVAs may be non-significant, but in combination they might be — variables are more meaningful together
  3. Greater power when two or more DVs are considered in combination
  4. Takes into account the inter-correlations among DVs
  5. Tests effects of many IVs and DVs within a single analysis — saves time, space, cost

⚠️ Disadvantages

  • Substantially more complicated than ANOVA — ambiguity about which IV affects each DV
  • One degree of freedom lost for each DV added
  • Lots of outputs and modelling strategies
  • Assumptions are numerous and difficult to assess
  • "The best recommendation is to avoid using unless necessary"
ℹ️
MANOVA vs. Multiple ANOVAs: Running 3 ANOVAs at α=0.05 inflates familywise error to 1−(0.95)³ = 14.3%. MANOVA maintains α. Additionally, if DVs correlate but individual ANOVAs show no effect, MANOVA may still detect a combined effect.

Research Questions answered by MANOVA

  1. What are the main effects of the independent variables?
  2. What are the interactions among the independent variables?
  3. What is the importance of the dependent variables?
  4. What is the strength of association between dependent variables?
  5. What are the effects of covariates (→ use MANCOVA)?
📐

Test Statistics (Four Multivariate Tests)

▼
StatisticDescriptionBest Used When
Wilks' Lambda (Λ)Ratio of within-group variance to total variance. Ranges 0–1; values near 0 = groups differ greatly. Most widely used — good balance between power and assumptionsDefault choice for most situations; manipulated experimental variables
Pillai's TraceSum of variance explained by discriminant functions. Most conservative and robust to violations (small cells, unbalanced, assumption violations)When Box's M is significant; unequal/small cell sizes; assumption violations
Hotelling's TraceSum of eigenvalues of H·E⁻¹Exactly 2 groups; manipulated IV with clean design and no internal validity problems
Roy's Largest RootLargest eigenvalue only; most powerful but strictest assumptionsDVs strongly correlated; beware — sensitive to violated assumptions
💡
For most situations, all four statistics give the same conclusion. When they disagree, use Pillai's Trace as it is the most robust. If Box's M shows unequal covariance matrices → use Pillai's Trace (Mertler and Vannatta, 2002).

Effect Size: Partial Eta Squared (η²)

Cohen's benchmarks: Small: η² = 0.02 Medium: η² = 0.13 Large: η² = 0.26

In SPSS: Options → "Estimates of effect size" → provides partial eta squared (ηp²) column. Ranges 0–1. Interpret like R² in regression.

📊

One-way vs Two-way MANOVA

▼

One-way MANOVA

One categorical IV/factor with ≥2 levels/groups; ≥2 numerical DVs. Tests if levels of a single IV differ on the combination of DVs. Example: Do helmet types (Full Face, Bucket, Half Face) differ on Brain Damage Index and Conscious Level?

Two-way MANOVA

Two categorical IVs/factors (e.g., Gender × Treatment) each with varying numbers of levels; ≥2 DVs. Tests main effects of each IV and their interaction on the combined DVs. If interaction is significant → run simple effect analysis by fixing one factor.

Variable/Data Requirements

VariableRequirements
Independent Variable (IV)One (or more for two-way) categorical IV/factor with ≥2 groups/levels; each factor must be independent
Dependent Variables (DVs)≥2 numerical/continuous variables; moderately correlated (r = 0.3–0.9); biologically/theoretically related
Cell sizesMinimum 10 per DV; more cases per group than number of DVs; preferably balanced. When cell counts >30, normality and equal variances are robust/less concerning
✅

All 6 Assumptions of MANOVA

▼
#AssumptionHow to CheckIf Violated
1Multivariate normality of residuals — all DVs normally distributed; any linear combination of DVs is normalHistograms/boxplots of residuals per group; Shapiro-Wilk (n<50) per DV per group; MVN package in R (Mardia, Henze-Zirkler tests). MANOVA robust if min cell count >30Use Pillai's Trace; transform DVs if severe
2Homogeneity of covariance matrices — covariance matrices equal across all levels of IV (multivariate extension of Levene's)Box's M test (SPSS: automatic; R: biotools::boxM). Threshold: p > 0.001 (test is very sensitive — use lenient criterion)Use Pillai's Trace; investigate unequal cell sizes
3Independence of observations — scores not influenced by other subjectsReview study design. ICC if clustering suspectedUse multilevel MANOVA
4No univariate or multivariate outliersUnivariate: boxplots per DV per group. Multivariate: Mahalanobis distance; Cook's distance < 1.0 (Tabachnick & Fidell); Leverage values < 0.5Investigate outlier — remove only if data error
5Linear relationship between each pair of DVs for each group of IVScatter plot matrices per group — look for elliptical pattern (no curvilinear)Non-linearity reduces power; consider transformation
6No multicollinearity among DVs — DVs moderately correlatedPearson correlation matrix: r should be 0.3–0.9. If r < 0.3 → run separate ANOVAs instead. If r > 0.9 → multicollinearity, remove one DVRemove redundant DV or form composite measure; check VIF > 10
⚠️
Box's M: p > 0.001 is acceptable (NOT p > 0.05). The test is extremely sensitive to non-normality and almost always significant with large samples. A significant Box's M with adequate sample size and equal cells is not usually a serious problem — switch to Pillai's Trace.

R: Checking Multivariate Outliers (Mahalanobis Distance)

R
Y <- as.matrix(df[, c("y1", "y2", "y3")])
mahal <- mahalanobis(Y, colMeans(Y), cov(Y))
cutoff <- qchisq(0.999, df = ncol(Y))  # df = number of DVs
which(mahal > cutoff)  # row indices of multivariate outliers
sum(mahal > cutoff)    # count — should be ~0

SPSS: Mahalanobis Distance (Optional Advanced Check)

Run Regression → Linear using all DVs as IVs and a case ID variable as DV. In Save, check Mahalanobis Distance. Sort MD descending. Critical χ² value at p = 0.001, df = number of DVs (e.g., 3 DVs → critical value = 16.27). Cases with MD > critical value are multivariate outliers.

In practice: check Cook's Distance (save from Multivariate menu) — values < 1.0 (Tabachnick & Fidell) are acceptable. Also check Leverage values < 0.5.

📋

7-Step One-way MANOVA Procedure (Lecturer)

▼
📌
Example dataset: MANOVA_1.sav — Effect of helmet types on Traumatic Brain Injury (TBI). IV: Helmet types (Full Face, Bucket, Half Face). DVs: Brain Damage Index + Conscious Level. n = 75 MVA cases.
  1. Hypothesis statements
    H₀: There is no significant difference in TBI (measured by Brain Damage Index and Conscious Level) based on types of helmet worn by motorcyclists (3 types: full, half, and bucket).
    Hₐ: There is a significant difference in TBI in at least one helmet type.
  2. Data exploration / descriptive statistics / univariate outliers / normality
    Check n per group (adequate cell size?), boxplots for outliers, normality (histograms/Shapiro-Wilk), scatter plot of DVs showing correlation (r = 0.691 = moderately correlated → OK for MANOVA)
  3. Univariable analysis
    Run one-way ANOVA for each DV separately — screen for individual effects before MANOVA
  4. Fitting the MANOVA model
    Analyze → GLM → Multivariate; set IV as Fixed Factor, DVs in Dependent Variables. Include Options (descriptive, effect size, homogeneity). Include Post Hoc (Bonferroni) if IV has >2 levels. Read Multivariate Tests table → Wilks' Λ → overall significance, effect size, power.
  5. Checking model assumptions
    5a. Normality — histograms/boxplots of residuals (Save → Residuals)
    5b. Linearity — scatter plots of DVs by groups (elliptical pattern = OK)
    5c. Homogeneity — Box's M (p > 0.001 → OK) + Levene's (p > 0.05 per DV)
    5d. Multicollinearity — Correlate → Bivariate: r should be 0.3–0.9
    5e. Outliers — Cook's distance < 1.0; Leverage < 0.5 (sort descending in data view)
  6. Follow-up: Post-hoc tests and pairwise comparison
    If MANOVA is significant → run univariate ANOVAs per DV (Tests of Between-Subjects Effects). Use Bonferroni-corrected α = 0.05/k where k = number of DVs (e.g., 2 DVs → α = 0.025). Then run pairwise comparisons (Bonferroni) for significant DVs via GLM Multivariate → Post Hoc OR Options → Compare main effects. Report adjusted means (Estimated Marginal Means).
  7. Interpretation, data presentation, and conclusion
    Report in APA format: Descriptive table (means ± SD/CI per group per DV); MANOVA multivariate test table (Wilks' Λ, F, df, p, η²); Univariate ANOVA results per DV; Post-hoc pairwise comparisons; Profile plots.
⚠️
Critical rule: Do NOT run follow-up univariate ANOVAs if the overall MANOVA is non-significant. The omnibus test must be significant first.

Roy-Bargman Step-Down Procedure (Alternative post-hoc)

For follow-up with correlated DVs: The theoretically most important DV is analyzed individually (DV1). The next DV (DV2) is analyzed with DV1 as covariate (controls DV1-DV2 relationship). DV3 is assessed with DV1 and DV2 as covariates, etc. This controls for the relationship between DVs.

Other Approaches (Alternative to MANOVA)

  • Multiple factorial ANOVAs — lesser power than MANOVA
  • Factor analysis on DVs, then factorial ANOVA — lesser power
  • Discriminant Function Analysis (DFA) — switch DV and IV
🖥️

SPSS Step-by-Step: One-way MANOVA

▼
ℹ️
SPSS uses General Linear Model → Multivariate for MANOVA. This is the correct procedure.
  1. Go to: Analyze → General Linear Model → Multivariate
  2. Move continuous DVs (e.g., Brain_Damage_Index, Conscious_Level) → Dependent Variables box
    Move categorical IV (e.g., Helmet_Type) → Fixed Factor(s) box
  3. Click Options:
    ✓ Descriptive statistics
    ✓ Estimates of effect size (gives partial η²)
    ✓ Homogeneity tests (Box's M + Levene's)
    ✓ Observed power
    Significance level: 0.05
  4. Click Post Hoc (only if IV has ≥3 levels):
    Move IV to Post Hoc Tests box → check Bonferroni
    OR: Use Options → Compare main effects → Bonferroni (provides adjusted means)
  5. Click Save → check Unstandardized residuals, Cook's distance, Leverage values (for assumption checking)
  6. Click Plots → Add profile plots for visual inspection of group differences
  7. Click OK

Reading SPSS MANOVA Output — Key Tables

TableWhat to Look ForAction
Box's Test of Equality of Covariance MatricesSig. valuep > 0.001 → assumption met → use Wilks' Λ. p < 0.001 → use Pillai's Trace instead
Multivariate TestsRow for your IV (e.g., HELMET_TYPE) → Wilks' Lambda or Pillai's Trace → F and Sig.If Sig. < 0.05 → MANOVA significant → proceed to univariate follow-up
Levene's Test of Equality of Error VariancesSig. per DVp > 0.05 → homogeneity of variance satisfied for each DV
Tests of Between-Subjects EffectsF and Sig. for each DV under your IV rowUse Bonferroni-corrected α = 0.05/k. Only interpret if MANOVA was significant
Multiple Comparisons (Post Hoc)Mean differences, Sig., 95% CI between pairs of groups for each DVReport significant pairwise differences
Estimated Marginal MeansAdjusted means ± SE per groupReport in results table

SPSS Syntax (Alternative to GUI)

SPSS Syntax
GLM BrainDamageIndex ConsciousLevel BY HelmetType
  /METHOD=SSTYPE(3)
  /INTERCEPT=INCLUDE
  /POSTHOC=HelmetType(BONFERRONI)
  /PRINT=DESCRIPTIVE ETASQ HOMOGENEITY
  /SAVE=COOK LEVER RESID
  /CRITERIA=ALPHA(.05)
  /DESIGN=HelmetType.

Example Results from MANOVA_1.sav (Helmet Types & TBI)

Multivariate test result: There was a statistically significant difference in TBI measurements based on types of helmets, F(4, 142) = 13.559, P = 0.008; Wilks' λ = 0.826, partial η² = 0.091.

Univariate follow-up (Bonferroni-corrected α = 0.025):
• Helmet type had a statistically significant effect on Conscious Level: F(2, 72) = 7.154; P = 0.001 ✓
• NOT significant for Brain Damage Index: F(2, 72) = 2.573; P = 0.083

Assumption checks: Box's M p > 0.05 → assumption met. Cook's Distance < 0.5 (all acceptable). Leverage values = 0.04 (small, not influential). r(DVs) = 0.689 → moderately correlated → acceptable.

💻

Complete MANOVA R Script (REAAAPP Framework)

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Data format: Wide format — one row per subject; IV is categorical (≥2 groups), DVs are continuous
  • Minimum sample: n ≥ 20 per group; more cases per group than number of DVs
  • DV correlation: DVs should be moderately correlated (0.3 < r < 0.9) — run cor() first
  • No severe outliers: Check boxplots and Cook's distance before running
  • Packages: pacman, tidyverse, haven, car, MVN, biotools, emmeans, gtsummary
R — Full MANOVA Workflow
# ── Packages ──────────────────────────────────────────────
if(!require("pacman")) install.packages("pacman")
pacman::p_load(tidyverse, haven, car, MVN, biotools, heplots, rstatix, emmeans, gtsummary)

# ── R: Read ────────────────────────────────────────────────
df <- haven::read_sav("MANOVA_1.sav")  # SPSS file
# df <- read_csv("data.csv")           # CSV alternative
df <- df |> janitor::clean_names()
glimpse(df)
table(df$helmet_type)  # check group sizes

# ── E: EDA ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
skimr::skim(df)
df |> group_by(helmet_type) |>
  summarise(across(c(brain_damage_index, conscious_level),
    list(mean = mean, sd = sd, n = ~sum(!is.na(.x))), .names = "{.col}_{.fn}"))

# Correlation between DVs (should be 0.3–0.9)
cor(df[, c("brain_damage_index", "conscious_level")]) |> round(2)

# Scatter plot of DVs by group
ggplot(df, aes(brain_damage_index, conscious_level, colour = factor(helmet_type))) +
  geom_point() + geom_smooth(method = "lm", se = FALSE) +
  labs(title = "DVs by Helmet Type")

# ── A: Assumptions ─────────────────────────────────────────
Y <- as.matrix(df[, c("brain_damage_index", "conscious_level")])

# 1. Multivariate normality (Mardia's test)
MVN::mvn(Y, mvnTest = "mardia")$multivariateNormality

# 2. Box's M test (p > 0.001 acceptable)
biotools::boxM(Y, df$helmet_type)

# 3. Mahalanobis distance (multivariate outliers)
mahal <- mahalanobis(Y, colMeans(Y), cov(Y))
which(mahal > qchisq(0.999, df = 2))

# 4. Normality of residuals per group
df |> group_by(helmet_type) |>
  summarise(p_bdi = shapiro.test(brain_damage_index)$p.value,
            p_cl  = shapiro.test(conscious_level)$p.value)

# ── A: Analyse ─────────────────────────────────────────────
manova_fit <- manova(cbind(brain_damage_index, conscious_level) ~ helmet_type,
                       data = df)

# Multivariate test statistics
summary(manova_fit, test = "Wilks")    # λ = main statistic
summary(manova_fit, test = "Pillai")   # use if Box's M violated
summary(manova_fit, test = "Hotelling")
summary(manova_fit, test = "Roy")

# ── A: Assess ──────────────────────────────────────────────
# Effect size
heplots::etasq(manova_fit, test = "Wilks")

# Univariate follow-up (only if MANOVA significant)
# Bonferroni-corrected α = 0.05 / 2 DVs = 0.025
summary.aov(manova_fit)

# Post-hoc pairwise for significant DVs
pairwise.t.test(df$conscious_level, df$helmet_type, p.adjust.method = "bonferroni")

# Or via emmeans
emm <- emmeans(lm(conscious_level ~ helmet_type, data = df), ~ helmet_type)
pairs(emm, adjust = "bonferroni")

# ── P: Present ─────────────────────────────────────────────
df |> tbl_summary(
  by = helmet_type,
  include = c(brain_damage_index, conscious_level),
  statistic = list(all_continuous() ~ "{mean} ({sd})"),
  missing = "no") |>
  add_p() |> bold_labels() |>
  modify_caption("**Table 1. Descriptive Statistics by Helmet Type**")
📤 Expected Output — MANOVA R WorkflowStep 1 — Packages: ✓ All packages loaded silently (install once, load each session) Step 2 — Descriptives: # A tibble showing mean ± SD per group per DV Step 3 — Outlier check: Cook's distance values; should be < 0.5 (Tabachnick & Fidell) Step 4 — Box's M: Box's M = 7.237, F(6, 129201) = 1.158, p = 0.326 → p > 0.05 ✓ (assumption met) Step 4 — Multivariate Tests: Wilks' Lambda = 0.826, F(4,142) = 3.559, p = 0.008, partial η² = 0.091 ✓ Significant Pillai's Trace = 0.175, F(4,144) = 3.456, p = 0.010 Step 5 — Univariate (Between-Subjects): Brain damage index: F(2,72) = 2.573, p = 0.083 (not significant at Bonferroni α = 0.025) Conscious level: F(2,72) = 7.154, p = 0.001 ✓ Significant Step 6 — Post-hoc (Bonferroni): Full Face vs Bucket on Conscious Level: mean diff = 11.32, p = 0.001 ✓ Table output: gtsummary tbl formatted for publication

APA Results Write-Up Template (from Lecturer's notes)

▼
✅
Template — Significant MANOVA:

A one-way multivariate analysis of variance (MANOVA) was performed on two dependent variables: [DV1] and [DV2]. Independent variable: [types/levels of IV]. Results of evaluation of assumptions of normality, homogeneity of variance-covariance matrices (Box's M of … indicates that the homogeneity of covariance matrices across groups is assumed, F(__, __) = __, p = __), linearity, and multicollinearity were satisfactory. Overall MANOVA with Wilks' Lambda criterion, the combined DVs were significantly different by [levels/type of IV] (Wilks' Λ = ___, F(__, __) = ___, p = ___, partial η² = ___). To investigate the impact of IV on the individual DVs, a univariate F-test using a corrected alpha level of 0.025 was performed. [Describe each one-way ANOVA result]. Pair-wise comparison indicates that a significant difference was found between ___ (which mean is higher?).

Worked example (from lecture): "A one-way MANOVA revealed a significant multivariate main effect for region, Wilks' λ = 0.465, F(9, 95.066) = 3.9, p < 0.001, partial eta squared = 0.225. Power to detect the effect was 0.964."

📊

Review: ANOVA Models → MANOVA

▼

The core component of all four analyses (ANOVA, ANCOVA, MANOVA, MANCOVA) is the ANOVA. An univariate ANOVA looks for the effect of a categorical independent variable (IV) on a single numerical/continuous DV. ANOVA tests whether group means differ based on the DV.

Metric DVNon-metric DV
Metric IVRegressionDiscriminant Analysis
Non-metric IVANOVA / MANOVAχ² (Chi-Square)
ℹ️
Key F-test principles: If group means do not differ significantly → IV does not have an effect on DV. If overall IV is significant → use post-hoc tests to explore which categories have the most effect.
📚

What is MANOVA? — Definition & Key Concepts

▼
✅
MANOVA = ANOVA with several dependent variables
Tests the hypothesis that one or more IV/factors have an effect on a set of two or more DVs. Tests for differences in two or more vectors of means.

Instead of a univariate F value, MANOVA obtains a multivariate F value (e.g., Wilks' λ) based on comparison of the error variance/covariance matrix and the effect variance/covariance matrix. The "covariance" is included because the two measures are probably correlated and we must take this into account when performing the significance test.

One-Way MANOVA

1 IV/Factor → 2+ DVs

E.g., Level of Education (High School, College, Graduate Degree) → Test Score + Annual Income

Two-Way MANOVA

2 IV/Factors → 2+ DVs + Interaction

E.g., Level of Education × Gender → Test Score + Annual Income (+ interaction effect)

✅ Why use MANOVA? (7 Advantages)

  1. Reduces experiment-wise Type I error — omnibus test protects against inflated α
  2. Individual ANOVAs may be non-significant, but combination might be — variables more meaningful together
  3. Greater power when two or more DVs are considered together
  4. Takes into account inter-correlations among DVs
  5. Tests effects of many IVs and DVs within a single analysis
  6. Power depends on combination of correlation between DVs and effect size
  7. Can run multi-way models with/without covariates

⚠️ Disadvantages

  • Substantially more complicated than ANOVA — ambiguity about which IV affects each DV
  • One degree of freedom lost for each DV added
  • Lots of outputs, tabulations and modelling strategies
  • Assumptions are numerous and difficult to assess
  • "The best recommendation is to avoid using unless necessary"
📝

Hypothesis Statements for MANOVA

▼
HypothesisStatement
H₀ (Null)There is no mean difference between groups/levels in all dependent variables in combination
Hₐ (Alternative)At least one mean difference across groups/levels in at least one of the dependent variables or in combination
💡
Demo (Helmet Study):
H₀: There is no significant difference in TBI (measured by "brain damage index" and "conscious level") based on types of helmet worn by motorcyclists (Full Face, Bucket, Open Face).
Hₐ: There is a significant difference in TBI in at least one of the other types of helmet worn.
📋

Variable/Data Requirements

▼
RequirementDetails
Independent Variable (IV)One categorical IV (factor) with ≥2 groups/levels; each factor should be independent
Dependent Variables (DVs)Numerical/continuous; ≥2, which are correlated at some degree (moderate) and biologically/theoretically related
Cell sizesMinimum 10 per DV; more cases in each group than number of DVs; when cell counts >30, normality and equal variances are robust
Multicollinearityr < 0.3 → may not be meaningful (consider separate ANOVAs); r > 0.9 → partially redundant (multicollinearity); target: 0.3–0.9 (moderate)
⚠️
Cell size issues: Small (<20) and unequal cell sizes are problems. Reduced power can mean a non-significant overall MANOVA but one or more ANOVAs are significant. When cells >30, normality and equal variance assumptions are robust.
⚙️

Model Assumptions of MANOVA (6)

▼
  1. Multivariate normality of residuals — All DVs must be distributed normally. Any linear combination of DVs must be normally distributed. All subsets of variables must have a multivariate normal distribution. Visualise with histograms/boxplots of residuals, or run Shapiro-Wilk per group per DV. MANOVA is robust to violations when minimum cell counts >30.
  2. Homogeneity of covariance matrices — In MANOVA, the covariance matrices (not just variances) must be equal across all levels of the IV. Tested with Box's M test: H₀ = covariance matrices are equal across groups. If significant → assumption violated (bigger problem with small/unequal cells). If Box's M is significant, use Pillai's Trace as the multivariate statistic (most robust). Also run Levene's test for each DV univariate homogeneity.
  3. Independence of observations — Subjects' scores on DVs should not be influenced by or related to scores of other subjects. Tested with intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) if lack of independence is suspected.
  4. No univariate or multivariate outliers — Detect univariate outliers using boxplots. Detect multivariate outliers using Cook's distance (<0.5 acceptable) and Mahalanobis distance. Leverage values should be small (inspect as Cook's distance companion).
  5. Linear relationships between each pair of DVs for each group of IV — Test by plotting a scatter plot matrix for each group. Non-linear relationships reduce the power of MANOVA.
  6. No multicollinearity among DVs — DVs should be moderately correlated. r <0.3 → consider separate ANOVAs. r >0.9 → multicollinearity problem. May check VIF (>10 problematic).

📊 Box's M Decision Rule

Box's M Not Significant (p > 0.05) → Assumption met → Use Wilks' Lambda
Box's M Significant (p < 0.05) → Assumption violated → Use Pillai's Trace (most robust, conservative)
"If your data fails this assumption, you may also need to use Levene's test of homogeneity of variance to determine where the problem may lie."

📊

Multivariate Test Statistics — Which to Choose?

▼
StatisticWhen to UseNotes
Wilks' Lambda (λ)Default recommendation — most widely usedGood balance between power and assumptions. λ near 0 = good. λ = ratio of W to T (Total SSCP). "Percent of variance in DVs NOT explained by IV differences."
Pillai's TraceWhen Box's M is significant; small/unequal cells; assumptions violatedMost conservative and robust. Recommended when equal variances cannot be assumed (Mertler & Vannatta, 2002)
Hotelling's TraceManipulated (experimental) variables; very clean design; no internal validity problemsSimilar power to Wilks' in balanced designs
Roy's Largest RootDVs strongly correlatedUseful when DVs are strongly correlated but sensitive to violated assumptions
ℹ️
Interpretation of Wilks' λ: Lambda varies between 0 and 1. Near 0 = better (variance NOT explained by IV is small). Effect size: partial η² = 0.02 (small), 0.13 (medium), 0.26 (large) — same as Cohen's convention.
🖥️

SPSS Step-by-Step: One-Way MANOVA (Helmet Study)

▼
ℹ️
Dataset: MANOVA 1.sav — RQ: To study the effect of helmet types on traumatic brain injury (TBI). IV: Types of helmets (Full Face, Bucket, Open Face). DVs: Brain damage index + Conscious level index. n=75 MVA cases (motorcyclist with head-on collision), n=25 per group.
  1. Step 1: Hypothesis statements
    H₀: No significant difference in TBI (brain damage index + conscious level) by helmet type. Hₐ: At least one significant difference in TBI by helmet type.
  2. Step 2: Data exploration
    Check sample size (n=25 per group — adequate & balanced). Run Correlate → Bivariate on both DVs to confirm moderate correlation (r = 0.689, p <0.001 in helmet study — acceptable). Check boxplots/histograms of each DV per group for normality and outliers. Inspect scatter plots of DV1 vs DV2 by group.
  3. Step 3: Univariable analysis
    Run one-way ANOVA individually for each DV:
    Analyze → Compare Means → One-Way ANOVA
    This gives preliminary indication of which DVs may show group differences independently.
  4. Step 4: Fitting the MANOVA model
    Analyze → General Linear Model → Multivariate

    SPSS Multivariate Dialog Setup

    • Move DVs (Brain damage index + Conscious level index) → Dependent Variables box
    • Move IV (Types of helmet) → Fixed Factor(s) box
    • Click Model → Full Factorial (default); Sum of squares: Type III
    • Click Post Hoc → Select IV → Choose Bonferroni (if IV has >2 levels)
    • Click Options → Check: Descriptive statistics; Estimates of effect size; Observed power; Homogeneity tests → Move IV to "Display Means for"
    • Click Save → Check: Unstandardized Predicted Values; Unstandardized Residuals; Cook's distance; Leverage values

    Reading the SPSS Multivariate Tests output: Look at the row for your IV (Helmet_types). Use Wilks' Lambda row → check Value, F, df, Sig, Partial Eta Squared, Observed Power. In helmet study: Wilks' λ=0.826, F(4,142)=3.559, p=0.008; partial η²=0.091 → statistically significant.

  5. Step 5: Checking model assumptions
    After clicking Save in step 4, SPSS creates new variables (residuals, Cook's, leverage). Run:

    5a. Normality of residuals:

    Graphs → Legacy Dialogs → Histogram

    Plot residuals for each DV with normal curve overlay, panelled by IV groups. In helmet study: histograms show approximately normal residuals for both DVs.

    Analyze → Descriptive Statistics → Explore → Plots: Normality with Kolmogorov-Smirnov/Shapiro-Wilk

    5b. Linearity:

    Graphs → Legacy Dialogs → Scatter/Dot → Simple Scatter

    Plot Brain damage Index (Y) vs Conscious level index (X), rows: by helmet type. Look for elliptical pattern (linear). No curvilinear pattern → assumption met.

    5c. Homogeneity of variance-covariance:

    Box's M test: Already in SPSS output. In helmet study: Box's M=7.237, F=1.158, p=0.326 → p>0.05 → assumption met → use Wilks' Lambda.
    Levene's test: Check output table. Both DVs: p>0.05 (Brain damage: p=0.531; Conscious level: p=0.097) → assumption met.

    5d. Multicollinearity & Outliers:

    Multicollinearity: r=0.689 — moderately correlated → acceptable.
    Cook's distance: Sort descending in Data view. All values <0.5 → no influential outliers.
    Leverage: All values = 0.04 (small, not influential).

  6. Step 6: Follow-up post-hoc tests and pairwise comparisons
    Because overall MANOVA is significant (omnibus F), run multiple univariate F-tests per DV to see which DVs differ. Use Bonferroni correction: new α = 0.05 / number of DVs = 0.05/2 = 0.025.

    From Tests of Between-Subjects Effects (helmet study):

    DVFdfpSignificant at α=0.025?
    Brain damage Index2.573(2,72)0.083❌ No
    Conscious level index7.154(2,72)0.001✅ Yes

    Post-hoc pairwise comparisons (Bonferroni) for Conscious level: Full Face vs Bucket (mean diff=11.32, p=0.001) → significant. Full Face vs Open Face (p=0.155) → not significant. Bucket vs Open Face (p=0.226) → not significant.

    Run via: Analyze → GLM → Multivariate → Post Hoc (Bonferroni) OR Options → Compare main effects (Bonferroni)
  7. Step 7: Interpretation, data presentation and conclusion
    Report in APA format: Descriptive table (means ± SD per group per DV); MANOVA multivariate test table (Wilks' Λ, F, df, p, η²); Univariate ANOVA results per DV; Post-hoc pairwise comparisons; Profile plots (Estimated Marginal Means).
📊

SPSS Output Tables — What to Read & Report

▼

Table 1: Between-Subjects Factors

Confirm equal and adequate sample size in each group (n=25 per helmet type in this study).

Table 2: Descriptive Statistics

Report means and SDs for each DV by group. Full Face (brain=31.52, conscious=34.08), Bucket (brain=25.48, conscious=22.76), Open Face (brain=29.68, conscious=28.16).

Table 3: Box's Test of Equality of Covariance Matrices

Check if significant. In helmet study: Box's M=7.237, p=0.326 → NOT significant → use Wilks' Lambda.

Table 4: Multivariate Tests

For the IV (Helmet_types): Read Wilks' Lambda row. Value=0.826, F(4,142)=3.559, p=0.008, partial η²=0.091, Power=0.860. Report: "There was a statistically significant difference in TBI measurements based on types of helmets, F(4,142)=3.559, p=0.008; Wilks' λ=0.826, partial η²=0.091."

Table 5: Levene's Test of Equality of Error Variances

Check both DVs. Brain damage: F(2,72)=0.638, p=0.531; Conscious level: F(2,72)=2.409, p=0.097 → both p>0.05 → homogeneity of variance met.

Table 6: Tests of Between-Subjects Effects

This is the equivalent of running individual ANOVAs for each DV. Use Bonferroni-corrected α=0.025. Report F, df, p, partial η² for each DV.

Table 7: Post-Hoc Multiple Comparisons (Pairwise)

Report for significant DVs only. Report mean difference (I–J), SE, p (Bonferroni), 95% CI for each pair.

✍️

✅
Helmet Study Example Write-Up:
"A one-way MANOVA revealed a statistically significant difference in TBI measurements based on types of helmets, F(4,142) = 3.559, p = 0.008; Wilks' λ = 0.826, partial η² = 0.091. To investigate the impact of helmet type on individual TBI measures, a univariate F-test using a corrected alpha level of 0.025 was performed. Helmet type had a statistically significant effect on conscious level index (F(2,72) = 7.154, p = 0.001, partial η² = 0.166) but not on brain damage index (F(2,72) = 2.573, p = 0.083). Post-hoc Bonferroni comparison revealed that participants wearing full face helmets had significantly higher conscious level index (M=34.08, SE=2.117) compared to those wearing bucket helmets (M=22.76, SE=2.117), mean difference = 11.32 (95% CI: 3.98, 18.66), p = 0.001."

Topic 2: MANCOVA

Multivariate Analysis of Covariance — MANOVA extended to include one or more continuous covariates. Controls for confounding while testing group differences on combined DVs.

MANOVA + CovariatePillai's TraceAdjusted MeansHomogeneity of Regression Slopes
🎯

What is MANCOVA?

▼

The one-way multivariate analysis of covariance (MANCOVA) can be thought of as an extension of the one-way MANOVA to incorporate a covariate, OR an extension of the one-way ANCOVA to incorporate multiple dependent variables.

This covariate is linearly related to the dependent variables and its inclusion can increase the ability to detect differences between groups of an IV.

MANCOVA looks at the influence of one or more IVs on the DVs while removing the effect of one or more covariate factors. One-Way MANCOVA first conducts a regression of the covariate variables on the DV, eliminating the influence of covariates. Then the residuals are subject to a MANOVA, testing whether the IV still influences the DVs after covariate influence is removed.

Two Key Advantages

1. Explaining within-group variance: MANCOVA looks at the unexplained variance and tries to explain some of it with the covariate(s). This increases the power of the MANOVA by explaining more variability. Note: entering a weak covariate actually decreases statistical power (over-fitting).

2. Controlling confounding factors: Eliminates the covariates' effects on the relationship between IVs and DVs — an effect typically tested using MANOVA.

Typical Research Questions

  • Whether exam performances differed based on test anxiety levels, whilst controlling for revision time
  • Do knowledge and attitude scores on obesity vary by education level after controlling for age?
  • Which disease indexes are better treated by X vs Y drug after controlling for duration of disease and patients' age?
  • Do attitude and feelings scores differ between signed/not-signed donor card groups after adjustment of exposure scores?

Variable Requirements

VariableRequirements
Independent Variable (IV)One categorical IV/factor with ≥2 groups/levels; independent
Dependent Variables (DVs)≥2 numerical/continuous; moderately correlated (r = 0.3–0.9); biologically/theoretically related
Covariate (CV)≥1 numerical/continuous variable; correlated with DVs (preferably r > 0.3); reliable, decided by design, biologically/theoretically plausible; no multicollinearity among covariates

SPSS handles up to 10 covariates in MANCOVA.

✅

MANCOVA Assumptions

▼

A. All MANOVA Assumptions (apply equally)

  1. DVs measured at interval or ratio level (numerical)
  2. Multivariate normality of residuals for each group of IV
  3. Homogeneity of covariance matrices (Box's M test)
  4. Independence of observations (by design)
  5. No univariate or multivariate outliers (inspect residuals)
  6. Linear relationship between each pair of DVs in each level of IV (scatter plots)
  7. No multicollinearity among DVs (r < 0.9)

B. Additional Covariate-Specific Assumptions

AssumptionHow to CheckAction if Violated
Linearity between covariate and DVsScatter plots of covariate vs each DVTransform covariate; consider removing it
Homogeneity of regression slopes — the most critical additional assumption. The slope of the regression of DVs on covariate must be equal (not significantly different) across all groups of IVTest interaction: IV × covariate in the model. Run GLM Multivariate with interaction term (IV*covariate). If interaction is NOT significant (p > 0.05) → assumption met → remove interaction term and run final modelIf violated, MANCOVA is not appropriate; consider separate regression analyses per group or Johnson-Neyman technique
No multicollinearity among covariatesCorrelation between covariates r < 0.9; VIFRemove redundant covariate
⚠️
Critical: Always check homogeneity of regression slopes BEFORE running the final MANCOVA. Run with the interaction term (IV*Covariate) first — if non-significant, proceed with main effects model. If significant, MANCOVA assumptions are violated.
📋

7-Step One-way MANCOVA Procedure (Lecturer)

▼
📌
Example dataset: music.MANCOVA.sav — RQ: To determine whether attitude & feelings scores (combined) differ between signed vs. not-signed donor card groups after adjustment of exposure scores towards organ donation issues. IV: Donor group (signed/not-signed). DVs: Attitude score + Feelings score. Covariate: Exposure score. n = 388.
  1. Hypothesis statements
    H₀: There is no significant means difference of attitude scores and feelings scores in combination between signed donor card and not-signed donor card group after adjustment of exposure scores.
    Hₐ: There is a significant difference in means of combined attitude and feeling scores (or at least one) between donor groups after adjustment.
  2. Data exploration / descriptive statistics / normality / outliers
    Check cell sizes (balanced/adequate?), distributions of DVs and covariate, univariate outliers, correlation between DVs, correlation of covariate with DVs (r > 0.3 preferred).
  3. Fitting the MANCOVA model (with the covariate)
    Analyze → GLM → Multivariate → DVs in Dependent Variables box, IV in Fixed Factors, covariate in Covariates box. Include Options (descriptive, effect size, homogeneity). Read Multivariate Tests: preliminary main effect model with covariate.
  4. Checking interaction (between IV*covariate)
    Add interaction term: in MANCOVA dialogue, click Model → Custom → add IV, covariate, and IV*covariate interaction. Check Multivariate Tests for the interaction term. If NOT significant (p > 0.05) → assumption of homogeneity of regression slopes met → remove interaction term → run final model.
    Example: Pillai trace: F(2,383) = 0.688, p = 0.503 → NOT significant → proceed
  5. Checking model assumptions
    After removing interaction term, re-run final model. Check residuals (normality, linearity), Box's M, Levene's, multicollinearity, Cook's distance/Leverage. Note: if Box's M significant → use Pillai's Trace.
  6. Follow-up: Post-hoc tests and pairwise comparison
    If MANCOVA is significant → run univariate ANCOVA per DV. Use Bonferroni-corrected α = 0.05/k (e.g., 2 DVs → α = 0.025). Report adjusted means (Estimated Marginal Means at covariate mean). Run post-hoc pairwise via GLM Multivariate → Post Hoc or Options → Compare main effects.
  7. Result presentations
    Interpretation in text; tables (descriptive + ANCOVA summary + post-hoc); plots (not necessary if no interaction).
🖥️

SPSS Step-by-Step: One-way MANCOVA

▼
  1. Go to: Analyze → General Linear Model → Multivariate
  2. Move DVs (Attitude, Feelings) → Dependent Variables
    Move IV (DonorGroup) → Fixed Factor(s)
    Move covariate (ExposureScore) → Covariate(s)
  3. Click Model → Custom → add: DonorGroup + ExposureScore + DonorGroup*ExposureScore (for interaction check first)
  4. Click Options: ✓ Descriptive, ✓ Effect size, ✓ Homogeneity tests, ✓ Estimated Marginal Means
  5. Click OK → Read Multivariate Tests for interaction term (DonorGroup*ExposureScore)
  6. If interaction NOT significant → Go back → Model → Full factorial (remove interaction) → rerun final model
  7. Report: Pillai's Trace (if Box's M significant) or Wilks' Λ, F, df, p, partial η²

Example Results (Donor Card Dataset)

Preliminary model (with covariate): Pillai's trace: F(2,384) = 5.208, p = 0.006; partial η² = 0.026 → significant overall MANCOVA

Interaction check: Pillai trace: F(2,383) = 0.688, p = 0.503 → NOT significant → homogeneity of regression slopes assumption met → remove interaction

Univariate ANCOVA (Bonferroni α = 0.025):
• Feelings score: significant, F(1,385) = 9.149, p = 0.003, partial η² = 0.023
• Attitude score: NOT significant, F(1,385) = 3.420, p = 0.065

Post-hoc pairwise (Feelings): Not-signed donor card had significantly higher mean feelings (31.03, 95%CI 29.79–32.28) vs. signed (28.33, 95%CI 27.10–29.59); mean diff = 2.701; 95%CI 0.692–4.710; P = 0.003

SPSS Syntax

SPSS Syntax
/* Step 1: Check interaction (homogeneity of regression slopes) */
GLM AttitudeScore FeelingsScore BY DonorGroup WITH ExposureScore
  /METHOD=SSTYPE(3)
  /DESIGN=DonorGroup ExposureScore DonorGroup*ExposureScore.

/* Step 2: Final MANCOVA (if interaction NS, remove it) */
GLM AttitudeScore FeelingsScore BY DonorGroup WITH ExposureScore
  /METHOD=SSTYPE(3)
  /POSTHOC=DonorGroup(BONFERRONI)
  /EMMEANS=TABLES(DonorGroup) WITH(ExposureScore=MEAN)
  /PRINT=DESCRIPTIVE ETASQ HOMOGENEITY
  /SAVE=COOK LEVER RESID
  /CRITERIA=ALPHA(.05)
  /DESIGN=DonorGroup ExposureScore.
💻

MANCOVA R Code (REAAAPP)

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Data format: Wide format; IV categorical, DVs continuous, covariate continuous
  • Covariate requirement: Must be linearly correlated with at least one DV (r > 0.3); check with cor()
  • Covariate independent of IV: Covariate should NOT significantly interact with IV (test homogeneity of regression slopes first)
  • Minimum sample: n ≥ 20 per group; more cases per group than number of DVs + covariates
  • Packages: tidyverse, haven, car, MVN, biotools, emmeans, gtsummary
R — MANCOVA Workflow
pacman::p_load(tidyverse, haven, car, MVN, biotools, emmeans, gtsummary)

# R: Read
df <- haven::read_sav("music.MANCOVA.sav")
df <- df |> janitor::clean_names()
glimpse(df)

# E: EDA — check covariate correlation with DVs
cor(df[, c("attitude", "feelings", "exposure")]) |> round(2)

# A: Assumptions
# 1. Box's M
Y <- as.matrix(df[, c("attitude", "feelings")])
biotools::boxM(Y, df$donor_group)

# 2. Check homogeneity of regression slopes (interaction)
model_interact <- manova(cbind(attitude, feelings) ~ exposure + donor_group + exposure:donor_group,
                           data = df)
summary(model_interact, test = "Pillai")
# If interaction p > 0.05 → assumption met → proceed

# A: Analyse — Final MANCOVA (without interaction)
mancova_fit <- manova(cbind(attitude, feelings) ~ exposure + donor_group, data = df)
summary(mancova_fit, test = "Pillai")   # Pillai if Box's M violated
summary(mancova_fit, test = "Wilks")    # Wilks if Box's M not violated

# A: Assess — Univariate ANCOVA follow-up
summary.aov(mancova_fit)  # α = 0.05/2 = 0.025

# Adjusted means (EMMs at mean of covariate)
emm <- emmeans(lm(feelings ~ exposure + donor_group, data = df), ~ donor_group)
summary(emm)
pairs(emm, adjust = "bonferroni")

# P: Present
tbl_regression(lm(feelings ~ exposure + donor_group, data = df)) |>
  bold_p(t = 0.025) |>
  modify_caption("**MANCOVA — Feelings Score (Univariate ANCOVA)**")
📤 Expected Output — MANCOVA R WorkflowInteraction check (Step 1): DonorGroup:ExposureScore interaction; p > 0.05 ✓ → proceed to MANCOVA Box's M test: If significant → use Pillai's Trace as primary test statistic Multivariate test (Pillai's Trace): Pillai's Trace = 0.026, F(2,384) = 5.208, p = 0.006, partial η² = 0.026 ✓ Significant Univariate ANCOVA (with covariate adjustment): Feeling score: F(1,385) = 9.149, p = 0.003, partial η² = 0.023 ✓ Significant Attitude score: F(1,385) = 3.420, p = 0.065, partial η² = 0.009 (not significant) Adjusted means (EMM): Not-signed: Feeling = 31.03 (95% CI: 29.79–32.28) Signed: Feeling = 28.33 (95% CI: 27.10–29.59)
✍️

MANCOVA Write-Up Template

▼
✅
Interpretation Template (from Lecturer's lecture notes):

Prior to conducting MANCOVA, dependent variables were shown to be moderately correlated (Pearson r = ___; P = ___) while Box's M and Levene's tests were [significant/not significant] suggesting that homogeneity of covariance matrices and homogeneity of variances assumptions are [not met/met]. Pillai's Trace multivariate results will be used [if Box's M significant]. All assumptions suggesting MANCOVA was appropriate.

The overall MANCOVA shows that there is a significant mean difference of attitude and feelings score in combination between donor groups, with adjustment of exposure scores. MANCOVA: Pillai's trace = 0.026, F(2,384) = 5.208, p = 0.006; partial Eta Squared = 0.026.

Univariate one-way ANCOVA shows those who not signed donor card has significantly higher mean of feeling 31.03 (95%CI 29.79, 32.28), compared to those who signed 28.33 (95%CI 27.10, 29.59). F(1,385) = 9.149, p = 0.003, partial eta squared 0.023, with adjustment of exposure scores.

There is no significant mean difference of attitude between donor groups F(1,385) = 3.420, p = 0.065, partial eta squared 0.009, with adjustment of exposure scores. There is no significant interaction between donor group and exposure scores F(2,383) = 0.688, p = 0.503.

📑 MANCOVA Lecture Notes (SPSS-Based)

Learning Objectives

▼
  1. Understand, apply and appraise MANCOVA models in research — concepts, assumptions, running analysis, interpretation and presentation
  2. MANCOVA models: (a) One-way MANCOVA (demo); (b) Two-way MANCOVA (classroom exercise)
  3. Critical appraisal — issues & applications in research (assignment/SGD)
ℹ️
Pre-reading: One-Way MANCOVA in SPSS Statistics — statistics.laerd.com
📚

What is MANCOVA?

▼
✅
MANCOVA = MANOVA + Covariate(s)
One-way MANCOVA can be thought of as: (a) an extension of MANOVA to incorporate a covariate, OR (b) an extension of ANCOVA to incorporate multiple dependent variables.

MANCOVA assesses for statistical differences on multiple continuous DVs by an IV, while controlling for a third variable called the covariate. Multiple covariates can be used (SPSS handles up to 10).

One-Way MANCOVA first conducts a regression of the covariate on the DVs, eliminating the influence of the covariates. Then the residuals (unexplained variance from regression) are subjected to a MANOVA, testing whether the IV still influences DVs after covariate influence is removed.

Advantage 1: Explaining within-group variance

MANCOVA looks at the unexplained variance and tries to explain some of it with the covariate(s), thus increasing the power of MANOVA by explaining more variability in the model.
Note: entering a weak covariate decreases statistical power instead of increasing it (over-fitting).

Advantage 2: Controlling confounding factors

MANCOVA eliminates the covariates' effects on the relationship between IVs and DVs — an effect typically tested using MANOVA. This controls for confounders not manipulated in the study design.

Typical Research Questions for MANCOVA

  • Whether a few exam performances differed based on test anxiety levels amongst students, whilst controlling for revision time
  • Do the knowledge and attitude scores on obesity vary by education level after controlling for age?
  • Which disease indexes (2 or more) are better treated by either X drug or Y drug after controlling for duration of disease and patients' age?
  • Do attitude and feelings scores in combination differ between signed/not-signed donor card groups after adjustment of exposure scores towards organ donation issues? (MANCOVA demo dataset)
📝

Hypothesis Statements for MANCOVA

▼
HypothesisStatement
H₀ (Null)There is no mean difference between groups/levels in all dependent variables in combination, adjusted by covariate
Hₐ (Alternative)At least one mean difference across groups/levels in at least one of the dependent variables or in combination, adjusted by covariate
💡
Demo (Organ Donation Study):
H₀: There is no significant means difference of attitude scores & feelings scores in combination between signed/not-signed donor card group after adjustment of exposure scores towards organ donation issues.
Hₐ: There is a significant difference in means of combined attitude and feeling scores (or at least one) between donor card groups after adjustment of exposure scores.
📋

Variable/Data Requirements — MANCOVA

▼
VariableRequirements
Independent Variable (IV)One categorical IV (factor) with ≥2 groups/levels; each factor independent
Dependent Variables (DVs)Numerical/continuous; ≥2; correlated at some degree (moderate) and biologically/theoretically related
Covariate (CV)✓ At least 1 numerical/continuous variable
✓ Correlated with DVs (preferably r >0.3)
✓ Reliable, decided by design, biologically/theoretically plausible
✓ No multicollinearity among covariates
✓ Covariate uncorrelated with IV (no interaction = homogeneity of regression slopes)
Cell sizesAs in MANOVA models: minimum 10 per DV; more cases per group than number of DVs
⚙️

Model Assumptions of MANCOVA

▼

A. All MANOVA Assumptions (see T1)

  1. DVs measured at interval or ratio level (numerical)
  2. Multivariate normality (residuals for each IV group)
  3. Homogeneity of covariance matrices (Box's M Test)
  4. Independence of observations (by design)
  5. No univariate or multivariate outliers (inspect residuals, Cook's distance, Leverage, Mahalanobis distance)
  6. Linear relationship between each pair of DVs in each level of IV (scatter plots)
  7. No multicollinearity among the DVs

B. Additional Covariate Assumptions

  1. Relationship between covariate and DVs: Covariate must be linearly correlated with each DV (check Pearson's r; preferably r >0.3). Assess with correlation analysis and scatter plots.
  2. Homogeneity of regression slopes: The relationship between the covariate and each DV must be the same (parallel) across all levels of the IV. Test by adding the IV×covariate interaction term to the model — if interaction is NOT significant, homogeneity of regression slopes is assumed. If interaction is significant → assumption violated.
  3. Covariate independent of IV: The covariate should be uncorrelated with the IV (no interaction). If covariate is correlated with IV, interpretation of covariate adjustment becomes problematic.
⚠️
Box's M test in MANCOVA: If Box's M is significant → assumption violated → use Pillai's Trace (most conservative and robust) instead of Wilks' Lambda. This is what happened in the Organ Donation demo dataset (Box's M: F=6.134, p<0.001 → Pillai's Trace used).
🖥️

SPSS Step-by-Step: One-Way MANCOVA (Organ Donation Study)

▼
ℹ️
Dataset: MANCOVA donor.sav — RQ: To determine whether attitude & feelings scores in combination differ between signed/not-signed donor card groups after adjustment of exposure scores towards organ donation issues.
IV: Signed donor card (yes/no). DVs: Attitude towards organ donation + Feelings towards organ donation. Covariate: Exposure to donation issues score. n=388 (196 yes, 192 no).
  1. Step 1: Hypothesis Statements
    H₀: No significant means difference of attitude scores & feelings scores in combination between donor card groups after adjustment of exposure scores.
    Hₐ: Significant difference in means of combined attitude and feelings scores (or at least one) between donor card groups after adjustment.
  2. Step 2: Data Exploration
    Check sample size (n>20 per cell — balanced & adequate). Run Bivariate Correlations on both DVs (attitude vs feelings r=0.316, p<0.001 — moderately correlated). Check descriptive statistics by group. Inspect scatter plots and histograms of residuals.
  3. Step 3: Fitting the MANCOVA Model
    Analyze → General Linear Model → Multivariate

    SPSS Multivariate Dialog Setup

    • Move DVs (Attitude towards organ donation + Feelings towards organ donation) → Dependent Variables box
    • Move IV (Signed donor card) → Fixed Factor(s) box
    • Move Covariate (Exposure to donation issues) → Covariate(s) box
    • Click Options → Check: Descriptive statistics; Estimates of effect size; Homogeneity tests → Move IV to "EM Means" for estimated marginal means
    • Click Save → Check: Unstandardized Predicted Values; Unstandardized Residuals; Cook's distances; Leverage values
    • Click OK → Read Multivariate Tests table — use Pillai's Trace (Box's M significant)

    Preliminary Main Effect Model Result (Organ Donation Study):
    Overall MANCOVA: Pillai's trace: F(2,384)=5.208, p=0.006; partial Eta Squared=0.026. → Significant overall effect.
    Univariate results: Feelings towards organ donation p=0.003 (significant); Attitude towards organ donation p=0.065 (not significant at α=0.025).

  4. Step 4: Checking Interaction (Homogeneity of Regression Slopes)
    Analyze → GLM → Multivariate → Model → Build Terms → Select "donor*exposure" interaction

    Test the interaction between IV (signed donor card) and covariate (exposure to donation issues). If NOT significant → homogeneity of regression slopes assumption met → remove interaction term and proceed with main effect model.

    Organ Donation Study result: donor × exposure interaction: Pillai trace: F(2,383)=0.688, p=0.503 → NOT significant → assumption met → proceed with main effect model only.

  5. Step 5: Checking Model Assumptions
    Remove interaction term from model, re-run, then check residuals:

    5a. Multivariate Normality:

    Graphs → Legacy Dialogs → Histogram

    Plot residuals for each DV, panelled by IV groups. Organ donation study: some issues in normality but since data is large (n=388), normality is assumed (robust to violations with large n).

    5b. Linearity:

    Graphs → Legacy Dialogs → Scatter/Dot

    Plot attitude (Y) vs feelings (X), panelled by donor card groups. Look for elliptical pattern (linear). In study: scatter plots show linear relationship.

    5c. Homogeneity (Box's M & Levene's):

    Box's M: F=6.134, p<0.001 → significant → assumption NOT met → use Pillai's Trace.
    Levene's: Attitude: F=18.285, p<0.001 (not met); Feelings: F=0.673, p=0.413 (met).
    Since Box's M significant → use Pillai's Trace.

    5d. Multivariate Outliers:

    Cook's Distance: Sort descending. One value (1.12) >1.0 (rule of thumb) → potential outlier but leverage values are very small (<0.22) → data may not be influential. Monitor but proceed.
    Mahalanobis Distance (optional): Run MLR with DVs as IVs and case ID as DV → Save MD → Sort → Check vs critical χ² (p=0.001, df=n_DVs).

    ℹ️
    Optional: Mahalanobis Distance
    To compute in SPSS: Analyze → Regression → Linear → Enter all DVs as predictors and ID as DV → Save → Mahalanobis distance → Sort descending. Critical χ² at p=0.001 with df=2 DVs = 13.82. Any MD >13.82 → potential multivariate outlier to investigate.
  6. Step 6: Follow-up Post-Hoc Tests and Pairwise Comparisons
    Because overall MANCOVA is significant, run multiple univariate ANCOVA F-tests per DV. Apply Bonferroni correction: new α = 0.05/2 DVs = 0.025.

    From Tests of Between-Subjects Effects (Organ Donation Study):

    DVCovariate (exposure)Donor card effectSig at α=0.025?
    Attitude towards organ donationF=29.405, p<0.001F=3.420, p=0.065❌ No
    Feelings towards organ donationF=32.028, p<0.001F=9.149, p=0.003✅ Yes

    Post-hoc pairwise comparison (Bonferroni, α=0.025): For feelings towards organ donation: not-signed donors had significantly higher mean feelings score (31.034, 95%CI: 29.609, 32.460) vs signed donors (28.334, 95%CI: 26.923, 29.744), mean diff=2.701 (95%CI: 0.692, 4.710), p=0.003. No significant difference for attitude scores (p=0.065).

  7. Step 7: Data Presentation and Conclusion
    Report: adjusted means (Estimated Marginal Means) evaluated at the covariate mean value (exposure=10.39 in demo). Use APA format for tables and text.
📊

APA Results Write-Up Template (One-Way MANCOVA)

▼

Complete Interpretation Template

Prior to conducting MANCOVA, dependent variables were shown to be moderately correlated (Pearson r = ___; P = ___) while Box's M and Levene's tests were [significant/not significant] suggesting that homogeneity of covariance matrices and homogeneity of variances assumptions are [not met/met]. Pillai's Trace multivariate results will be used [if Box's M significant]. All assumptions suggesting MANCOVA was appropriate.

The overall MANCOVA shows that there is a significant mean difference of [DV1] and [DV2] in combination between [IV groups], with adjustment of [covariate]. MANCOVA: Pillai's trace = ___, F(___, ___) = ___, p = ___; partial Eta² = ___.

Univariate one-way ANCOVA shows [Group A] has significantly [higher/lower] mean of [DV1/DV2] [M=___, 95%CI ___, ___], compared to [Group B] [M=___, 95%CI ___, ___]. F(___, ___) = ___, p = ___, partial eta squared ___, with adjustment of [covariate].

There is no significant mean difference of [other DV] between [IV] groups F(___, ___) = ___, p = ___, partial eta squared ___, with adjustment of [covariate]. There is no significant interaction between [IV] and [covariate] F(___, ___) = ___, p = ___.

✅
Organ Donation Study Example (from lecture):
"Prior to conducting MANCOVA, dependent variables were shown to be moderately correlated (Pearson r=0.316; P<0.001) while Box's M and Levene's tests were significant suggesting that homogeneity of covariance matrices and homogeneity of variances (univariate) assumptions are not met. Pillai's Trace multivariate results will be used. All the assumptions suggesting that MANCOVA was appropriate.

The overall MANCOVA shows that there is a significant mean difference of attitude and feelings score in combination between donor groups, with adjustment of exposure scores. There is lower partial eta squared when covariate exposure scores are included. MANCOVA: Pillai's trace=0.026, F(2,384)=5.208, p=0.006; partial Eta Squared=0.026.

Univariate one-way ANCOVA shows those who not signed donor card has significantly higher mean of feeling 31.03 (95%CI 29.79, 32.28), compared to those who signed 28.33 (95%CI 27.10, 29.59). F(1,385)=9.149, p=0.003, partial eta squared 0.023, with adjustment of exposure scores.

There is no significant mean difference of attitude between donor groups F(1,385)=3.420, p=0.065, partial eta squared 0.009, with adjustment of exposure scores. There is no significant interaction between donor group and exposure scores F(2,383)=0.688, p=0.503."
💡
MANCOVA vs MANOVA (what changes in the interpretation):
(1) Add statement about covariate correlation with DVs; (2) Report Box's M and state Pillai's Trace used if significant; (3) Add "with adjustment of [covariate]" at end of each univariate ANCOVA result sentence; (4) Report adjusted means (EMM) not raw means; (5) Report and interpret interaction check result.
📚

Topic 3: Data Visualization

Graphical representation of information and data using visual elements like charts, graphs, and maps. Using ggplot2 to create reproducible and effective plots.

ggplot2Grammar of GraphicsChart TypesModel Plots
🎨

What is Data Visualization?

▼

Data visualization is the graphical representation of information and data. This is done using visual elements like charts, graphs, and maps. Data visualization tools provide an accessible way to see and understand trends, outliers, and patterns in data.

Objective: Data visualization tools and technologies are used to analyze massive amounts of information and make data-driven decisions.

Infographics vs. Visualization

Infographics = presentation (tell a story with designed visuals)
Visualization = exploration (see what data have to say)

Why Visualize?

  • Simple and clear
  • Has sufficient information
  • See what data have to say
  • Tell stories with data
  • Graphics broadcast; statistics narrowcast

From Data to Wisdom (Hierarchy)

LevelDescription
Unstructured information = realityRaw observations in the world
Data = records of observationsOrganized records (rows and columns)
Knowledge = perception or meaning of dataPatterns identified through analysis
Wisdom = deep understandingActionable insight with context

What Makes Good Graphics? (Edward Tufte)

  • Data substance rather than about methodology or graphic design
  • No distortion to what the data has to say
  • Presence of many numbers in a small space
  • Coherence for large data sets
  • Encourages the eye to compare different pieces of data
  • Reveals the data at several levels of detail — from broad overview to fine structure
  • Serves a reasonably clear purpose: description, exploration, tabulation, or decoration
  • Closely integrated with statistical and verbal descriptions
  • Minimalism and efficiency: complex ideas communicated with clarity, precision and efficiency
  • Gives viewers the greatest number of ideas in the shortest time with the least ink

Two Key Things to Look For

A) Pattern

Plot based on daily data — as time goes by, bumpier graph with more fluctuations. Same data based on monthly basis gives smoother plot. Choose aggregation level appropriate for your research question.

B) Relationship

Unlike statistics — relationship shown in graphs not necessarily for answering hypothesis. Used to compare distributions between groups, show trends, identify correlations.

Questions to Ask Before Making Graphs

  • Which variable or variables do I want to plot?
  • What type is that variable? (factor/categorical or numerical?)
  • Am I plotting a single variable, two variables, or three variables together?
  • What is the purpose? (description, exploration, comparison, trend, correlation)
📊

Introduction to ggplot2 — Grammar of Graphics

▼

ggplot2 is an elegant, easy, and versatile general graphics package in R. It implements the grammar of graphics concept. The advantage: it fastens the learning process and facilitates creating complex graphics.

ggplot2 Building Blocks (Always in this order)

⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Package: ggplot2 (part of tidyverse) — install once with install.packages("tidyverse")
  • Data format: Must be a data.frame or tibble — ggplot2 does NOT accept matrices directly
  • Layered grammar: Every ggplot call needs at minimum: ggplot() + aes() + at least one geom_*()
  • Aesthetic mapping: Variables must be inside aes(); fixed values (e.g., colour="red") go OUTSIDE aes()
R — ggplot2 Structure
ggplot(data = df, aes(x = var1, y = var2)) +   # 1. Data + aesthetics
  geom_point() +                                 # 2. Geometric object (what to draw)
  labs(title = "Title", x = "X label",           # 3. Labels
       y = "Y label", colour = "Legend") +
  theme_bw()                                     # 4. Theme (appearance)

Common Plot Examples

⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Packages: tidyverse (includes ggplot2 + dplyr), ggpubr (publication-ready plots), corrplot (correlation matrix), Hmisc (rcorr)
  • Bar chart: Use geom_bar() for counts; geom_col() for pre-computed values; provide fill= for grouping
  • Histogram: Only requires x aesthetic; bins or binwidth controls granularity — try both
  • Scatter + regression line: Add geom_smooth(method="lm") for linear fit; method="loess" for curved fit
  • Correlation matrix: Requires a numeric-only data.frame — remove factor/character columns first
R — Bar, Histogram, Scatter, Boxplot
pacman::p_load(tidyverse, ggpubr, corrplot, Hmisc)

# 1. Bar chart — frequency of a categorical variable
ggplot(df, aes(x = group)) +
  geom_bar(fill = "steelblue") +
  labs(title = "Count by Group", x = "Group", y = "Count") +
  theme_bw()

# 2. Histogram — distribution of continuous variable
ggplot(df, aes(x = score)) +
  geom_histogram(bins = 20, fill = "#2980b9", colour = "white") +
  labs(title = "Distribution of Score", x = "Score", y = "Frequency") +
  theme_classic()

# 3. Box plot — compare distributions by group
ggplot(df, aes(x = group, y = score, fill = group)) +
  geom_boxplot() +
  geom_jitter(width = 0.1, alpha = 0.4) +      # add raw data points
  labs(title = "Score by Group") +
  theme_bw() + theme(legend.position = "none")

# 4. Scatter plot — relationship between two continuous variables
ggplot(df, aes(x = var1, y = var2, colour = group)) +
  geom_point(alpha = 0.6) +
  geom_smooth(method = "lm", se = TRUE) +
  labs(title = "Scatter: var1 vs var2") +
  theme_minimal()

# 5. Line plot — trend over time
ggplot(df, aes(x = time, y = value, group = id, colour = group)) +
  geom_line(alpha = 0.5) +
  geom_point() +
  labs(title = "Trend Over Time") +
  theme_bw()

# 6. Correlation matrix plot
num_vars <- df |> select(where(is.numeric))
cor_mat  <- cor(num_vars, use = "complete.obs")
corrplot::corrplot(cor_mat, method = "color", type = "upper",
                   addCoef.col = "black", tl.cex = 0.8, number.cex = 0.7)

# 7. Faceted plots — multiple panels by group
ggplot(df, aes(x = score)) +
  geom_histogram(fill = "steelblue") +
  facet_wrap(~ group) +
  labs(title = "Distribution of Score by Group") +
  theme_bw()

# 8. Mean ± SD bar chart (for MANOVA results)
df |>
  group_by(group) |>
  summarise(mean = mean(score, na.rm = TRUE),
            sd   = sd(score, na.rm = TRUE),
            n    = n(),
            se   = sd/sqrt(n)) |>
  ggplot(aes(x = group, y = mean, fill = group)) +
  geom_col(width = 0.6) +
  geom_errorbar(aes(ymin = mean - se, ymax = mean + se), width = 0.2) +
  labs(title = "Mean ± SE by Group", y = "Mean Score") +
  theme_bw()
📤 Expected Output — Common ggplot2 Chart Typesgeom_bar(): Bar chart window opens — each category bar height = count geom_histogram(): Distribution shape visible — check for normality visually geom_point() + geom_smooth(): Scatter with regression line ± shaded confidence band Console: `geom_smooth() using formula = y ~ x` geom_boxplot(): Box (IQR), whiskers (1.5×IQR), dots (outliers) corrplot(): Colour matrix — blue = positive correlation, red = negative → Save plot with: ggsave("plot.png", width=8, height=6, dpi=300) → If blank plot appears: check data has no NAs in mapped variables
📤 Expected Output — ggplot2 Layer Structure→ Each + adds a new layer on top of the previous → Order matters: geom layers drawn in order added → theme_bw() / theme_minimal() = clean academic publication style → Common error: "object not found" → check column names match exactly (case-sensitive) → Scale_*() functions control axis labels, colours, breaks, limits

SPSS Chart Builder — Key Charts

▼

Creating Charts in SPSS

  1. Go to: Graphs → Chart Builder
  2. From the Gallery tab, select chart type (Bar, Line, Scatter, Histogram, Boxplot)
  3. Drag variables to the X and Y axis drop zones
  4. For grouped charts, drag grouping variable to "Cluster on X" or "Color" zone
  5. Click OK to generate
  6. Double-click the chart in output to open Chart Editor for formatting

Common SPSS Charts by Purpose

PurposeChart TypeSPSS Path
Distribution of continuous variableHistogramGraphs → Chart Builder → Histogram
Compare groups on continuous DVBar chart (error bars) or BoxplotGraphs → Chart Builder → Bar or Boxplot
Relationship between two continuous variablesScatter plotGraphs → Chart Builder → Scatter/Dot
Trend over timeLine graphGraphs → Chart Builder → Line
MANOVA profile plotsProfile plotIn GLM Multivariate → Plots menu
Normality checkQ-Q plot + histogramAnalyze → Descriptive → Explore → Plots → Normality plots with tests
Residual plotsScatter of residuals vs. fittedSave residuals from GLM → then Graphs → Scatter

Legacy Dialogs (Alternative to Chart Builder)

Graphs → Legacy Dialogs → [Bar / Line / Scatter / Histogram / Boxplot / Error Bar]

Legacy Dialogs are simpler for standard charts. Chart Builder offers more customisation options.

Model Visualization in R

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Requires a fitted model object: e.g., lm(), glm(), lmer(), coxph() — run your model first
  • jtools: Best for simple lm()/glm() models; summ() gives tidier output than summary()
  • sjPlot: Works with lm, glm, lmer, coxph — tab_model() generates HTML regression table
  • ggeffects: Plots predicted/marginal effects — specify terms as the variable(s) to visualise on x-axis
  • modelsummary: Ideal for comparing multiple models side-by-side in one table
R — Model Plots (jtools, sjPlot, ggeffects)
pacman::p_load(jtools, sjPlot, ggeffects, modelsummary)

# jtools: summarise and visualize regression models
library(jtools)
fit <- lm(score ~ age + bmi + group, data = df)
summ(fit)                                         # neat model summary

# Effect plot — predicted values with CI for one predictor
effect_plot(fit, pred = age, interval = TRUE, plot.points = TRUE)

# Coefficient plot
plot_summs(fit)

# sjPlot: publication-ready model output plots
library(sjPlot)
plot_model(fit, type = "est",          # forest plot of coefficients
           show.values = TRUE, p.adjust = "none")

# sjPlot: LMM results table
tab_model(fit, show.re.var = TRUE, show.icc = TRUE)

# ggeffects: marginal effects / predicted values
library(ggeffects)
eff <- ggpredict(fit, terms = "age")
plot(eff)

# modelsummary: compare multiple models
library(modelsummary)
modelplot(fit)                                   # visual coefficient plot
modelsummary(list("Model 1" = fit))             # table comparing models

# SEM path diagram (semPlot — covered in Topic 8)
# semPlot::semPaths(fit, what = "std", style = "lisrel")

Key references (from lecture): R Graphics Cookbook (r-graphics.org); ggplot2 documentation (ggplot2.org); R4DS data visualisation chapter (r4ds.had.co.nz); ggplot2-extensions gallery. Hans Rosling's Gapminder (gapminder.org) for animated visualization inspiration.

S.E. t val. p 2.5% 97.5% (Intercept) X.XX X.XX X.XX 0.XXX X.XX X.XX tab_model(model): HTML regression table opens in Viewer Shows: β, 95% CI, p-value, R², ICC (for mixed models) plot(ggpredict(model, terms="age")): Line plot: predicted outcome vs age with 95% CI band Title: "Predicted values of outcome" msummary(list(m1,m2,m3)): Side-by-side table comparing 3 models — coefficients with stars

Topic 4: Statistical Writing

Reporting statistical results clearly and completely in biomedical journals. Based on APA, SAMPL, and MJMS guidelines. References: Arifin et al. (2016) MJMS; Wilkinson (1999) APA Task Force; Lang & Altman SAMPL Guidelines.

APA FormatSAMPL GuidelinesMJMS StyleConfidence IntervalsEffect Sizes
✍️

Core Principles of Statistical Writing

▼
ℹ️
ICMJE Guideline (International Committee of Medical Journal Editors): "Describe statistical methods with enough detail to enable a knowledgeable reader with access to the original data to verify the reported results. When possible, quantify findings and present them with appropriate indicators of measurement error or uncertainty (such as confidence intervals). Avoid relying solely on statistical hypothesis testing, such as P values, which fail to convey important information about effect size."

Main Points (Statistical Writing)

  • Tell a cohesive, concise story
  • Clearly state your hypotheses and how they relate to your analyses
  • Provide enough detail such that your audience can understand what you did and why you did it
  • For commonly used techniques (e.g., OLS regression), a single sentence description may suffice. For complicated or unfamiliar techniques, more explanation is required
  • Describe the model building process clearly (e.g., reference category, coding scheme)
  • Mention assumptions were assessed — but usually not every individual diagnostic test result unless one was grossly violated

Structure: What to Write in a Paper

Methods Section

  • Design: Make clear what type of study. Do not cloak a study in one guise to give it the reputation of another
  • Population: Define the population clearly. If control/comparison groups, present how they are defined
  • Measurement: Explicitly define variables, how they relate to study goals, units of measurement
  • Power and sample size: Document effect sizes, assumptions, and analytic procedures in power calculations
  • Statistical software: Name, version, specific packages (e.g., "IBM SPSS for Windows version 22.0"; "psych version 1.5.8 and lavaan version 0.5-20 in R")

Results Section

  • Two parts: descriptive statistics + analyses
  • Descriptive statistics introduce variables to the audience
  • For each analysis: state the hypothesis, identify variables, confirm assumptions were met, report test name (one- or two-tailed), alpha level, test statistic with df, p-value, effect size, CI
  • Report complications: missing data, attrition, protocol violations, outlier handling
  • Discussion: Think of credibility, generalizability, and robustness
🔢

Formatting Numbers (MJMS Guidelines — Arifin et al., 2016)

▼
ItemRuleExample
PercentagesOne decimal place generally; ≥2 decimal places if value < 1.0%34.5%; 0.82%
Descriptive statisticsOne additional decimal place beyond original data precisionCholesterol in 1 dp → mean in 2 dp: mean = 4.82, SD = 2.11 mmol/L
Test statisticsTwo decimal places (t, F, χ²)F = 7.83; t = 2.45; χ² = 8.33
P-values (MJMS)Three decimal places; write value not "NS"; smallest reported = <0.001P = 0.003; P = 0.124; P < 0.001
Risk ratios / OR / HRTwo decimal places (Cochrane rule); three significant digits for small ratiosOR = 2.24; HR = 0.86; RR = 1.05
Dashes between numbersAvoid "–" between numbers (mistaken for negative sign). Use "to""20 to 29 years" NOT "20–29 years"
Table numbers: alignmentLast digits must be right-aligned in tablesSee example tables
Italic formattingItalicise: t, F, χ², r, R², α (Cronbach's), pt(78) = 7.83; F(3,36) = 13.41; p = 0.003
⚠️
Do NOT use "±" between mean and SD (it is a mathematical operator meaning plus-or-minus, not "SD"). Write: mean (SD) or mean (SD = value). Example: "the mean age was 46.5 (SD = 3.0)" or in table header: "Mean (SD)".
📊

Reporting Descriptive Statistics

▼
Variable TypeReportFormat in TextFormat in Table Header
Continuous, normally distributedMean and standard deviation (SD)"The mean age was 46.5 (SD = 3.0)"Mean (SD)
Continuous, non-normalMedian and IQR (or range)"The median income was RM 3,500 (IQR: 2,800–4,200)"Median (IQR)
CategoricalCount (n) and percentage (%)"Most respondents were female (n = 45, 60.0%)"n (%)
Confidence interval for single mean95% CI"The mean BMI was 22.5 (95% CI: 21.5, 23.5)"Mean (95% CI)
Confidence interval for proportion95% CI"The prevalence was 34.5% (95% CI: 30.5%, 38.5%)"% (95% CI)
ℹ️
On Confidence Intervals: Many journals push for CIs in all results. The CI gives a range of values such that if the experiment were run many times (e.g., 10,000 times), the range would contain the true parameter 95% of the time. Do NOT use SE to indicate variability of a data set — use SD or IQR instead. SE is essentially a 68% confidence coefficient; use the 95% CI instead.
⚠️
N vs. n: Use lowercase n for sample size (NOT capital N — that refers to population size). Write "n = size" in table headers. Total sample size in table description.

When to Include P-values in Descriptive Tables

For demographic/baseline tables (Table 1), it is generally NOT necessary to report statistical tests and P-values — the main concern is comparability of participants (reflects sampling), not population inference. Report descriptive statistics by group and for overall sample to give visual impression of comparability.

🔬

Reporting Common Statistical Tests (MJMS Format)

▼
TestRequired Reporting ElementsExample
Independent t-testMean (SD) per group; mean difference (95% CI); t-statistic (df); P-valueMean difference = 10.0 (95% CI: 7.5, 12.6); t(78) = 7.83; P < 0.001
Paired t-testMean (SD) pre and post; mean difference (95% CI); t(df); P-valueMean difference = –11.5 (95% CI: –13.1, –9.9); t(29) = –14.92; P < 0.001
One-way ANOVAn and mean (SD) per group; F(df1,df2); P-value; post-hoc resultsF(3, 36) = 13.41; P < 0.001. Post-hoc Bonferroni shows Okinawa vs others P < 0.001
Pearson correlationr (Pearson) or ρ (Spearman); 95% CI; P-value; nr = 0.55 (95% CI: 0.40, 0.68); P < 0.001
Linear regressionβ (adjusted); 95% CI; P-value; R²; model F-statisticβ = 9.4 (95% CI: 8.6, 10.2); P < 0.001; R² = 0.65
Logistic regressionOR (adjusted); 95% CI; P-value; reference category; likelihood ratio testOR = 2.24 (95% CI: 1.04, 4.82); P = 0.045; ref = Female
Chi-square testn (%) per cell; χ²(df); P-value; full contingency table for primary outcomesχ²(1) = 8.33; P = 0.004
MANOVAWilks' Λ or Pillai; F(df1,df2); P; partial η²; follow-up F per DVWilks' Λ = 0.465; F(9, 95.07) = 3.90; P < 0.001; η² = 0.225
Cox regressionHR (unadjusted + adjusted); 95% CI; P; note reference categoryAdjusted HR = 0.86 (95% CI: 0.74, 0.99); P = 0.041
Survival (KM)Median survival with CI; log-rank test; n at risk at key timepointsMedian survival = 24.5 months (95% CI: 20.1, 28.9); log-rank χ²(1) = 5.43; P = 0.020
✅
Reporting Interaction (from lecture notes): Present unadjusted and adjusted ratios side by side — allows reader to determine effect of confounding. Measures of interaction on additive scale: RERI (Relative Excess Risk due to Interaction), AP (Attributable Proportion), S (Synergy Index). RERI = RR++ – RR+– – RR–+ + 1. AP = RERI/RR++.
📋

Tables and Figures — Rules

▼

Presentation Forms

FormBest ForKey Rules
TextBrief results; highlighting key findings from tablesSuitable for "A total of 100 patients were recruited" or "Only gender and salary were significantly associated with..."
TableDetailed statistical results; demographic profiles; regression results; cross-tabulationsTable description/title at TOP; abbreviations + footnotes at BOTTOM (use superscript Roman letters a, b, c...); all abbreviations defined in footnotes even if defined elsewhere; last digits right-aligned; do NOT duplicate data from text
FigureTrends; geographic distribution; comparisons of means over time; pathological images; KM curvesFigure description placed BELOW the figure; reserve for results more presentable in visual form
💡
Howard Wainer on tables: "Tables are for communication, not data storage." Tables multitask about as well as humans — the simpler the task, the more you can multitask. Tables should have a purpose; they should contribute to and be integrated with the rest of the text. Organize the table visually as well as functionally. Data in tables should not be duplicated elsewhere in the text.
