SAMPL Guidelines — Key Reporting Rules by Analysis

▼

Lang & Altman. SAMPL (Statistical Analyses and Methods in the Published Literature) Guidelines. Published in: Science Editors' Handbook, European Association of Science Editors, 2013.

General Principles for Reporting Statistical Methods

  • Identify any statistical procedures used to modify raw data before analysis (transformation, recoding, collapsing)
  • Describe the purpose of the analysis; identify variables; summarise with descriptive statistics
  • Identify the smallest difference considered clinically important
  • Make clear which method was used for each analysis (not just a general list)
  • Verify data conformed to assumptions: skewed data → non-parametric; paired data → paired tests; linear regression → confirmed linearity
  • Indicate whether/how adjustments were made for multiple comparisons
  • Report how outlying data were treated
  • Say whether tests were one- or two-tailed; justify one-tailed tests
  • Report the alpha level (e.g., 0.05) that defines statistical significance
  • Name the statistical package or program

General Principles for Reporting Statistical Results

  • Report numbers with appropriate precision — round as much as reasonable
  • Report total sample AND group sizes for each analysis
  • Report numerators and denominators for all percentages
  • Summarise normal data: mean (SD) — NOT mean ± SD
  • Summarise non-normal data: medians and interpercentile ranges, ranges, or both — report upper and lower boundaries, not just the size
  • Do NOT use SE to indicate variability of a dataset — use SD, IQR, or ranges
  • P-values: report as equalities (P = 0.03) not inequalities (P < 0.05); smallest = P < 0.001; do NOT write "NS" — give the actual P value
  • Report 95% CI for primary outcomes at minimum

Regression-Specific SAMPL Requirements

  • Report the regression equation
  • For multiple regression: report alpha for univariable analysis; whether collinearity and interaction were assessed; variable selection process (forward-stepwise, best subset, etc.)
  • Report β (regression coefficients) with 95% CI and P-values, preferably in a table
  • Report R² for simple regression or R² for multiple regression
  • Specify whether and how the model was validated
  • Do not extend regression line beyond minimum and maximum values of data
⚠️

Common Mistakes to Avoid

▼
MistakeCorrect Approach
Writing "P < 0.05" or "NS" without the actual valueAlways report exact P-value: P = 0.032; P = 0.247; P < 0.001
Using mean ± SD (± is a mathematical operator)Write: mean (SD) or mean (SD = value)
Reporting SE as measure of variabilityUse SD for variability; SE is for precision of the mean estimate (use CI instead)
Using capital N for sample sizeUse lowercase n for sample size; N = population size
Dash between numbers (e.g., 20-29)"20 to 29 years old" — avoid dashes that could be mistaken for minus signs
Reporting test result only in text without tableUse table for detailed results; text to highlight key findings
Abbreviations not defined in table footnotesAll abbreviations must be defined in footnotes even if defined elsewhere
Interpreting non-significant interaction from main effectsIf interaction is significant, report simple slopes/marginal means — never main effects alone
Running follow-up ANOVAs when MANOVA not significantStop at MANOVA if omnibus test not significant
Not reporting effect sizeAlways report: η², partial η², r, R², d, OR, HR — alongside P-value
Not specifying reference category in regressionClearly state the reference category for all categorical predictors
Looking at data before screening/cleaningScreen data first — graphics can detect coding errors that statistics miss

Topic 5: Correlated Numerical Data

Linear Mixed Models (LMM) & Generalised Estimating Equations (GEE) for nested, clustered, longitudinal and repeated-measures data — course lecturer, USM

LMM GEE Random Effects ICC lme4 / lmerTest geepack Longitudinal Multilevel
📋

Introduction to Correlated Data

▼

Many designs in educational, clinical, and epidemiological research generate multilevel data arising from "nested" data structures, where observations are nested within larger units. Examples:

  • Students (Level 1) nested within Schools (Level 2)
  • Patients (Level 1) nested within Physicians (Level 2) nested within Hospitals (Level 3)
  • Repeated measurements (Level 1) nested within Individuals (Level 2) — longitudinal data
  • Nursing home staff (Level 1) nested within Nursing Homes (Level 2)
  • SBP measurements every 30 min × 4 times (Level 1) per Patient (Level 2)

The primary issue with nested data structures is that they violate the independence assumption required by classic techniques such as ANOVA and OLS multiple regression. Level-1 units within the same level-2 unit tend to be more similar to each other than to units in other clusters (within-cluster dependence). This violation leads to excessive Type I errors and biased standard errors (usually underestimated) when using traditional methods.

⚖️

LMM vs GEE: When to Use Which

▼
FeatureLMM / GLMMGEE
Model typeSubject-specific (conditional)Marginal (population-averaged)
InferenceCluster-specific effectAverage effect across population
Random effectsExplicitly modelled (u0j, u1j)Not modelled — treated as nuisance
Missing dataMAR (requires complete cluster model)MCAR (robust sandwich SE)
Use whenBetween-cluster variability is scientifically important; hierarchical questionPopulation-average treatment effect; robust SE needed
R packagelme4, lmerTestgeepack

Lecturer (Lecturer): "Traditional methods — paired t-test, MANOVA — are not suitable if there are many covariates. The more recent and robust methods are GEE and Mixed Models."

📊

Data from Lecturer's Practicals

▼

Scottish School Leavers Survey (practical_linear_mixed_model): n = 33,988 students, 508 schools. Outcome: total attainment score (0–75). Predictors: cohort year, sex, social class, school type, urban/rural, denomination.

Norwegian Nursing Home (Corr_Num): n = 288 staff, 5 nursing homes. Outcome: Perception of Management (POM) score. Predictors: employment status (permanent/intermediate), working condition score, Norwegian mother tongue.

Cancer HDP (multilevel_mod): n = 8,525 patients, 407 physicians, 35 hospitals. Outcome: mobility score. 3-level structure: patients → physicians → hospitals.

🔢

Null / Intercept-Only Model

▼

Start with the simplest model — no explanatory variables — to decompose variance:

yₖₐ = β₀ + u₀ₐ + eₖₐ
  • β₀ = overall mean (across all clusters)
  • u₀ⱼ = cluster-specific deviation (Level-2 residual), u₀ⱼ ~ N(0, σ²ᵤ)
  • eᵢⱼ = individual-level residual (Level-1), eᵢⱼ ~ N(0, σ²ₑ)

Intra-class Correlation (ICC) — proportion of total variance attributable to between-cluster differences:

ICC = σ²ᵘ / (σ²ᵘ + σ²ᵉ)

Example (Scottish Schools, null model): σ²ᵤ = 61.02, σ²ₑ = 258.36 → ICC = 61.02/(61.02+258.36) = 0.191. This means 19.1% of variance in attainment is between schools.

Compare null multilevel model vs single-level OLS using log-likelihood ratio test. If multilevel differs significantly, clustering must be accounted for.

📈

Random Intercept Model (RI)

▼

Intercepts vary across clusters; slopes are fixed (same for all clusters):

yₖₐ = β₀ + β₁xₖₐ + u₀ₐ + eₖₐ

Or equivalently:

yₖₐ = β₀ₐ + β₁xₖₐ + eₖₐ    where β₀ₐ = β₀ + u₀ₐ

The intercept for cluster j is β₀ + û₀ⱼ. The slope β₁ is the same for all clusters.

Scottish Schools example: score = 30.559 + 1.215 × cohort90. For each cohort year increase, average score increases by 1.215 points, while each school has its own baseline intercept.

📉

Random Slope Model (RS)

▼

Both intercepts AND slopes vary across clusters:

yₖₐ = β₀ + β₁xₖₐ + u₀ₐ + u₁ₐxₖₐ + eₖₐ

Or:

yₖₐ = β₀ₐ + β₁ₐxₖₐ + eₖₐ    β₀ₐ = β₀ + u₀ₐ    β₁ₐ = β₁ + u₁ₐ
  • u₀ⱼ = random intercept deviation for cluster j
  • u₁ⱼ = random slope deviation for cluster j
  • Both assumed to follow bivariate normal with zero means and covariance matrix

Lecturer: Random effects u₀ⱼ and u₁ⱼ are assumed to follow normal distributions with zero mean. The variance of the random slope can be large (slopes very different across clusters) or small (slopes nearly identical — single-level regression would suffice).

If model fails to converge: switch to bobyqa optimizer — lmerControl(optimizer = "bobyqa")

Scottish Schools RS model results: Intercept variance = 42.86, Slope variance (cohort90) = 0.16, Corr(u₀,u₁) = −0.39 (schools with higher baseline tend to show slower growth)

🔀

Cross-Level Interaction

▼

Interaction between a Level-1 variable and a Level-2 variable. Allows the effect of an individual-level predictor to differ depending on a cluster-level characteristic.

scoreₖₐ = β₀ + β₁cohort90 + β₂female + β₃sclass + β₄schtype + β₅schurban + β₆cohort90×schtype + u₀ₐ + u₁ₐcohort90 + eₖₐ

Lecturer's result: The interaction cohort90×schtype is ~6× its SE — strong evidence that the effect of cohort year differs for independent vs state schools (β = −0.599, p < 0.001). Independent schools show smaller cohort gains.

📐

Model Comparison & Selection

▼
  • Use anova(model1, model2) with ML estimation (REML=FALSE) to compare nested models via likelihood ratio test (LRT)
  • Compare AIC/BIC — lower is better
  • Log-likelihood difference × 2 = chi-square statistic with df = difference in parameters
  • Start from null model → add fixed effects → add random slopes → test interactions

Scottish Schools: Null logLik = −143,269.5 (df=3) vs single-level logLik = −145,144.4 (df=2). Difference = 2 × 1,874.9 — overwhelming evidence for clustering.

RS vs RI: anova(rs_gend, rs_gend_sl) → χ²(3) = 5.24, p = 0.155 — no evidence that gender slope varies across schools; revert to fixed gender effect.

✅

Model Assumptions Checking

▼
  • Normality of Level-1 residuals: qqmath(model) from lattice — should lie on diagonal
  • Normality of random effects: REsim() from merTools → plotREsim() — caterpillar plot of cluster-specific random effects with CIs
  • Homoscedasticity: plot(model) — fitted vs residuals should show no pattern
  • Random effects distribution: dotplot(ranef(model)) — visual spread of school-level random effects
  • Influential clusters: plotREsim() highlights clusters whose CI does not cross zero — they differ significantly from average
🌐

What is GEE?

▼

Generalised Estimating Equations (GEE) is a marginal (population-averaged) approach to analysing correlated data. Unlike LMM which estimates cluster-specific effects through random effects, GEE estimates the average effect across the entire population, accounting for the correlation between observations within the same cluster without modelling that correlation explicitly.

Lecturer (Lecturer) explicitly lists GEE as one of the two main modern methods for repeated-measures/longitudinal numerical outcomes: "The more recent and robust methods are: (1) Generalised Estimating Equations (GEE), (2) Mixed Models."

GEE is classified under Marginal Models — one approach among subject-specific effects models, dynamic models, and growth-curve models for longitudinal/panel data (Rabe-Hesketh & Skrondal, 2008).

📐

GEE Model Specification

▼

GEE extends Generalised Linear Models (GLM) to correlated data. The mean model is the same as GLM:

g(μₖₐ) = β₀ + β₁xₖₐ₁ + β₂xₖₐ₂ + ... + βₖxₖₐₖ
  • For continuous outcome: identity link → equivalent to population-averaged linear regression
  • For binary: logit link → population-averaged logistic regression
  • For count: log link → population-averaged Poisson

GEE uses a working correlation matrix to account for within-cluster correlation. The key insight: even if this correlation structure is misspecified, the regression coefficient estimates remain consistent (assuming correct mean model), and standard errors are corrected using the robust (sandwich) estimator.

Var(β̂) = A⁻¹ B A⁻¹    (sandwich estimator)

where A = model-based variance, B = empirical variance from data. This is why GEE is sometimes called Quasi-likelihood with robust SE.

🔗

Working Correlation Structures

▼
Structurecorstr=DescriptionWhen to use
Independence"independence"All correlations = 0; ignores clusteringBaseline only; underestimates SE
Exchangeable"exchangeable"Equal correlation between any 2 obs in same cluster (ρ estimated from data)Cross-sectional clusters; no time ordering
AR(1)"ar1"Correlation decays with time: Corr(t, t+k) = ρᵏLongitudinal data — closer time points more correlated
Unstructured"unstructured"All unique pairwise correlations estimatedFew time points, large n; most flexible but uses many parameters
M-dependent"userdefined"Non-zero correlation only up to m lagsWhen correlation drops to zero after m time points

Choosing structure: QIC (Quasi-likelihood Information Criterion) — lower QIC favours better correlation structure. Use QIC() from geepack. For robustness, exchangeable or AR(1) are most common in epidemiology.

🔄

GEE vs LMM: Interpretation Difference

▼

The key conceptual difference — using a longitudinal example (SBP measured 4× per patient):

AspectLMM (subject-specific)GEE (marginal)
β interpretation"For a given patient, a 1-unit increase in x is associated with β change in y""In the population, a 1-unit increase in x is associated with β change in average y"
Random effectsExplicitly estimated (BLUPs for each cluster)Not estimated — nuisance parameters
Missing dataValid under MARValid under MCAR; robust SE partially protects
Policy question"What is the effect for this specific patient/school?""What is the effect if we intervene on the whole population?"

Rule of thumb: If the research question is about the average population-level treatment effect → use GEE. If the question is about between-cluster variability or subject-specific prediction → use LMM.

📋

Step-by-Step GEE Workflow (REAAAPP)

▼
  1. Research Question: Is there a population-averaged association between X and Y, accounting for correlation within clusters?
  2. Environment: Load geepack, tidyverse, gtsummary
  3. Arrange data: Long format (one row per observation), sorted by cluster ID then time
  4. Assumption checks:
    • Identify cluster variable (id variable)
    • Check correlation within clusters (ICCbin or manual)
    • Confirm outcome distribution (continuous → identity, binary → logit, count → log)
  5. Analyse:
    • Fit independence model first (baseline)
    • Fit exchangeable / AR(1) model
    • Compare using QIC(); select best working correlation
    • Obtain robust SE from sandwich estimator
  6. Present: Report β with 95% CI (robust SE), p-values, working correlation structure, QIC
  7. Post-check: Sensitivity analysis — does changing correlation structure change conclusions?
📦

Setup & Packages

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • R version: R ≥ 4.1.0 recommended
  • Install packages once with install.packages(); load each session with library()
  • Data format: Long format (one row per observation/time point); cluster/group ID variable must exist
  • Outcome variable: Continuous (for LMM); binary for GLMM

Data Preparation (Nursing Home Example)

▼
R
# Data: 288 nursing home staff, 5 homes (Norway)
# Outcome: scorepom (Perception of Management, 0-100)
# Level-1: nhid (nursing home ID), Level-2: individual staff

data <- read_sav("data.sav")

data <- data %>%
  mutate_if(is.labelled, as.factor) %>%
  dplyr::select(nhid, status, mothert, scorewc, scorepom) %>%
  mutate(
    status  = recode(status, `1` = "Permanent", `2` = "Intermediate"),
    mothert = recode(mothert, `1` = "Present",  `2` = "Absent")
  )

glimpse(data)
# Rows: 288, Cols: 5
# nhid: chr "1","1","1","4",...
# status: fct Permanent/Intermediate
# mothert: fct Present/Absent
# scorewc: dbl (working condition score)
# scorepom: dbl (outcome)

# Descriptive table
data %>%
  tbl_summary(
    label = list(
      nhid     ~ "Nursing Home",
      status   ~ "Employment Status",
      mothert  ~ "Norwegian Mother Tongue",
      scorewc  ~ "Working Condition Score",
      scorepom ~ "Perception of Management Score"
    )
  ) %>%
  modify_caption("**Table 1. Characteristics of Respondents**") %>%
  bold_labels()
🔢

Step 1: Null Model & ICC

▼
R
# ── Single-level baseline ──────────────────────────────────────────
m.lm <- lm(scorepom ~ 1, data = data)

# ── Null multilevel model (random intercept, no predictors) ────────
m0 <- lmer(scorepom ~ 1 + (1 | nhid), data = data, REML = FALSE)
summary(m0)

# Extract variances for ICC
var_u  <- as.numeric(VarCorr(m0)$nhid)    # Level-2 variance (between-home)
var_e  <- sigma(m0)^2                      # Level-1 variance (within-home)
ICC    <- var_u / (var_u + var_e)
cat("ICC =", round(ICC, 3))

# Tidy output
tidy(m0) %>% kbl() %>% kable_styling()

# LRT: Is multilevel model better than single-level?
logLik(m.lm); logLik(m0)

An ICC > 0.05 (5%) is generally considered sufficient to justify multilevel modelling.

📈

Step 2: Random Intercept Model

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Data: Long format with columns: outcome (continuous), time, predictor(s), cluster ID
  • REML = TRUE for fixed-effect estimates (default); use REML = FALSE for model comparison via LRT
  • Convergence: If optimizer fails, try control=lmerControl(optimizer="bobyqa")
  • Minimum groups: ≥ 5 clusters recommended for stable random effect estimates
R
# ── Random intercept — add Level-1 predictors ──────────────────────
ri <- lmer(scorepom ~ status + mothert + scorewc + (1 | nhid),
           data = data, REML = FALSE)
summary(ri)

# Tidy with 95% CI
tidy(ri, conf.int = TRUE) %>% kbl() %>% kable_styling()

# ── Cancer data (3-level: patient → doctor → hospital) ─────────────
cancer <- read.csv("https://stats.idre.ucla.edu/stat/data/hdp.csv")
cancer <- cancer %>%
  mutate(across(where(is.character), as_factor),
         across(where(is.integer), as.numeric),
         mobility = as.numeric(mobility))

cancer_1 <- cancer %>%
  select(mobility, Age, Sex, LengthofStay, Experience, HID, DID)

ri_cancer <- lmer(mobility ~ Age + Sex + LengthofStay + Experience +
                  (1 | DID), data = cancer_1, REML = FALSE)
summary(ri_cancer)

# Fixed effects interpretation:
# (Intercept) = 7.534
# Age: 0.0255 (p<.001) — older patients have higher mobility score
# Sexfemale: -0.012 (NS) — no gender difference
# Experience: -0.155 (p<.001) — more experienced doctors → lower score?

Step 3: Random Slope Model

▼
R
# ── Scottish schools data ──────────────────────────────────────────
# score.sch: n=33,988, 508 schools
score.sch <- read_dta("5.1.dta") %>%
  mutate(
    female2  = factor(female, labels = c("male","female")),
    class2   = factor(sclass, labels = c("managerial+prof","intermediate","working","unclassified")),
    schtype2 = factor(schtype, labels = c("state-funded","independent"))
  )

# Random intercept first
ri <- lmer(score ~ cohort90 + (1 | schoolid), data = score.sch, REML = FALSE)

# Random slope for cohort90
rs <- lmer(score ~ cohort90 + (1 + cohort90 | schoolid),
           data = score.sch,
           control = lmerControl(optimizer = "bobyqa"),  # prevents non-convergence
           REML = FALSE)
summary(rs)
# Random effects: schoolid Intercept variance=42.86, cohort90 variance=0.16, Corr=-0.39
# Fixed: Intercept=30.61, cohort90=1.234

# Add gender (fixed coefficient)
rs_gend <- lmer(score ~ cohort90 + female2 + (1 + cohort90 | schoolid),
                data = score.sch, REML = FALSE,
                lmerControl(optimizer = "bobyqa"))

# Add social class + school type (Level-2)
rs_full <- lmer(score ~ cohort90 + female2 + factor(sclass) + schtype + schurban +
                cohort90:schtype + (1 + cohort90 | schoolid),
                data = score.sch, REML = FALSE,
                lmerControl(optimizer = "bobyqa"))

# Cross-level interaction: cohort90:schtype
# Independent schools show different cohort trend vs state schools
📊

Step 4: Model Comparison

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • REQUIRED: Models must be refit with REML=FALSE before comparing with anova()
  • Models being compared: Must be nested (simpler model is a restricted version of the complex one)
  • df difference: Should equal the number of additional parameters in the complex model
R
# ── Compare models with LRT (REML=FALSE required) ──────────────────
anova(m0, ri)    # Null vs RI — does adding predictors help?
anova(ri, rs)    # RI vs RS — does allowing slope to vary help?
anova(rs_gend, rs_gend_sl)  # Is random slope for gender needed?

# ── AIC / BIC ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
AIC(m0, ri, rs)
BIC(m0, ri, rs)

# ── Prediction ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
pred_score <- fitted(ri)   # fitted values (includes random effects)
augmented  <- augment(rs)  # .fitted, .resid, .fixed etc.
📤 Expected Output — LRT Model Comparison Df AIC BIC logLik deviance Chisq Chi Df Pr(>Chisq) r1_ml 5 51612 51646 -25801 51602 r2_ml 7 45933 45980 -22960 45919 5682 2 < 2e-16 *** → r2_ml (random slope) significantly better than r1_ml (random intercept only) → Pr(>Chisq) < 0.001 → retain the more complex model
✅

Step 5: Diagnostics & Visualisation

▼
R
# ── Normality of residuals ─────────────────────────────────────────
library(lattice)
qqmath(rs)            # Q-Q plot of Level-1 residuals

# ── Fitted vs residuals (homoscedasticity) ─────────────────────────
plot(rs)

# ── Random effects distribution ────────────────────────────────────

dotplot(randoms)      # Caterpillar plot per school

# ── Random effects simulation (merTools) ──────────────────────────
library(merTools)
re.sim <- REsim(rs)
plotREsim(re.sim)     # Schools whose CI ≠ 0 differ significantly from average

# ── Prediction plot ────────────────────────────────────────────────
rs_res <- augment(rs)
ggplot(rs_res, aes(x = cohort90, y = .fitted, group = schoolid)) +
  geom_line(alpha = 0.3) +
  geom_smooth(aes(group = NULL), method = "lm", se = FALSE, colour = "red") +
  labs(title = "Fitted scores per school against cohort year")

Setup for GEE

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Data format: Long format; must have: subject ID, time/wave variable, outcome, predictors
  • Outcome type: Continuous (identity link) or binary (logit link) or count (log link)
  • Working correlation: Exchangeable (uniform) for repeated measures; AR-1 for ordered time; Independence as baseline
  • GEE vs LMM: GEE estimates population-average (marginal) effects; LMM estimates subject-specific effects
R
# GEE packages
library(geepack)     # main GEE package — geeglm()
library(tidyverse)
library(gtsummary)
library(broom)       # tidy() for geeglm

# Data MUST be sorted by cluster ID (id) then by time/occasion
# geepack requires: id variable, corstr, family
🔢

Basic GEE — Continuous Outcome (Identity Link)

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Required columns: id (subject), time, SBP (outcome), predictor variables
  • Sorted data: Data MUST be sorted by id then time — run arrange(id, time) first
  • id and waves: id must be numeric or factor; consistent number of waves per subject (unbalanced is OK)
R
# Example: repeated SBP measurements in patients
# Data in long format: one row per occasion per patient
# Variables: sbp (outcome), time, treatment, patient_id

# Sort data first
long_data <- long_data %>% arrange(patient_id, time)

# ── Independence (baseline — ignores clustering) ──────────────────
gee_ind <- geeglm(sbp ~ time + treatment,
                  data   = long_data,
                  id     = patient_id,
                  family = gaussian(link = "identity"),
                  corstr = "independence")

# ── Exchangeable (equal correlation between any 2 occasions) ────────
gee_exch <- geeglm(sbp ~ time + treatment,
                   data   = long_data,
                   id     = patient_id,
                   family = gaussian(link = "identity"),
                   corstr = "exchangeable")

# ── AR(1) (correlations decay with lag) ────────────────────────────
gee_ar1 <- geeglm(sbp ~ time + treatment,
                  data   = long_data,
                  id     = patient_id,
                  family = gaussian(link = "identity"),
                  corstr = "ar1")

# ── Unstructured ───────────────────────────────────────────────────
gee_unstr <- geeglm(sbp ~ time + treatment,
                    data   = long_data,
                    id     = patient_id,
                    family = gaussian(link = "identity"),
                    corstr = "unstructured")
📤 Expected Output — GEE (exchangeable correlation)Call: geeglm(formula = SBP ~ time + age + sex, id = id, data = df, corstr = "exchangeable") Coefficients: Estimate Std.err Wald Pr(>|W|) (Intercept) XXX.XX X.XX XXX.X < 2e-16 *** time X.XX X.XX X.X 0.XXX age X.XX X.XX XX.X < 0.001 *** sexMale XX.XX X.XX XX.X 0.XXX Estimated Scale Parameters: (Intercept) = XXX.XX Correlation: alpha = 0.XX (exchangeable) → Interpret β as population-average change in SBP per unit increase in predictor
📊

Selecting Working Correlation with QIC

▼
R
# QIC = Quasi-likelihood Information Criterion (Pan, 2001)
# Lower QIC = better correlation structure
library(geepack)

QIC(gee_ind)    # independence
QIC(gee_exch)   # exchangeable
QIC(gee_ar1)    # AR(1)
QIC(gee_unstr)  # unstructured

# QIC also returns QICu (independence model QIC for comparison)
# Select the model with lowest QIC

# After selecting best correlation structure:
summary(gee_exch)
# Coefficients:
#              Estimate  Std.err     Wald Pr(>|W|)
# (Intercept)  120.50    1.23     9594.07   <2e-16 ***
# time          -2.35    0.45       27.22   <0.001 ***
# treatment     -8.12    1.10       54.41   <0.001 ***
# Estimated correlation: rho = 0.42 (exchangeable)
📋

Tidy Output & Confidence Intervals

▼
R
# Tidy results with 95% CI (robust SE)
tidy(gee_exch, conf.int = TRUE) %>%
  kbl(digits = 3) %>%
  kable_styling()

# Manual 95% CI (robust SE from summary)
coef_table <- summary(gee_exch)$coefficients
beta <- coef_table[, "Estimate"]
se   <- coef_table[, "Std.err"]   # this IS the robust SE
ci_lo <- beta - 1.96 * se
ci_hi <- beta + 1.96 * se

# Present in gtsummary style
tbl_regression(gee_exch,
               exponentiate = FALSE,   # TRUE for binary/Poisson for OR/RR
               label = list(time ~ "Follow-up time", treatment ~ "Treatment group"))
🔄

GEE with Binary & Count Outcomes

▼
R
# ── Binary outcome (logistic GEE) ─────────────────────────────────
# Interpret coefficients as LOG ODDS (exponentiate for OR)
gee_logit <- geeglm(remission ~ Age + Sex + time,
                    data   = cancer_long,
                    id     = patient_id,
                    family = binomial(link = "logit"),
                    corstr = "exchangeable")

# Exponentiated = Population-Averaged Odds Ratio
tidy(gee_logit, exponentiate = TRUE, conf.int = TRUE)

# ── Count outcome (Poisson GEE) ────────────────────────────────────
gee_pois <- geeglm(count ~ time + treatment + offset(log(n)),
                   data   = count_data,
                   id     = cluster_id,
                   family = poisson(link = "log"),
                   corstr = "ar1")

# Exponentiated = Population-Averaged Incidence Rate Ratio (IRR)
tidy(gee_pois, exponentiate = TRUE, conf.int = TRUE)
✅

GEE Sensitivity Analysis

▼
R
# Compare β estimates across correlation structures
# If conclusions are robust, coefficients should be similar

bind_rows(
  tidy(gee_ind,   conf.int=TRUE) %>% mutate(corstr="independence"),
  tidy(gee_exch,  conf.int=TRUE) %>% mutate(corstr="exchangeable"),
  tidy(gee_ar1,   conf.int=TRUE) %>% mutate(corstr="ar1"),
  tidy(gee_unstr, conf.int=TRUE) %>% mutate(corstr="unstructured")
) %>%
  filter(term != "(Intercept)") %>%
  ggplot(aes(x = corstr, y = estimate, ymin = conf.low, ymax = conf.high)) +
  geom_pointrange() +
  geom_hline(yintercept = 0, linetype = "dashed") +
  facet_wrap(~term, scales = "free_y") +
  labs(title = "Sensitivity of GEE estimates to correlation structure")
🔑

LMM — Fixed Effects: How to Interpret

▼

Based on: USM DrPH Lecture Notes (2022); Snijders & Bosker (2012); Singer & Willett (2003)

Key principle: LMM fixed effects are cluster-specific (conditional) — the effect of X on Y for a given cluster (school/patient/site) with its average random effect.
CoefficientTemplate Sentence
Continuous predictor (L1) "For a given [cluster], a 1-unit increase in [X] was associated with a [β] [unit] [increase/decrease] in [outcome], holding other predictors constant (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Categorical predictor "Compared to [reference group], [group] had a [β] [unit] [higher/lower] [outcome] within the same cluster (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Time (longitudinal) "[Outcome] [increased/decreased] by [β] [units] per [time unit] on average (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Intercept "The estimated mean [outcome] was [β₀] [units] when all predictors were at their reference value, for a cluster with an average random effect."
Worked example (Scottish Schools dataset):
"A two-level random intercept model was fitted with attainment score as the outcome and cohort year as the Level-1 predictor, nested within schools. The null model ICC was 0.191, indicating 19.1% of variance was attributable to between-school differences, justifying multilevel modelling (Snijders & Bosker, 2012). After adjusting for cohort year and sex, attainment increased by 1.23 points per cohort year (β=1.23, 95% CI: 1.18–1.24, p<0.001). Female students scored 1.94 points higher than males (β=1.94, 95% CI: 1.62–2.27, p<0.001). The random intercept variance was τ₀²=45.99, indicating substantial between-school variation in baseline attainment."

Ref: Lecturer. Linear Mixed Effect Models. USM DrPH, 2022; Snijders TAB

📌 PYQ Worked Example — stress.csv (2023/24 Q3, 2020/21 Q2)
Formula: score ~ time + sex + (1|districtid), REML=TRUE | n=511 districts
Fixed EffectβSE95% CIpInterpretation
(Intercept)50.180.5649.08–51.28<0.001Estimated baseline stress for a male at time=0 in an average district
time−2.140.28−2.69 to −1.59<0.001"For a given district, each one-unit increase in time was associated with a decrease of 2.14 points in stress score, holding sex constant."
sexFemale3.620.382.88–4.36<0.001"Compared to males (reference), females had a 3.62-point higher stress score on average, within the same district and time point."

Random effects: Intercept variance τ₀² = 35.12 (SD=5.93); Residual σ² = 84.71. ICC = 35.12/(35.12+84.71) = 0.293 → "29.3% of variance in stress score was attributable to between-district differences, justifying multilevel modelling."

, Bosker RJ (2012). Multilevel Analysis, 2nd ed. Sage; Singer JD, Willett JB (2003). Applied Longitudinal Data Analysis. Oxford UP; Hox JJ, Moerbeek M, van de Schoot R (2017). Multilevel Analysis, 3rd ed. Routledge.

📐

LMM — Random Effects, ICC & Variance Components

▼
ICC formula: ICC = τ₀² / (τ₀² + σ²) where τ₀² = between-cluster variance, σ² = within-cluster residual variance (Snijders & Bosker, 2012, p.17)
ComponentTemplate / Threshold
ICC ≥ 0.05 "The null model ICC was [value], indicating [ICC×100]% of variance was attributable to between-[cluster] differences, justifying multilevel modelling (Hox et al., 2017)."
ICC < 0.05 "Although the ICC was below 0.05, multilevel modelling was retained to account for the design effect and to provide unbiased standard errors."
Random intercept variance (τ₀²) "The random intercept variance (τ₀² = [value]) indicated [substantial/moderate/small] variation in baseline [outcome] across [clusters]."
Residual (σ²) "The within-cluster residual variance was σ² = [value], representing unexplained individual-level variation."
LRT model comparison "A likelihood ratio test showed that Model B (random slope) fitted significantly better than Model A (random intercept only) (χ²([df])=[value], p<0.05). Note: use ML (not REML) for LRT comparisons."
⚠ SPSS path: Analyze → Mixed Models → Linear.
📌 PYQ Worked Example — LMM Random Effects (stress.csv)
  • Null model ICC: "The null model indicated an ICC of 0.293, meaning 29.3% of the total variance in stress score was attributable to between-district differences. This substantial clustering effect justified the use of multilevel modelling (Hox et al., 2017)."
  • Random intercept variance (r1 model): "The random intercept variance was τ₀² = 35.12, reflecting considerable variation in baseline stress scores across the 511 districts. District-level SD = 5.93 points."
  • Residual variance: "The within-district residual variance was σ² = 84.71, indicating unaccounted individual-level variation in stress score."
  • Random slope model (r2) — 2023/24 Q3(b): "The random slope model (score ~ time + sex + (1+time|districtid)) showed intercept variance τ₀² = 35.12, slope variance τ₁² = 4.87 (SD=2.21), and intercept-slope correlation r = −0.31. This negative correlation indicates that districts with higher baseline stress tended to show steeper decreases over time."
  • LRT model comparison — 2023/24 Q3(d): "A likelihood ratio test comparing r1 vs r2 (REML=FALSE) yielded χ²(2) = 8.43, p = 0.015, indicating r2 (random slope) provided significantly better fit than r1 (random intercept only). The random slope model was therefore retained."
ICC must be computed manually from SPSS output: ICC = Variance(Intercept) / [Variance(Intercept) + Residual]. Use REML for final parameter estimates; switch to ML for model comparison LRT.
🔄

GEE — Population-Averaged Interpretation

▼

Based on: Liang & Zeger (1986); Diggle et al. (2002); Fitzmaurice, Laird & Ware (2011)

Key principle: GEE estimates are population-averaged (marginal) — the effect of X on the mean of Y across all individuals in the population, not for a specific individual or cluster (Fitzmaurice et al., 2011, p.353).
ElementTemplate Sentence
Continuous predictor "In the population, a 1-unit increase in [X] was associated with a mean [increase/decrease] of [|β|] [units] in [outcome] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], Wald χ²(1) = [value], p [</>] 0.05)."
Categorical predictor "In the population, [comparison group] had a population-averaged [outcome] [β] [units] [higher/lower] than [reference group] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Working correlation "An [exchangeable/AR(1)/unstructured] working correlation structure was selected based on the lowest QIC (Pan, 2001). The estimated within-subject correlation was ρ = [value]."
Standard errors "Robust (sandwich) standard errors were applied to ensure valid inference irrespective of working correlation misspecification (Liang & Zeger, 1986)."
✓ Full GEE reporting template:
"Generalised estimating equations (GEE) with an exchangeable working correlation structure (QIC = [value]) were used to estimate population-averaged effects on [outcome], accounting for repeated measurements within [subjects]. Robust standard errors were applied. After adjusting for [covariates], [predictor] was associated with a population-averaged [increase/decrease] of [|β|] [units] in [outcome] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], Wald χ²(1) = [value], p = [value])."
LMM vs GEE — choose the right interpretation:
• LMM: "For a given patient, …" → cluster-specific (use for individual clinical decisions)
• GEE: "In the population, …" → population-averaged (use for public health policy)
(Fitzmaurice et al., 2011, p.353; Diggle et al., 2002)

Ref: Liang KY, Zeger SL (1986).

📌 PYQ Worked Example — GEE Population-Averaged (visualize.csv / stress.csv)
Formula: score ~ time + sex, id=districtid, corstr="exchangeable" | Outcome: stress score (continuous)
ParameterβRobust SE95% CIp (Wald)Interpretation
(Intercept)50.180.6148.98–51.38<0.001Population baseline stress for males at time=0
time−2.140.31−2.75 to −1.53<0.001"In the population of districts, each one-unit increase in time was associated with an average decrease of 2.14 points in stress score (95% CI: −2.75 to −1.53)."
sexFemale3.620.422.79–4.45<0.001"On average across the population, females had 3.62 points higher stress score compared to males (95% CI: 2.79–4.45)."

Working correlation: Exchangeable (QIC used for selection). QIC comparison: Exchangeable (QIC=4512) < Independence (QIC=4897) < AR-1 (QIC=4688) → exchangeable structure selected.
Sentence: "Using GEE with an exchangeable working correlation structure (Liang & Zeger, 1986), the population-averaged estimates indicated... These represent marginal effects applicable to the entire district population rather than specific district effects."

LMM vs GEE decision (stress.csv context): "Since district-specific predictions are of interest for district health officers to target interventions, LMM was preferred over GEE. Had the aim been to estimate the population-average effect for national policy, GEE would be more appropriate (Fitzmaurice et al., 2011)."

Biometrika, 73:13–22; Diggle PJ, Heagerty P, Liang KY, Zeger SL (2002). Analysis of Longitudinal Data, 2nd ed. Oxford UP; Fitzmaurice GM, Laird NM, Ware JH (2011). Applied Longitudinal Analysis, 2nd ed. Wiley; Pan W (2001). Biometrics, 57:120–125; USM DrPH Notes.

📋

Reporting Checklist — LMM & GEE

▼
LMM must report:
  • Fixed effects: β, SE, 95% CI, t, df, p
  • Random effects: τ₀² (intercept variance), σ² (residual)
  • ICC from null model
  • Estimation method (ML vs REML)
  • Model comparison: AIC, BIC, LRT χ² if applicable
  • Number of clusters and observations
GEE must report:
  • Working correlation structure chosen + QIC
  • Estimated correlation (ρ)
  • β, robust SE, 95% CI, Wald χ², p
  • State "population-averaged" interpretation explicitly
  • Confirm robust (sandwich) SEs used
  • Sample size: N subjects, total observations

⚠ Key phrase: Always include "for a given district" or "within the same cluster" for LMM fixed effects — this distinguishes LMM (cluster-specific) from GEE (population-averaged).

Output lineInterpretation
time Variance = 4.87 (SD=2.21)"The rate of change in stress over time varied significantly across districts (slope variance τ₁² = 4.87, SD = 2.21)."
Corr = −0.31"The negative intercept-slope correlation (r = −0.31) suggests districts with higher baseline stress tended to show steeper decreases over time."

Step 4 — Likelihood Ratio Test (LRT): Which model fits better? 
# anova(r1, r2) with REML=FALSE
     Df   AIC      BIC      logLik   deviance  Chisq  Chi Df  Pr(>Chisq)
r1    5   58491.2  58528.1  -29240.6  58481.2
r2    7   58486.7  58538.3  -29236.4  58472.7   8.43   2      0.0148 *
✍ Write: "A likelihood ratio test comparing the random intercept model (r1) to the random slope model (r2) was statistically significant (χ²(2) = 8.43, p = 0.015), indicating that allowing the effect of time to vary across districts significantly improved model fit. The random slope model (r2) was therefore retained for final inference. Note: models were refitted using maximum likelihood (ML) for the LRT comparison (Singer & Willett, 2003)."
✍ Complete Results Paragraph (copy & adapt):

"A two-level linear mixed model was fitted with stress score as the outcome and time and sex as Level-1 predictors, with observations nested within 511 districts (N = 8,176). The null model intraclass correlation coefficient was 0.293, indicating that 29.3% of the total variance in stress score was attributable to between-district differences, justifying the multilevel approach (Hox et al., 2017).

In the final random slope model, for a given district, each one-unit increase in time was associated with a significant decrease of 2.14 points in stress score (β = −2.14, SE = 0.28, 95% CI: −2.69 to −1.59, p < 0.001). Females had a significantly higher stress score than males within the same district and time point (β = 3.62, SE = 0.38, 95% CI: 2.88–4.36, p < 0.001). The random slope variance indicated that the rate of change in stress over time differed across districts (τ₁² = 4.87, SD = 2.21), with districts having higher baseline stress showing steeper declines (r = −0.31). A likelihood ratio test confirmed that the random slope model provided significantly better fit than the random intercept model (χ²(2) = 8.43, p = 0.015)."

GEE — Full Output Worked Example: Reading R Output Line by Line

▼

Dataset: stress.csv | Model: geeglm(score ~ time + sex, id=districtid, corstr="exchangeable", family=gaussian)

Step 1 — Choose Working Correlation Structure (QIC) 
# QIC comparison (lower = better)
Structure        QIC        QICu
Independence     4897.3     4880.1
Exchangeable     4512.6     4489.4  ← lowest QIC → selected
AR-1             4688.1     4671.2
Unstructured     4521.0     4490.0
✍ Write: "An exchangeable working correlation structure was selected based on the lowest QIC value (QIC = 4512.6), indicating that observations within the same district tend to have equal pairwise correlations regardless of their temporal distance (Pan, 2001)."
Step 2 — GEE Coefficients Table: Population-averaged effects 
Coefficients:
             Estimate Std.err  Wald    Pr(>|W|)
(Intercept)  50.18    0.61     6773.8  <2e-16 ***
time         -2.14    0.31     47.7    <2e-16 ***
sexFemale     3.62    0.42     74.3    <2e-16 ***

Estimated Scale Parameter: 102.34
Number of clusters: 511   Maximum cluster size: 16
RowKey difference vs LMMInterpretation sentence
(Intercept) 50.18Population mean at time=0 for males across all districts"The population-averaged stress score for males at time zero was estimated at 50.18 (SE=0.61, p<0.001)."
time −2.14***No "for a given district" — this is the population average effect"In the population, each one-unit increase in time was associated with an average decrease of 2.14 points in stress score (β=−2.14, SE=0.31, 95% CI: −2.75 to −1.53, Wald χ²(1)=47.7, p<0.001)."
sexFemale 3.62***Average difference across all districts, not within one district"On average across the population of districts, females had a 3.62-point higher stress score than males (β=3.62, SE=0.42, 95% CI: 2.80–4.44, Wald χ²(1)=74.3, p<0.001)."
⚠ Critical difference: GEE SE (0.31 for time) is larger than LMM SE (0.28) because GEE uses robust sandwich SEs. This is expected and appropriate — GEE protects against correlation misspecification (Liang & Zeger, 1986).
Step 3 — Scale Parameter and Cluster Information
OutputMeaningReport as
Scale Parameter = 102.34Overall residual variance estimate (not split by level like LMM)Usually not reported in main text; mention if discussing model diagnostics
Clusters: 511511 districts (clusters) — this is the unit of clustering"Data from 511 districts (max cluster size = 16 observations)"
Max cluster size: 16Largest number of repeated observations in one districtReport in methods section for GEE
⚡ LMM vs GEE — When the Numbers Differ
AspectLMM resultGEE resultWhy different?
β time−2.14−2.14Same here — both estimate the same underlying effect (Gaussian outcome, linear model)
SE time0.28 (model-based)0.31 (robust/sandwich)GEE uses robust SE — more conservative, valid even if correlation structure is wrong
InterpretationCluster-specific: "for a given district…"Population-averaged: "in the population…"Fundamental philosophical difference (Fitzmaurice et al., 2011)
When to useDistrict health officer needs to predict for a specific districtMinistry of Health setting national policy for all districtsResearch question drives the choice, not statistical convenience
✍ Complete GEE Results Paragraph (copy & adapt):

"Generalised estimating equations (GEE) with an exchangeable working correlation structure were used to estimate population-averaged effects on stress score, accounting for repeated observations clustered within 511 districts (N = 8,176 observations). The exchangeable structure was selected based on the lowest QIC (4512.6; Pan, 2001). Robust (sandwich) standard errors were applied to ensure valid inference regardless of working correlation misspecification (Liang & Zeger, 1986).

In the population of districts, each one-unit increase in time was associated with a statistically significant decrease of 2.14 points in stress score (β = −2.14, SE = 0.31, 95% CI: −2.75 to −1.53, Wald χ²(1) = 47.7, p < 0.001). On average across the population, females had a significantly higher stress score than males by 3.62 points (β = 3.62, SE = 0.42, 95% CI: 2.80–4.44, Wald χ²(1) = 74.3, p < 0.001). These estimates represent the average effect across the population of districts, rather than the effect within any specific district."

Topic 6: Machine Learning – Regression

Linear Regression · Decision Tree Regression · Neural Network Regression — Lecturers, School of Computer Sciences, USM

Linear Regression Decision Tree Neural Network rpart h2o.deeplearning RMSE / MAE / R² Dropout 📅 Updated June 2026
What is Machine Learning?

Machine learning is transforming healthcare by enabling data-driven insights and personalized treatments. Regression techniques uncover patterns, predict outcomes, and support clinical decision-making.

Applications in Health and Medicine

  • Predictive Modeling for Disease Diagnosis
    • Early Detection — ML models identify subtle patterns to diagnose diseases sooner
    • Differential Diagnosis — Regression techniques can help distinguish between similar conditions based on symptoms
    • Personalized Risk — Predictive models can assess an individual's likelihood of developing a disease
  • Forecasting Patient Outcomes and Prognosis
    • Survival Analysis — Regression can predict the probability and timing of events, such as disease progression or mortality
    • Length of Stay — Models can forecast how long a patient will remain in hospital, aiding resource planning
    • Readmission Risk — Identifying patients likely to be readmitted allows for targeted interventions to prevent complications
  • Personalized Treatment Recommendations
    • Optimal Dosing — Regression can determine the most effective drug dosages for individual patients
    • Device Selection — Predictive models can guide the choice of medical devices and technologies
    • Lifestyle Interventions — Regression can identify the most impactful lifestyle changes for improving patient outcomes
How Do Machines Learn?

Data Types

Machine learning operates on structured (tabular), unstructured (text, images), and semi-structured data.

Learning from Examples

Machines learn by identifying patterns in example data. Given labelled data (actual values), the model learns to predict outcomes for new, unseen data.

Types of Machine Learning

TypeDescriptionExample
Supervised LearningLearning from labelled data with known outcomesPredicting diabetes progression given patient attributes
Unsupervised LearningFinding patterns in unlabelled data without known outcomesClustering patients by symptom profile
Reinforcement LearningLearning through interaction with an environment using rewards/penaltiesDrug dosage optimization
Supervised Learning: Classification vs Regression
ClassificationRegression
Predicts a category / discrete class labelPredicts a continuous / numerical value
Example: Will the patient develop diabetes? (Yes/No)Example: What will the patient's diabetes progression score be?
Output: Class label (e.g., 0 or 1)Output: Real-valued number (e.g., 57.9, 180.3)

What Can We Predict with Regression?

  • Numerical quantities like profit, cost, sales volume
  • Patient disease progression score
  • Hospital consultation fees
  • Housing prices based on size, rooms, floors
  • Ice cream sales based on temperature
Comparison of ML Regression Methods
MethodStrengthsWeaknesses
Linear RegressionSimple, interpretable, fast; go-to first method; handles correlated variablesTrouble with very large number of variables or categorical variables with many levels; assumes linearity
Decision TreeIntuitive tree structure, handles non-linearity, no assumptions about data distributionProne to overfitting; sensitive to small changes in data (high variance)
Neural NetworkLearns complex non-linear patterns, very flexible, performs well with large datasetsBlack box model, requires more data and computation, many hyperparameters to tune
Linear regression is the bread-and-butter prediction method for statisticians and data scientists. Always try linear regression first, and only use more complicated methods if they actually outperform it.
Introduction to Linear Regression

Linear regression is a supervised learning approach that models the dependence of Y on x as being linear.

Example: Predicting housing prices based on different sizes — given labelled data (actual price of each example), the model learns to predict price for unseen data (regression problem — predicting real-valued outputs).

Dependent and Independent Variables

TermAlso Known AsRole
Independent variablePredictor, attribute, feature, input, xThe variable used to make predictions
Dependent variableResponse variable, outcome, target, YTHE OUTCOME — the variable we want to predict

Exercise Examples

  • Students noted who held the door open more often, males or females → IV: Male or female; DV: Frequency of opening the door
  • Sports students vs Psychology students in a high jump competition → IV: Sport vs Psychology; DV: Height of jump

When two variables are related, it means that when one variable changes, the other will change on average by a certain amount. Y changes with x — the question is how much Y changes with any given change of x.

Univariate Linear Regression

Linear regression with 1 independent variable (attribute), x, and 1 dependent variable (target), Y.

Equation

f(x) = w₀ + w₁x
ParameterNameMeaning
w₀Intercept (Y-intercept)The value of Y when X = 0
w₁Slope / CoefficientQuantifies the effect of X on Y; the rate of change of Y with respect to X
f(x)Dependent variableThe variable we are trying to predict
XIndependent variableThe predictor/attribute

In R: m.regression <- lm(size ~ weight, data = <yourdata>)

Slope Interpretation

  • Positive Slope (w₁ > 0): As x increases, y also increases
  • Negative Slope (w₁ < 0): As x increases, y decreases
  • Zero Slope (w₁ = 0): Y does not change with x; no relationship between the variables
Method of Least Squares

The fitted line is most often determined by the method of "least squares".

  1. Deviations from the fitted line are called residuals
  2. Calculate the square of each residual, then total them up — this gives the sum of squares
  3. Repeatedly tweak the regression equation until you get the lowest possible sum of squares
  4. Find the line that minimises the sum of the squared residuals

Residuals are a measure of how far from the regression line data points are. RMSE is a measure of how spread out these residuals are — in other words, it tells you how concentrated the data is around the line of best fit.

Finding Regression Slope and Intercept (Step-by-Step)

Given the equation: y′ = a + bx

Step 1: Prepare Data Table

Calculate ∑x, ∑y, ∑xy, ∑x², and n (number of observations).

Step 2: Calculate b (slope) and a (intercept)

Find b:

b = [n(∑xy) − (∑x × ∑y)] / [n(∑x²) − (∑x)²]

b = [6(20,485) − (247 × 486)] / [6(11,409) − 247²]

b = (122,910 − 120,042) / (68,454 − 61,009)

b = 2,868 / 7,445 = 0.385225


Find a:

a = [(∑y × ∑x²) − (∑x × ∑xy)] / [n(∑x²) − (∑x)²]

a = [(486 × 11,409) − (247 × 20,485)] / [6(11,409) − 247²]

a = [5,544,774 − 5,059,795] / 7,445 = 484,979 / 7,445 = 65.14

Step 3: Insert Values into Equation

y′ = 65.14 + 0.385225x
Multivariate Linear Regression

Linear regression with p independent variables (attributes): x = x₁, x₂, …, xp, and 1 dependent variable (target), Y.

Example: Predicting House Price

x₁ Size (feet²)x₂ Number of Bedroomsx₃ Number of FloorsY Price (RM)
151032520,000
185042650,000
100021310,000
85021250,000

Equation

f(x) = w₀ + w₁x₁ + w₂x₂ + … + wpxp

Parameter estimation uses ordinary least squares (OLS) — minimising the sum of squared residuals across all p predictors.

Linear Regression Takeaways

  • Linear regression is the go-to statistical modeling method for quantities
  • Always try linear regression first, and only use more complicated methods if they actually outperform a linear regression model
  • Linear regression will have trouble with problems that have a very large number of variables, or categorical variables with a very large number of levels
  • Linear regression can predict well even in the presence of correlated variables, but correlated variables lower the quality of the advice
Lab 1: Linear Regression in R (Diabetes Dataset)

Dataset: diabetes.csv (11 attributes)

  • age — Age of the patient
  • sex — Gender of the patient
  • bmi — Body mass index
  • bp — Blood pressure
  • S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6 — Six blood serum measurements
  • diabetes — A quantitative measure of disease progression one year after baseline (TARGET)

Goal: Predict if a patient's diabetes progression given the attributes (continuous value → regression problem).

⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Dataset: diabetes.csv — 442 patients; columns: age, sex, BMI, BP, S1–S6, target (disease progression)
  • Outcome: target is continuous → regression (not classification)
  • Data check: Run sum(is.na(diabetes)) — must be 0 before modelling
  • Random seed: Add set.seed(123) before sample() for reproducible split
  • Package: Metrics provides rmse() and mae() functions
R
install.packages("Metrics")
library(Metrics)

# Load data
diabetes <- read.csv("diabetes.csv")
View(diabetes)
str(diabetes)

# Split 70:30 train/test
sample_ind <- sample(nrow(diabetes), nrow(diabetes)*0.7)
train <- diabetes[sample_ind,]
test  <- diabetes[-sample_ind,]

# Build linear regression model (all attributes)
lr <- lm(diabetes ~ ., train)
summary(lr)
# The residuals show how well the model fits the data.
# The "Estimate" column lists the coefficients (slope) of each attribute.

# Evaluate on test set
test$pred <- predict(lr, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred)
# Result: RMSE = 57.9175
The residuals tell how well the model fits the data — they are the vertical distances between data points and the regression line. RMSE = 57.9175.
🔢

Finding Slope & Intercept — Manual Calculation

▼

To find the regression line ŷ = a + bx manually, use the least squares method. Residuals (observed − predicted) are squared and summed; we find the line that minimises this sum.

Formulas

Slope (b):

b = (n·Σxy − Σx·Σy) / (n·Σx² − (Σx)²)

Intercept (a):

a = (Σx²·Σy − Σx·Σxy) / (n·Σx² − (Σx)²)

In R: m <- lm(y ~ x, data=df)

Worked Example (n = 6)

GivenValue
Σx247
Σy486
Σxy20,485
Σx²11,409
n6

Step-by-Step Solution

  1. Find b (slope):
    b = (6 × 20,485 − 247 × 486) / (6 × 11,409 − 247²)
    b = (122,910 − 120,042) / (68,454 − 61,009)
    b = 2,868 / 7,445 = 0.38
  2. Find a (intercept):
    a = (11,409 × 486 − 247 × 20,485) / 7,445
    a = 484,979 / 7,445 = 65.14
  3. Regression equation: ŷ = 65.14 + 0.38x

Interpretation

  • Slope (0.38): for every 1 unit increase in x, y increases by 0.38 units on average
  • Intercept (65.14): baseline value of y when x = 0
  • Prediction: if x = 10, ŷ = 65.14 + 0.38(10) = 68.94
💡

Linear Regression — Key Takeaways (June 2026)

▼
Linear regression is the go-to statistical modelling method for predicting numerical quantities. Always try it first — only use more complex methods if they demonstrably outperform it.
  • Supervised learning — requires labelled data (actual outcome values known)
  • Regression problem = predicting real-valued (continuous) outputs
  • Will struggle when there are very many variables or categorical variables with many levels
  • Can predict well even with correlated predictors, but multicollinearity lowers coefficient interpretation quality
  • The fitted line is determined by least squares — minimising Σ(residual)²

Source: Lecturers, School of Computer Sciences, USM — Slides June 2026

Introduction to Decision Tree Regression

Decision tree regression is a powerful machine learning technique used to model and predict continuous target variables. This approach constructs a tree-like model of decisions and their possible consequences, allowing for intuitive and interpretable analysis of complex relationships in data.

Structure

  • Hierarchical model that composes of internal decision nodes (m) and leaf nodes
  • Each decision node m implements a test with discrete outcomes labelling the branches
  • Each leaf node has a numeric output instead of a category or class

Example: Predicting House Price

A decision tree splits by features like number of rooms and build-up area:

  • Buildup ≥2500 → Yes: #rooms ≥5 → RM800k / No: RM600k
  • Buildup ≥2500 → No: #rooms ≥5 → RM400k / No: RM300k / RM100k

App Recommendation Example

Recommending apps to new users based on age and gender (classification); same tree concept for regression predicts continuous values.

Building a Decision Tree

How Do We Obtain a Good Split?

  • Metric: Sum of Squared Errors (SSE) for regression (vs entropy/Gini for classification)
  • The goal is to partition the data into subsets such that each subset contains homogeneous values (with minimum standard deviation in the case of regression)
  • The goal is to decrease the standard deviation after the data is split into subsets
  • Hence, the attribute that can provide the highest decrease in standard deviation is chosen

SSE Formula

SSE = ∑(yi − 𝛸)2

The algorithm selects the split that minimizes the sum of the squared deviations from the mean in the two separate partitions.

Example: Predicting Price (in Mil)

Split feature "Year" at Year > 2010:

  • Yes branch: SSE = 0.05167
  • No branch: SSE = 0.01732

The algorithm tries all possible splits for all features and picks the one with minimum total SSE.

Pruning Decision Trees

Since the tree is grown from the Training Set, a fully developed tree typically suffers from overfitting. Overfitting occurs when a decision tree model is too complex and fits the training data too closely, compromising its ability to generalize to new, unseen data.

The tree is pruned to minimize the sum of: (1) the output variable variance in the validation data, and (2) the number of terminal nodes.

Pruning is performed on a last-in first-out basis — the last grown node is the first to be subject to elimination.

Two Types of Pruning

TypeDescription
Pre-pruningEarly stopping criteria — selecting a depth before perfect classification. Stop the tree-building process early before it produces leaves with very small samples.
Post-pruningGrow the tree to perfect classification then prune the tree. Allow the tree to grow until all leaves are pure and/or have no training error (it may be overfitted), then prune (cut) the tree to obtain the optimal tree.
Pre-Pruning (Early Stopping)

Stop the tree-building process early before it produces leaves with very small samples. Three parameters:

ParameterDescriptionExample
maxdepthMaximum depth of a tree — length of the longest path from root node to a leaf node. Stops tree building when depth equals the value set.maxdepth=6
minsplitMinimum number of samples that must exist in a node in order for a split to be attempted.minsplit=70 — a node can be further split only if it has more than 70 samples
minbucketMinimum number of samples in any terminal node.minbucket=5 — every leaf node should have at least 5 samples
If only one of minbucket or minsplit is specified, the code either sets minsplit to minbucket×3 or minbucket to minsplit/3, as appropriate.
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Dataset: Same diabetes.csv with 70/30 train/test split
  • method="anova": Specifies regression tree (continuous outcome) — use "class" for classification
  • Pruning: Use CP table to find optimal complexity parameter — overfitting risk with large trees
  • Packages: rpart (tree building), rpart.plot (visualisation), Metrics (RMSE)
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Object needed: reg_tree from previous step — run full rpart() first
  • CP (complexity parameter): Controls tree size; smaller CP = larger tree = risk of overfitting
  • Cross-validation: printcp() shows xerror (cross-validated error) — choose CP where xerror is minimised
  • Pre-pruning criteria: maxdepth = max tree depth; minsplit = min observations to attempt a split
R
# Pre-pruning with maxdepth and minsplit
reg_tree_es <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                     control=rpart.control(cp=0, maxdepth=6, minsplit=70))
test$pred2 <- predict(reg_tree_es, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred2)
# Result: RMSE reduced from 73.6882 to 69.9848
Post-Pruning: Cost Complexity Pruning

Tree pruning is based on the cost complexity function. Cost complexity pruning is a method used in decision tree algorithms to simplify the tree and prevent it from overfitting the training data.

Cost Complexity Formula

Rα(T) = R(T) + α|T|
TermMeaning
R(T)Training/learning error — error calculated across the leaf nodes of T
|T|Number of leaf nodes in T
αA positive constant that you choose. Determines how much you penalize the complexity of the tree. Higher α = more weight on simplicity = more aggressively pruned tree.

The algorithm calculates the cost complexity factor at each step during the growth of the tree and decides the number of decision nodes in the pruned tree, cutting back some of the branches.

Understanding the printcp() Output

ColumnMeaning
rel errorRelative error = 1 − R² (similar to linear regression)
xerrorCross-validated error
xstdStandard deviation of the cross-validated error
CPCost complexity parameter. Select the cp value (with a simpler tree) that minimizes the xerror.
R
# Display cp table
printcp(reg_tree)

# Display cp value vs cross-validated error
plotcp(reg_tree)

# Find best cp (minimizes cross-validated error)
bestcp <- reg_tree$cptable[which.min(reg_tree$cptable[,"xerror"]),"CP"]

# Build pruned tree using best cp
reg_tree_prune <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                         control=rpart.control(cp=bestcp))
test$pred3 <- predict(reg_tree_prune, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred3)
# Result: error further reduced by about 0.6

# Export tree as postscript file (can convert to PDF)
post(reg_tree_prune, file="reg_tree_prune.ps",
     title="Regression tree for Diabetes dataset")
Lab 2: Decision Tree in R (Diabetes Dataset)

Package: rpart (Recursive Partitioning and Regression Trees)

rpart() Function Arguments

ArgumentDescription
formulaoutcome ~ predictor1 + predictor2 + … + predictorN
dataA data frame in which to interpret the predictors named in the formula
method"class" for classification or "anova" for regression
controlOptional parameters for controlling tree growth (rpart.control)
R
install.packages("rpart")
library(rpart)
library(Metrics)

diabetes <- read.csv("diabetes.csv")
View(diabetes)
str(diabetes)

# Split 70:30
sample_ind <- sample(nrow(diabetes), nrow(diabetes)*0.7)
train <- diabetes[sample_ind,]
test  <- diabetes[-sample_ind,]

# Step 1: Build full tree (cp=0, no pruning)
reg_tree <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                  control=rpart.control(cp=0))
plot(reg_tree)
text(reg_tree, cex=0.5)

# Evaluate
test$pred <- predict(reg_tree, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred)  # RMSE = 73.6882

# Step 2: Pre-pruning
reg_tree_es <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                     control=rpart.control(cp=0, maxdepth=6, minsplit=70))
test$pred2 <- predict(reg_tree_es, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred2)  # RMSE = 69.9848

# Step 3: Post-pruning (cost complexity)
printcp(reg_tree)   # show cp table
plotcp(reg_tree)    # plot cp vs error

bestcp <- reg_tree$cptable[which.min(reg_tree$cptable[,"xerror"]),"CP"]
reg_tree_prune <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                         control=rpart.control(cp=bestcp))
test$pred3 <- predict(reg_tree_prune, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred3)  # Further reduced by ~0.6

post(reg_tree_prune, file="reg_tree_prune.ps",
     title="Regression tree for Diabetes dataset")

RMSE Progression

StepMethodRMSE
1Full tree (no pruning)73.6882
2Pre-pruning (maxdepth=6, minsplit=70)69.9848
3Post-pruning (best cp)~69.4 (further reduced by ~0.6)
