SSE Worked Example — House Price Dataset

▼

To choose the best split at each node, the tree evaluates ALL features and MANY possible thresholds, selecting the split with the lowest combined SSE.

Year# RoomsBuildup (sq ft)Price (Mil RM)
2010315000.20
2015420000.35
2012314500.25
2000316000.15
2018419000.40
2014314500.27
2008525000.45
2011316000.26

Splitting on Year > 2010

✅ Yes branch (Year > 2010): SSE = 0.01732

❌ No branch (Year ≤ 2010): SSE = 0.05167

Why SSE?

  • Tree does not know where to split during training
  • For each candidate split: "How small are the errors if I split here?"
  • Chooses split with lowest combined SSE (lowest variance)
Key distinction: SSE is used during training to choose the best splits. The mean (μ) of the leaf node is used during prediction to output the value. The tree tries Year > 2005, Year > 2010, #rooms > 1, #rooms > 2, etc., and picks the one with the lowest combined SSE.
✂️

Pruning: Pre-pruning vs Post-pruning

▼
Overfitting occurs when a decision tree is too complex — it fits training data too closely and cannot generalise to new data. Solution: Pruning = removing unnecessary splits.

Pre-pruning (Early Stopping)

Stop tree-building before it produces leaves with very small samples.

  • Max Depth — length of longest path from root to leaf
  • Min Samples per Leaf — minimum sample count each leaf must have
  • Min Split — minimum samples in a node to attempt a split (e.g., min split = 5: only split if node has > 5 samples)
  • Max Features — restrict number of features considered at each split

In R: rpart.control(maxdepth=6, minsplit=70)

Post-pruning (Cost Complexity)

Allow the tree to grow fully (until all leaves are pure), then trim it back.

  • Tree grows with no training error — may be heavily overfitted
  • After fully grown: remove branches that do not improve generalisation
  • Performed last-in, first-out — last grown node is first pruned
  • Goal: minimise (output variable variance in validation data + number of terminal nodes)
  • Result: simpler tree that generalises better to new data

In R: prune(tree, cp = best_cp)

Effect on RMSE (Diabetes Dataset)

#ModelRMSE
1Full tree (no pruning)73.69
2Pre-pruning (maxdepth=6, minsplit=70)69.98
3Post-pruning (best cp)~69.4
Motivation for Neural Networks

Use biology as inspiration for mathematical model.

Biological Neurons

  • Two interconnected brain cells (neurons)
  • Possess axons and dendrites that enable the neuron to communicate; transmit and receive electrical and chemical signals
  • A signal is passed over or altered by a synapse. It can also increase or decrease the strength of the connection between neurons

Artificial Neural Network (Perceptron)

  • Has interconnected artificial neurons
  • Possesses similar traits with biological neurons to process information, to learn and to self-organise
Elements of ANN

Single Neuron (Processing Element — PE)

A single neuron with inputs and outputs. Implemented as software simulations of the parallel process of interconnected artificial neurons/elements.

ElementFunction
Connection weight (w)The relative strength (mathematical value) of the input data
Summation functionComputes the weighted sums of all input elements: S = ∑i=1n XiWi
Transfer function (f)Thresholding function — converts the weighted sum into the neuron's output

Formulae

Single neuron: Y = X₁W₁ + X₂W₂

Multiple neurons: Yj = ∑i=1n XiWij

Example Calculation

X₁=3, W₁=0.2; X₂=1, W₂=0.4; X₃=2, W₃=0.1; using Sigmoid transfer function:

  • Summation: Y = 3(0.2) + 1(0.4) + 2(0.1) = 1.2
  • Transfer (Sigmoid): YT = 1/(1 + e−1.2) = 0.77

Practice

X₁=4, W₁=0.3; X₂=1, W₂=0.5; X₃=3, W₃=0.1:

  • Y = 4(0.3) + 1(0.5) + 3(0.1) = 2.0
  • YT = 1/(1 + e−2) = 1/(1 + 0.135) = 0.88
Activation (Transfer) Functions
FunctionRangeFormulaUse Case
Sigmoid (Logistic)[0, 1]YT = 1 / (1 + e−Y)Binary classification output
Hyperbolic Tangent (tanh)[−1, 1]YT = (eY − e−Y) / (eY + e−Y)Hidden layers; outputs centered around 0
Rectified Linear Unit (ReLU)[0, ∞)YT = max(0, Y)Most common in hidden layers; avoids vanishing gradient
Linear(−∞, ∞)YT = YRegression output layer
Neural Network Architecture
LayerRole
Input LayerReceives the raw data (e.g., attributes such as customer, age, income level). Passes information to hidden layers for feature extraction.
Hidden Layer(s)The core of the neural network, capable of learning intricate representations of the input data. Takes input from the previous layer and converts into outputs. The number and size of these layers can be adjusted to capture the complexity of the dataset. Because of the hidden layer, neural networks can be non-linear predictive models.
Output LayerProduces the final predictions based on learned representations. Numeric values as output (e.g., consultation fee for regression; 1/0 for classification). The number of output neurons depends on the number of classes.

Network Types

  • Single Hidden Layer — One hidden layer between input and output; allows non-linearity; needs two weight matrices
  • Deep Neural Network — Deeper architecture; keep stacking hidden layers
  • Single output neuron — For regression or binary classification
  • Multiple output neurons — For multi-class classification (number of neurons = number of classes)

The processing elements (PE) of ANN are the artificial neurons. Each neuron receives inputs, processes them, and delivers a single output. Parallel processing — each PE performs the computations at the same time.

Training Neural Networks: Loss & Backpropagation

Loss

  • Neural network needs to be trained to produce the correct output correctly
  • Neural networks learn to output correct predictions like how humans learn — told what we are doing is right or wrong
  • Neural network learns by comparing the output (prediction) with the output that was meant to produce
  • The difference between the prediction and the ground truth is called error (loss)
  • To train a neural network, the loss needs to be reduced
  • The weights need to be adjusted in such a way that the prediction is close to the actual output (loss is minimized)

Backpropagation

  • Algorithm to compute the gradient of the loss function with respect to each weight
  • Propagates error backwards from output layer through the network
  • Updates weights (W₁, W₂) to reduce prediction error
  • Repeat backpropagation and weight update until predicted value is close to actual value

Forward Propagation (Perceptron)

Inputs → Weights → Sum → Non-linearity (activation) → Output

Regularization

Techniques to make predictive models perform well on new inputs. The model generalizes well outside the training data. To prevent overfitting or a high variance, we must use regularization. Variance is reduced to make the model not sensitive to small fluctuations in training data.

TechniqueDescription
L1 Regularization (Lasso)Penalizes the absolute values of the weights; encourages sparsity (some weights become zero)
L2 Regularization (Ridge)Penalizes the square of the weights; leads to smaller but more evenly distributed weights
Early StoppingStops training before overfitting occurs using a validation set
DropoutRandomly drops out neurons during training to prevent co-adaptation
Early Stopping

A technique that stops the training algorithm before overfitting occurs. Makes use of a set of data (validation set) to determine if the model is overfitting or not.

  1. During training (backpropagation and weight update), evaluate model using validation set
  2. If the validation accuracy is improving → continue training
  3. If the validation accuracy is not improving → stop the training
  4. At each iteration of validation set evaluation, store a copy of the model parameters if the accuracy is improved

h2o Parameters for Early Stopping

ParameterDescriptionDefault
stopping_roundsEarly stopping based on convergence of stopping_metric. Stop if the metric does not improve for k scoring events (0 to disable)5
stopping_metricMetric to use for early stopping (e.g., "rmse", "mse")—
stopping_toleranceRelative tolerance for metric-based stopping (stop if improvement is not at least this much)0
score_intervalShortest time interval (in seconds) to wait between model scoring—
Dropout Regularization

A regularization technique that drops out neurons randomly in a neural network.

  • Each hidden layer is given a probability to remove some of its neurons
  • Neurons of the input layer can also be dropped
  • All the ingoing and outgoing links of the neurons are removed as well
  • Perform training on the diminished network
  • Different iterations — neurons are removed randomly from the network and backpropagation training is performed

Why Dropout Works

  • The neurons cannot co-adapt to other neurons because they cannot expect all the neurons will always be present since neurons are stochastically removed
  • The neurons will be forced to learn good representation of the data while not relying on other neurons
  • Smaller weight parameters make some neurons negligible → neural network becomes less complex → less overfitting
  • During dropout, some neurons get deactivated with a random probability P → neural network becomes less complex → less overfitting

h2o Parameters for Dropout

ParameterDescriptionDefault
activationActivation function. To implement dropout, choose "TanhWithDropout", "RectifierWithDropout", or "MaxoutWithDropout"Rectifier
input_dropout_ratioInput layer dropout ratio (can improve generalization, try 0.1 or 0.2)0
hidden_dropout_ratioHidden layer dropout ratios (can improve generalization), specify one value per hidden layer0.5
Hyperparameter Tuning

Hyperparameter tuning is a critical step in developing an effective neural network regression model. By optimizing the model architecture, learning rate, regularization techniques, and batch size, you can find the right balance between model complexity and generalization. This process may involve grid search, random search, or more advanced techniques like Bayesian optimization to explore the hyperparameter space efficiently.

  1. Model Architecture — Optimize the network layers, neurons, and activation functions
  2. Learning Rate — Find the optimal learning rate to balance convergence and stability
  3. Regularization — Tune regularization techniques like L1/L2 regularization, dropout, and early stopping
  4. Batch Size — Determine the ideal batch size for efficient training
⚖️

How Weights Are Learned (June 2026)

▼
A weight tells the model how important a factor is. Weights are learned automatically — the model is never told what they should be at the start.

Weight Learning Process

  1. Initialise: Model guesses initial weights (random)
  2. Forward pass: Calculate a prediction using current weights
  3. Measure error: Check how wrong the prediction is (loss function)
  4. Backpropagate: Adjust weights little by little using gradient descent
  5. Repeat until prediction converges close to the actual value

Worked Example — HbA1c Prediction

StageValue
Model prediction6.38%
Actual value7.00%
Error−0.62% (too low)
ActionWeights adjusted upward to reduce error in next iteration

Source: Lecturer — Slides June 2026

Performance Metrics Overview
MetricDescriptionGoal
R² (R-squared)Represents the squared correlation between observed outcome values and predicted values by the model. The higher the R², the better the model.Closer to 1 = better
RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error)Measures the average prediction error made by the model in predicting the outcome for an observation. That is, the average difference between the observed known outcome values and the predicted values. Commonly used in climatology, forecasting, and regression analysis to verify experimental results.Lower = better
MAE (Mean Absolute Error)An alternative to the RMSE that is less sensitive to outliers. Corresponds to the average absolute difference between observed and predicted outcomes.Lower = better
R-Squared (R²) — Coefficient of Determination

R² = fraction of variation accounted for (explained by) the fitted line. R-square is also called coefficient of determination.

R² = 1 − (SSres / SStot)
  • R² = 0 → worst possible regression model
  • R² = 1 → perfect model
  • R² = 0.6 → 60% of variations in dependent variable Y can be explained by the independent variables in the regression model

Example Interpretation

92% of the variation in ice cream sales is explained by the temperature of the day.

Review Question: Calculating R²

Given regression line equation: ŷ = 0.143 + 1.229x (number of lectures per day vs hours spent at university).

To calculate R²: find the sum of the residuals squared (SSres) and the total sum of squares (SStot).

In decision tree output: rel error = 1 − R² (same relationship as linear regression)

RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error)
RMSE = √[ (1/n) ∑(yi − ŷi)2 ]
  • Measures average model error in original units of the outcome variable
  • The lower the RMSE, the better the model
  • Residuals are a measure of how far from the regression line data points are; RMSE is a measure of how spread out these residuals are
  • Tells you how concentrated the data is around the line of best fit
  • More sensitive to large errors (outliers) than MAE because errors are squared before averaging
MAE (Mean Absolute Error)
MAE = (1/n) ∑|yi − ŷi|
  • Measures the average magnitude of the errors of the predictions
  • Measures the average absolute difference between the actual and predicted values
  • If absolute value is not taken, the average error becomes the Mean Bias Error — measures average model bias
  • An alternative to RMSE that is less sensitive to outliers
  • The lower the MAE, the better the model
Metrics Summary Table & Model Comparison
ModelDatasetRMSENotes
Linear Regressiondiabetes.csv57.9175Baseline model
Decision Tree (full, cp=0)diabetes.csv73.6882Overfitted
Decision Tree (pre-pruning)diabetes.csv69.9848maxdepth=6, minsplit=70
Decision Tree (post-pruning)diabetes.csv~69.4Best cp from xerror
Neural Network (baseline)consultation_fee.csv0.2247No regularization
Neural Network (early stopping)consultation_fee.csv0.2167stopping_rounds=3, tol=0.05
Neural Network (+ hidden dropout)consultation_fee.csv0.1894hidden_dropout_ratio=c(0.5,0.5)
Neural Network (+ input dropout)consultation_fee.csv0.1864input_dropout_ratio=0.1
Note: Linear regression and decision tree use diabetes.csv (RMSE in original units); neural network uses consultation_fee.csv with normalized Fees (RMSE in normalized scale 0–1).
🧮

MSE & RMSE — Worked Examples (June 2026)

▼

MSE Worked Example

Observed (Y)Predicted (Ŷ)(Y−Ŷ)²
6058(60−58)² = 4
7071(70−71)² = 1
8079(80−79)² = 1
8587(85−87)² = 4
9092(90−92)² = 4

MSE = (4+1+1+4+4) / 5 = 2.8

MSE of 2.8 means: on average, predictions deviate by approximately 2.8 units² from the true exam scores. By squaring, larger errors are penalised more heavily.

RMSE Worked Example

Using the same MSE = 2.8 from above:

RMSE = √MSE = √2.8 ≈ 1.67

Interpretation

  • RMSE = 0 → perfect fit
  • Lower RMSE → better model, predictions closer to true values
  • Higher RMSE → large deviation between residuals and ground truth
  • RMSE is in the same units as the outcome variable (unlike MSE)
RMSE is more interpretable than MSE because the units match the outcome. Use MAE if you have outliers (less sensitive to extreme errors).

Correlation Coefficient (r) — Quick Reference

r valueMeaning
+1Perfect positive relationship — one variable increases, the other increases proportionally
0No relationship between the variables
−1Perfect negative relationship — one increases, the other decreases proportionally

Review Question — R² Interpretation

Regression equation: ŷ = 0.143 + 1.229x (lectures per day vs. hours at university)

If R² = 0.60, this means: 60% of the variation in hours at university is explained by number of lectures per day. The remaining 40% is due to other factors not in the model.

Source: Lecturer — Slides June 2026

Lab 1: Full Linear Regression Script (linear.R)
R
install.packages("Metrics")
library(Metrics)

diabetes <- read.csv("C:/Users/Hp/Documents/regression/diabetes.csv")
View(diabetes)
str(diabetes)

# Split 70:30 train/test
sample_ind <- sample(nrow(diabetes), nrow(diabetes)*0.7)
train <- diabetes[sample_ind,]
test  <- diabetes[-sample_ind,]

# Build linear regression model (all attributes ~.)
lr <- lm(diabetes ~ ., train)
summary(lr)

# Evaluate on test set
test$pred <- predict(lr, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred)
# RMSE = 57.9175
Lab 2: Full Decision Tree Script (decision_tree.R)
R
install.packages("rpart")
library(rpart)
library(Metrics)

diabetes <- read.csv("C:/Users/Hp/Documents/regression/diabetes.csv")
View(diabetes)
str(diabetes)

# Split 70:30
sample_ind <- sample(nrow(diabetes), nrow(diabetes)*0.7)
train <- diabetes[sample_ind,]
test  <- diabetes[-sample_ind,]

# --- Full tree (no pruning) ---
reg_tree <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                  control=rpart.control(cp=0))
plot(reg_tree)
text(reg_tree, cex=0.5)

test$pred <- predict(reg_tree, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred)  # 73.6882

# --- Pre-pruning ---
reg_tree_es <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                     control=rpart.control(cp=0, maxdepth=6, minsplit=70))
test$pred2 <- predict(reg_tree_es, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred2)  # 69.9848

plot(reg_tree_es)
text(reg_tree, cex=0.5)

# --- Post-pruning (cost complexity) ---
printcp(reg_tree)   # CP table: rel error=1-R2, xerror=CV error, xstd=SD of CV error
plotcp(reg_tree)    # Plot cp vs cross-validated error

bestcp <- reg_tree$cptable[which.min(reg_tree$cptable[,"xerror"]),"CP"]

reg_tree_prune <- rpart(diabetes ~ ., train, method="anova",
                         control=rpart.control(cp=bestcp))
test$pred3 <- predict(reg_tree_prune, test)
rmse(test$diabetes, test$pred3)  # Further reduced by ~0.6

post(reg_tree_prune, file="reg_tree_prune.pdf",
     title="Regression tree for Diabetes dataset")
Lab 3: Full Neural Network Script (nn.R)

Dataset: consultation_fee.csv

  • Qualification, Experience, Rating, Place, Profile, Fees
  • Target: Fees.norm (normalized consultation fee)
  • Package: H2O Scalable Machine Learning Platform
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Java required: H2O needs Java ≥ 8 — verify with java -version in your system terminal (not R)
  • RAM: Set max_mem_size ≤ 50% of system RAM (e.g., "2G" if you have 4GB)
  • H2OFrame: Must convert R data.frame to H2OFrame using as.h2o() — lm/rpart objects do NOT work
  • hidden layers: Default is c(200,200) — start simple with c(50,50) for small datasets to avoid overfitting
  • Always shutdown: Run h2o.shutdown(prompt=FALSE) when done to release memory
R
install.packages("h2o")
library(h2o)
library(Metrics)

# Start H2O
h2o.init(nthreads=-1, max_mem_size="2G")

# Load data into H2O (H2O implements its own data.frame)
consultation.frame <- h2o.importFile("consultation_fee.csv")
str(consultation.frame)

# Normalize numeric columns (Experience, Rating, Fees are in different scales)
normalize <- function(x) { return <- ((x - min(x)) / (max(x) - min(x))) }
consultation.frame$Experience.norm <- normalize(consultation.frame$Experience)
consultation.frame$Rating.norm     <- normalize(consultation.frame$Rating)
consultation.frame$Fees.norm       <- normalize(consultation.frame$Fees)

# Split 70:30
split <- h2o.splitFrame(consultation.frame, ratios=0.7)
train  <- split[[1]]
test   <- split[[2]]

# --- Step 1: Baseline NN (mostly default parameters) ---
# x: column numbers of attributes (cols 1,4,5,7,8)
# y: column 9 (Fees.norm, the target)
nn <- h2o.deeplearning(x=c(1,4,5,7,8), y=9,
                        training_frame=train,
                        epochs=500, mini_batch_size=32,
                        hidden=c(20,20), seed=1)
plot(nn)  # plot scoring history
pred <- h2o.predict(nn, test)
rmse(test$Fees.norm, pred)  # RMSE = 0.2247

# --- Step 2: Early Stopping ---
# Stop when RMSE does not improve >=0.05 for 3 consecutive scoring events
nn <- h2o.deeplearning(x=c(1,4,5,7,8), y=9,
                        training_frame=train,
                        epochs=500, mini_batch_size=32,
                        hidden=c(20,20), seed=1,
                        stopping_metric="rmse",
                        stopping_rounds=3,
                        stopping_tolerance=0.05,
                        score_interval=1)
pred <- h2o.predict(nn, test)
rmse(test$Fees.norm, pred)  # RMSE = 0.2167

# --- Step 3: Dropout Regularization (hidden layer) ---
# activation="RectifierWithDropout" required for dropout
# hidden_dropout_ratio: default 0.5 per hidden layer
nn <- h2o.deeplearning(x=c(1,4,5,7,8), y=9,
                        training_frame=train,
                        epochs=500, mini_batch_size=32,
                        hidden=c(20,20), seed=1,
                        stopping_metric="rmse",
                        stopping_rounds=3,
                        stopping_tolerance=0.05,
                        score_interval=1,
                        activation="RectifierWithDropout",
                        hidden_dropout_ratio=c(0.5,0.5))
pred <- h2o.predict(nn, test)
rmse(test$Fees.norm, pred)  # RMSE = 0.1894 (significantly reduced)

# --- Step 4: Add Input Dropout ---
nn <- h2o.deeplearning(x=c(1,4,5,7,8), y=9,
                        training_frame=train,
                        epochs=500, mini_batch_size=32,
                        hidden=c(20,20), seed=1,
                        stopping_metric="rmse",
                        stopping_rounds=3,
                        stopping_tolerance=0.05,
                        score_interval=1,
                        activation="RectifierWithDropout",
                        hidden_dropout_ratio=c(0.5,0.5),
                        input_dropout_ratio=0.1)
pred <- h2o.predict(nn, test)
rmse(test$Fees.norm, pred)  # RMSE = 0.1864 (further reduced)

References

  • https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/h2o/h2o.pdf
  • H2O Deep Learning Tutorial

Topic 7: Factor Analysis

Based on lecture notes & practicals by Lecturer & Lecturer — Biostatistics & Research Methodology Unit, USM

EFA CFA KMO & Bartlett Cronbach's α Principal Axis Promax Rotation lavaan / Mplus
📚

Resources — Lecturer (USM)

▼

TopicLecture NotesPractical

Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) cfa_medstat_lecture.pdf cfa_medstat_practical.html
🌐 Lecturer's DrPH Portal — all lecture notes, practicals & datasets

Last update: January 2024. Use the tabs above — EFA — Lecture and CFA — Lecture — for the lecturer's full content and R code.

Introduction

Research results are no longer trustworthy when an instrument (questionnaire / tool) used to collect the data is not valid. Therefore, the quality of an instrument is absolutely important for meaningful empirical research.

Quality of an instrument can be assessed based on validity and reliability.

Establishing evidence of validity is an ongoing process that must be shared by test developers and test users.

Validity vs Reliability — Dart Board Illustration

Consider the dart boards shown below (consider red circle is on target, other than red circle is off target):

ResultDescription
Results are neither consistent nor accurateDarts scattered randomly — no pattern, not on target
Results are consistent but off-targetDarts clustered together but in the wrong area
Results are both consistent and accurateDarts clustered together and on target — ideal

While reliability is necessary, it alone is not sufficient. Therefore, validity of the questionnaire is necessary.

Overview of Validity & Reliability

Measurement tool / questionnaire

ValidityReliability
Diagnostic testValidity testInternal consistencyStability (Agreement)
A tool vs a gold standardA tool/questionnaireA tool/questionnaireTest-retest / Intra-rater and inter-rater
ROC, Sensitivity, SpecificityContent / Criterion / ConstructCronbach's alpha (Numerical); ICC (Numerical); Kappa (Categorical)—
Types of Validity
  • Content validity — Experts in the field
  • Criterion validity — Comparison with another scale
    • Concurrent — At least two scales to compare; compare with one or more well established scale(s)
    • Predictive — Two scales taken at different timeline
  • Construct validity — Factor analysis
    • Convergent — Items that belong to the same factor share a high proportion of variance in common.
    • Discriminant validity — The extent to which one factor is distinct from the other factor
Construct Validity

Construct — measured by a tool / questionnaire

How well a tool/questionnaire measures a theoretical concept that it is supposed to measure.

Evidence of how relationship between items and components reflect construct.

Analyses:

  • Factor analysis
  • Reliability
Factor Analysis

Factoring — converting the items into factors and then extracting just a few factors which are able to account for a substantial proportion of the variance in the items.

  • Group items that have something in common
  • Simplify long list of items into smaller group or factor

Types of Factor Analyses

  • Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA)
  • Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA)

Common Key Words

  • Construct = factor = concept = domain = idea
  • Factoring = grouping = simplify
PCA vs Factor Analysis
Principal Components Analysis (PCA)Factor Analysis (FA)
Composites descriptive of the information contained in the variables (items)Measured variables are indicators of the factors
Causal flow is from the measured variables to the latent (factor)Causal flow is from the factor to the measured indicator variables
Extraction Methods
  • PCA — Uses 1s on the diagonal of the correlation matrix in the process of extraction. Is not a factor analysis.
  • Principal axis factoring — Uses estimates of communalities (shared variance) on the diagonal in the extraction process
  • Unweighted and generalised least squares factoring — Minimise the off-diagonal differences between observed and reproduced correlation matrices
  • Maximum likelihood — Uses weights proportional to the relatedness of the variables; maximise the probability of having sampled the correlation matrix from a multivariate normally distributed population
  • Alpha factoring — Extraction strategy is designed to maximise the reliability
  • Image factoring — The shared factor variance is estimated via multiple regression to predict the value of the variable
Rotation Methods

Factor rotation is designed to achieve simple structure for interpretation. It is an iterative process of ‘twisting’ the factor structure in the multidimensional space until a satisfactory ‘fit’ is established.

Orthogonal Factor RotationOblique Factor Rotation
Keeps the factors independent of each other during the rotation processDoes not require the rotation process to keep the factors uncorrelated; permits the factors to be correlated up to a certain degree
Methods: Varimax*, quartimax, equimaxMethods: Direct oblimin, promax
Working Example — Exercise Motivation Scale

Researcher would like to determine the validity and reliability of the Exercise Motivation Scale in assessing the motives of exercise among students in USM.

  • Dataset: exercise motivation.sav
  • 7 items: 4 items for Factor Mastery (M1–M4), 3 items for Factor Enjoyment (E1–E3)
  • Likert scale responses
Summary Points
  • Make sure your objective is clearly defined and operationalized.
  • Validity refers to the credibility or believability of the measure. It measures what it is supposed to measure.
  • Reliability is the degree to which an assessment tool produces stable and consistent results.
  • While reliability is necessary, it alone is not sufficient. Therefore, validity of the questionnaire is necessary.
  • Decision of removing a poor item should only be done after adequate theoretical support was carried out (discuss with the experts, re-check the wording of the poor item).
EFA – Step 1: State the Research Question / Hypothesis

Research question:
Is Exercise Motivation Scale valid in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM?

Alternative hypothesis:
Exercise Motivation Scale is a valid questionnaire in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM.

Dataset: exercise motivation.sav

EFA – Step 2: Explore the Data

Explore the data for data entry error, missing values.

  • Check the range of data for each item using Descriptive — check for any data entry error
  • Check the range: minimum and maximum — make sure all items are within the range

SPSS: Descriptive Statistics

  1. Analyze → Descriptive Statistics → Descriptives
  2. Move all items into the Variable(s) box
  3. Click Options → check: Mean, Std. deviation, Minimum, Maximum
  4. Click Continue → OK
EFA – Step 3: Explore the Factors / Domains

Before fixing the number of factors, first run EFA to examine the factor structure using the default extraction (based on eigenvalues > 1) and check the scree plot.

SPSS Steps

  1. Analyze → Dimension Reduction → Factor
  2. Move all items into the Variables box
  3. Click Descriptives → check: Initial solution, Coefficients, Significance levels, KMO and Bartlett's test → Continue
  4. Click Extraction → Method: Principal axis factoring; Extract: Based on Eigenvalue > 1; Display: Unrotated factor solution, Scree plot → Continue
  5. Click Rotation → Method: Promax (oblique); Display: Rotated solution, Loading plot(s) → Continue
  6. Click OK

Key SPSS Output Interpretation

OutputWhat to CheckCut-off
Correlation matrixItems should correlate based on content (convergent); items with different content uncorrelated (discriminant)r >0.3, p <0.05
KMO (Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin)Degree of common variance among items<0.5 don't factor; 0.5–0.59 Miserable; 0.6–0.69 Mediocre; 0.7–0.79 Middling; 0.8–0.89 Meritorious; 0.9–1.00 Marvelous. >0.6 generally recommended
Bartlett's Test of SphericitySufficient non-zero inter-correlations among items to warrant EFAp <0.05 (significant)
Communalities (extraction)% of item variance explained by extracted factors>0.5 recommended (Hair et al., 2010); >0.25 acceptable
EigenvaluesHow worthwhile a factor is in terms of the itemsCut-off EG >1; examine cumulative % variance
Scree plotLook for the arrow pointing to the ‘elbow’ — shows substantial decline. Number of distinct EG above this point = number of factors to retain—
Based on the EG and scree plot, there are two distinct factors. Re-run EFA by fixing the number of factors as ‘2’.
EFA – Step 4: Fix the Number of Factors

Re-run EFA by specifying the exact number of factors to extract (based on findings from Step 3).

SPSS Steps

  1. Analyze → Dimension Reduction → Factor
  2. Move all items into the Variables box
  3. Click Extraction → Method: Principal axis factoring; under Extract, select Fixed number of factors → enter the number (e.g., 2) → Continue
  4. Click Rotation → Promax → Continue
  5. Click OK

Interpreting Pattern Matrix (Factor Loadings)

  • Values represent direct correlations between the items and the factors
  • The first four items have high loadings on Factor 1 and low loadings on Factor 2
  • The last three items show the opposite pattern
  • >0.5 is recommended (Hair et al., 2010)
  • >0.3 is commonly used in EFA
  • Check for cross-loading — one or more items with comparable loadings in two or more factors (≥2 factors). Items with cross-loading problem may be considered for removal.

Interpreting Factor Correlation Matrix

  • Values represent the correlation between items and factors when accounting for the factor inter-correlations
  • <0.85 is recommended (Kline, 2011)
  • If >0.85, there is multicollinearity between the factors — factors are not distinct from each other. May be subjected to be combined as one factor with theoretical support/evidence
  • >0.85 also indicates poor discriminant validity
Repeat the EFA if there is an item removed from the construct.
EFA – Step 5: Results Interpretation

Two factors were extracted among the seven items in Exercise Motivation Scale.

These factors were named as Mastery and Enjoyment.

  • For Factor Mastery, items were found to have communalities ranging from 0.514 to 0.715.
  • For Factor Enjoyment, items were found to have communalities ranging from 0.370 to 0.491.
  • Factor loading ranging from 0.719 to 0.821 for Factor Mastery, and 0.638 to 0.688 for Factor Enjoyment.
  • All items were retained in the EFA model as no deletion was required.
EFA – Step 6: Conclusion

Exercise Motivation Scale is a valid questionnaire in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM.

📝

How to Interpret EFA Findings — Step by Step

▼

1 — KMO (Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin) Measure of Sampling Adequacy

KMO ValueInterpretationProceed with EFA?
< 0.50Unacceptable / Don't factor❌ No — increase sample or revise items
0.50 – 0.59Miserable⚠️ Not recommended
0.60 – 0.69Mediocre⚠️ Proceed with caution
0.70 – 0.79Middling✅ Acceptable
0.80 – 0.89Meritorious✅ Good
0.90 – 1.00Marvelous✅ Excellent

Write-up: "The KMO measure of sampling adequacy was 0.XXX, indicating [level] sampling adequacy, which was [acceptable / good / excellent] for EFA."

2 — Bartlett's Test of Sphericity

ResultInterpretation
p < 0.05 ✅Significant — sufficient correlations among items to proceed with EFA. The correlation matrix is NOT an identity matrix.
p ≥ 0.05 ❌Not significant — items are uncorrelated. EFA is NOT appropriate. Review items.

Write-up: "Bartlett's test of sphericity was statistically significant (χ²([df]) = [value], p < 0.001), indicating that the correlation matrix was significantly different from an identity matrix and EFA was appropriate."

3 — Communalities (h²)

h² ValueInterpretationAction
≥ 0.50Good — item shares ≥50% of its variance with extracted factors✅ Retain
0.25 – 0.49Acceptable (Hair et al., 2010)⚠️ Retain with caution / consider content
< 0.25Poor — item is poorly explained by the factor structure❌ Consider removing

Write-up: "Items were found to have communalities ranging from [min] to [max], indicating that [X%–Y%] of each item's variance was explained by the extracted factors."

4 — Eigenvalues & Number of Factors

MethodRuleNote
Kaiser's RuleRetain factors with eigenvalue > 1Most common; can over- or under-extract
Scree PlotRetain factors above the "elbow" (break/bend)Subjective; use together with Kaiser
Parallel AnalysisRetain factors where observed eigenvalue > simulated eigenvalueMost accurate method (Dr Wan recommends)
Cumulative VarianceRetain enough factors to explain ≥ 60% variance (social sciences)Supplementary criterion

Write-up: "Eigenvalues greater than 1 and inspection of the scree plot suggested [n] factors, together explaining [X]% of total variance."

5 — Factor Loadings (Pattern Matrix)

Loading |λ|InterpretationAction
≥ 0.70Excellent — strong indicator of the factor✅ Retain
0.50 – 0.69Good✅ Retain
0.30 – 0.49Acceptable (commonly used cut-off)✅ Retain; borderline for sample <200
< 0.30Poor / weak loading❌ Consider removing the item
⚠️ Cross-loading: An item that loads ≥ 0.30 on TWO or more factors simultaneously. Remove items with cross-loadings — they do not clearly belong to one factor. Remove the worst cross-loader first, then re-run EFA.

6 — Factor Correlation Matrix

Correlation (r) between factorsInterpretationAction
< 0.85✅ Acceptable — factors are sufficiently distinct (discriminant validity)Keep oblique rotation (Promax)
≥ 0.85❌ Too high — factors may be measuring the same constructConsider merging factors or reviewing item allocation
< 0.32Factors are nearly orthogonalConsider orthogonal rotation (Varimax) instead

Example Write-Up Paragraph (Validity)

"The KMO measure of sampling adequacy was 0.XXX (Marvelous / Meritorious) and Bartlett's test of sphericity was statistically significant (χ²([df]) = [value], p < 0.001), confirming that the data were suitable for factor analysis. Principal axis factoring with Promax rotation was conducted. Based on eigenvalues greater than 1 and inspection of the scree plot, [n] factors were extracted, accounting for [X%] of the total variance. [Number] items were removed due to low communalities (<0.25) or cross-loadings. The final solution retained [n] items across [n] factors, with factor loadings ranging from [min] to [max] and communalities ranging from [min] to [max]. Factor correlations were all below 0.85, confirming discriminant validity."
📤 Expected Output — Lab 3: Neural Network Regression (H2O)h2o.init(): H2O cluster started | Version: X.XX.X h2o.performance(nn_model, newdata=test_h2o): H2ORegressionMetrics: deeplearning MSE: XXXX.XX RMSE: ~50–60 ← should be similar or better than linear (~54) MAE: ~40–50 R²: 0.XX → With n=442: NN may overfit; compare train RMSE vs test RMSE → If test RMSE >> train RMSE → overfitting; increase epochs or add regularisation → Use h2o.varimp() to see feature importance
📤 Expected Output — CP Table (printcp) Root node error: XXXXX / XXX = XXX.XX CP nsplit rel error xerror xstd 1 0.XX 0 1.000 1.05 0.09 ← no splits yet (root) 2 0.XX 1 0.XXX 0.XX 0.0X 3 0.0XX 3 0.XXX 0.XX 0.0X ← minimum xerror → select this CP ... → Use cp = value at minimum xerror row for pruning → plotcp() generates visual: choose leftmost tree whose xerror < min(xerror) + xstd
📤 Expected Output — Lab 1: Linear Regressionstr(diabetes): "data.frame": 442 obs. of 11 variables summary(lm_model): Coefficients (key predictors): bmi XXX.XX *** ← strongest predictor of progression s5 XXX.XX *** Multiple R-squared: 0.518 Adjusted R-squared: 0.507 F-statistic: 46.3 on 10 and 431 DF, p-value: < 2.2e-16 Test set error: RMSE ≈ 54 (units = same as outcome; lower is better) MAE ≈ 44 → R² ≈ 0.52 → model explains ~52% of variance in diabetes progression score
📤 Expected Output — Decision Tree Regressionprintcp(reg_tree): Root node error: XXXXX/XXX = XXX.XX n = XXX CP nsplit rel error xerror xstd 1 0.XX 0 1.00 1.05 0.09 2 0.XX 1 0.XX 0.XX 0.0X ... ← choose row with lowest xerror After pruning: RMSE (test) = ~58–65 (slightly higher than linear regression — tree may underfit) rpart.plot output: Tree diagram with split conditions at each node Leaf nodes show predicted mean ± n observations → If tree RMSE > linear regression RMSE → linear model fits this data better
SPSS: Full EFA Procedure

Step 1: Data Exploration (Descriptives)

  1. Analyze → Descriptive Statistics → Descriptives
  2. Move all scale items into the Variable(s) box
  3. Options → check Mean, Std. deviation, Minimum, Maximum
  4. Continue → OK
  5. Interpret: Check min/max are within valid range; check for data entry errors

Step 2: Initial EFA (Explore Factors)

  1. Analyze → Dimension Reduction → Factor
  2. Move all items to Variables box
  3. Click Descriptives:
    • Statistics: Initial solution
    • Correlation Matrix: Coefficients, Significance levels, KMO and Bartlett's test
    • Continue
  4. Click Extraction:
    • Method: Principal axis factoring
    • Analyze: Correlation matrix
    • Extract: Based on Eigenvalue > 1
    • Display: Unrotated factor solution, Scree plot
    • Continue
  5. Click Rotation:
    • Method: Promax (oblique rotation)
    • Display: Rotated solution, Loading plot(s)
    • Continue
  6. OK

Interpret: Check KMO (>0.6), Bartlett's (p<0.05), eigenvalues, and scree plot to determine number of factors.

Step 3: Fixed-Factor EFA (After determining number of factors)

  1. Analyze → Dimension Reduction → Factor
  2. Move all items to Variables box
  3. Click Extraction:
    • Method: Principal axis factoring
    • Extract: Fixed number of factors → enter number (e.g., 2)
    • Display: Unrotated factor solution
    • Continue
  4. Click Rotation → Promax → Continue
  5. Click Options:
    • Missing Values: Exclude cases listwise
    • Coefficient Display Format: Suppress small coefficients, Absolute value below 0.3
    • Continue
  6. OK

Interpret: Check communalities (>0.5), pattern matrix loadings (>0.3 commonly used), cross-loadings, factor correlation matrix (<0.85).

SPSS: Reliability Analysis (Cronbach's Alpha) — Per Factor

Repeat for each factor identified in EFA.

Example: Factor Mastery (M1–M4)

  1. Analyze → Scale → Reliability Analysis
  2. Move items belonging to this factor (e.g., M1, M2, M3, M4) into the Items box
  3. Model: Alpha
  4. Click Statistics:
    • Descriptives for: Item, Scale, Scale if item deleted
    • Inter-Item: Correlations
    • Continue
  5. OK

Interpret: Check Cronbach's alpha (>0.60 commonly used, should not exceed 0.95); corrected item-total correlation (>0.5); squared multiple correlation (>0.5); Alpha if item deleted (if value > overall alpha, consider removing item).

Example: Factor Enjoyment (E1–E3)

  1. Repeat same steps with items E1, E2, E3
SPSS Output Key Interpretations

KMO & Bartlett's Test

  • KMO — Indicate degree of common variance among the items. Higher KMO indicates items share common factors. Lower KMO indicates lack of common factors among the items. When KMO is high, there are common factors among the items to warrant an EFA.
  • Bartlett's Test of Sphericity — If p <0.05, significant test indicates that there are sufficient non-zero inter-correlations among the items to warrant an EFA.

Communalities Table

  • % of item variance explained by the extracted factors
  • For example, the extraction communality of 0.659 for Mastery 1 is the amount of variance in this item that is explained by the factors
  • >0.5 is recommended (Hair et al., 2010). However, could be based on researcher's judgment on a particular item with communality <0.5. >0.25 is considered acceptable.

Total Variance Explained

  • Eigenvalues (EG) — how worthwhile a factor is in terms of the items
  • Cut-off point for EG >1
  • The cumulative % column indicates that the extracted factors explain approximately X% of the total item variance

Scree Plot

  • Look for the arrow pointing to the ‘elbow’ in the scree plot which shows substantial decline
  • Number of distinct EG above this point — scree plot suggests how many factors should be retained
  • Based on both EG and scree plot: determine number of distinct factors

Pattern Matrix (after rotation)

  • Values represent direct correlations between items and factors
  • Check factor loadings >0.3 (commonly used), >0.5 recommended
  • Check for cross-loading — items with comparable loadings across two or more factors may be considered for removal

Factor Correlation Matrix

  • Values represent correlation between factors
  • <0.85 recommended (Kline, 2011)
  • If >0.85 — multicollinearity between factors; factors not distinct; may be combined with theoretical support. Also indicates poor discriminant validity.

Reliability Output

  • Higher Cronbach's alpha value indicates higher reliability, but should not exceed 0.95
  • >0.95 indicates that some items are redundant
  • >0.60 cut-off value commonly used by researchers
  • Corrected item-total correlation — correlation between value of an item to total value of other items in a construct. >0.5 recommended. Negative correlation indicates reverse coding of the item
  • Squared multiple correlation — High value indicates high contribution of the item to the reliability of the construct. Low value indicates the removal of the item. >0.5 recommended (Hair et al., 2010)
  • Alpha if item deleted — If value is more than overall alpha, might indicate removal of the item to improve the Cronbach's alpha
📝

How to Interpret SPSS EFA Output

▼

Reading Each Section of SPSS Output

SPSS Output SectionWhat to CheckDecision Rule
KMO and Bartlett's TestKMO value; Bartlett's Sig.KMO > 0.60; Bartlett's p < 0.05 → proceed
Communalities tableExtraction column (h²)h² > 0.50 (Hair et al.); > 0.25 acceptable
Total Variance ExplainedEigenvalues > 1; Cumulative %Count eigenvalues >1; cumulative ≥ 60% preferred
Scree PlotElbow pointCount factors above the break/bend
Pattern MatrixFactor loadings per itemLoading > 0.30 (or 0.40); no cross-loading (<0.30 on other factors)
Structure MatrixCorrelation of item with factorCompare with Pattern Matrix to detect cross-loading items
Factor Correlation MatrixCorrelation between factors< 0.85 = discriminant validity confirmed

Pattern Matrix vs Structure Matrix — Key Difference

Pattern Matrix (Use This)

Shows the unique contribution of each factor to each item after accounting for factor intercorrelations. This is the primary table to report for oblique rotation (Promax). Use these loadings for interpretation.

Structure Matrix (Reference Only)

Shows the simple correlation between each item and factor (includes shared variance). Values are always larger than Pattern Matrix. Use to detect cross-loading items (if Pattern is clean but Structure is high on multiple factors).

SPSS Reliability Output — How to Interpret

SPSS OutputWhat It MeansCut-off
Cronbach's AlphaOverall internal consistency of all items in the factor> 0.60 acceptable; > 0.70 good; > 0.80 excellent; < 0.95 (avoid redundancy)
Corrected Item-Total CorrelationCorrelation of each item with the total scale score (excluding that item). Negative value → reverse-code the item.> 0.30 acceptable; > 0.50 good
Squared Multiple CorrelationR² of the item predicted by all other items — measures how well the item fits with the rest> 0.50 recommended (Hair et al., 2010)
Cronbach's Alpha if Item DeletedWhat alpha would be if this item were removedIf this value > current alpha → consider removing the item
Item deletion rule: Remove an item ONLY if (1) Alpha if deleted > current alpha AND (2) Corrected item-total correlation < 0.30. Always consider content validity — do not delete items purely based on statistics if they are theoretically important.
R Code: Setup & Data Import
R
# Install required packages
install.packages("foreign")
install.packages("psych")
install.packages("GPArotation")

# Load libraries
library(foreign)
library(psych)
library(GPArotation)

# Open dataset and explore data
data = read.spss("exercise motivation.sav", use.value.labels = F, to.data.frame = T)
dim(data)   # list number of observations (row) and variables (column)
names(data) # list variable names
head(data)  # list first 6 observations
R Code: Exploratory Factor Analysis
R
# Prepare EFA dataset (remove non-item variables)
dataEFA = subset(data, select = -c(ID, gender))
dim(dataEFA)
names(dataEFA)
head(dataEFA)
describe(dataEFA)

# Step 1: Check assumptions
KMO(dataEFA)              # KMO test — sampling adequacy
cortest.bartlett(dataEFA) # Bartlett test — sphericity

# Step 2: Examine scree plot and eigenvalues
scree = scree(dataEFA)
print(scree)  # look at scree plot and EG, indicates number of factors

# Step 3: Run EFA with principal axis factoring (pa) and promax rotation
fa = fa(dataEFA, nfactors = 2, fm = "pa", rotate = "promax")
print(fa, cut = 0.3, digits = 3)  # cut-off point factor loading 0.3, decimal 3
print(fa, digits = 3)             # check cross-loading

# Notes on output interpretation:
# h2 = item communalities — cut off 0.5 (Hair et al., 2010), but 0.25 is acceptable
#      depends on researcher decision; h2 is the % of item variance explained by the extracted factor
# Correlation between factors (0.301) — acceptable (cut off <0.85; Brown, 2015)
# Stop if result is satisfactory or remove any problematic item

# Step 4: Example of removing an item and re-running
fa1 = fa(dataEFA[-1], nfactors = 2, fm = "pa", rotate = "promax")  # removing item 1
print(fa1, cut = 0.3, digits = 3)
R Code: Internal Consistency (Cronbach's Alpha)
R
# Define factor item groupings
names(dataEFA)
F1 = c("M1", "M2", "M3", "M4")  # Factor 1: Mastery
F2 = c("E1", "E2", "E3")        # Factor 2: Enjoyment

# Factor 1: Mastery
alpha.F1 = alpha(dataEFA[F1])    # Cronbach's alpha for Factor Mastery
print(alpha.F1, digits = 3)

# Factor 2: Enjoyment
alpha.F2 = alpha(dataEFA[F2])    # Cronbach's alpha for Factor Enjoyment
print(alpha.F2, digits = 3)

Interpreting R Output

  • raw_alpha — Cronbach's alpha. Cut-off >0.60; should not exceed 0.95
  • r.cor — Corrected item-total correlation. >0.5 recommended
  • r.drop — Correlation if item removed; similar to corrected item-total
  • alpha if item dropped — If value > overall alpha, consider removing that item
  • h2 — Communalities in fa() output. >0.5 recommended, >0.25 acceptable
Internal Consistency — Cronbach's Alpha Overview

Consistency, repeatability

Cronbach's alpha coefficient is commonly used to determine the internal consistency reliability.

Within each construct, items are correlated to each other, measuring the same construct.

The reliability is checked for each construct identified in EFA.

Internal Consistency – Step 1: State the Research Question / Hypothesis

Research question:
Is Exercise Motivation Scale reliable in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM?

Alternative hypothesis:
Exercise Motivation Scale is a reliable questionnaire in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM.

Internal Consistency – Step 2: Explore the Data

Explore the data for data entry error, missing values.

This step can be skipped if EFA-data exploration is done prior to internal consistency reliability testing.

Internal Consistency – Step 3: Steps in Data Analysis

Factor: Mastery (M1–M4)

SPSS: Analyze → Scale → Reliability Analysis

  1. Move items M1, M2, M3, M4 into the Items box
  2. Model: Alpha
  3. Statistics → check: Item, Scale, Scale if item deleted; Inter-Item Correlations → Continue
  4. OK

SPSS Output — Factor: Mastery

  • Higher value indicates a higher reliability, but should not exceed 0.95
  • >0.95 indicates that some items are redundant
  • >0.60 cut-off value commonly used by researchers
  • Cronbach's alpha is 0.859 — indicates a high level of internal consistency for Factor Mastery
  • Descriptive statistics of the items
  • Correlation among the items

Item-Total Statistics

  • Values indicate the change of Cronbach's alpha if the item is removed
  • From the table, removal of any item would not result in a higher Cronbach's alpha (compare with 0.859). If value is more than 0.859, might indicate removal of the item to improve the Cronbach's alpha.
  • Corrected item-total correlation — correlation between value of an item to total value of other items in a construct. >0.5 recommended. Negative correlation indicates reverse coding of the item.
  • Squared multiple correlation — >0.5 recommended (Hair et al., 2010). High value indicates high contribution of the item to the reliability of the construct. Low value indicates the removal of the item.

Factor: Enjoyment (E1–E3)

  1. Analyze → Scale → Reliability Analysis
  2. Move items E1, E2, E3 into the Items box
  3. Model: Alpha → Statistics (same as above) → Continue → OK

SPSS Output — Factor: Enjoyment

  • Cronbach's alpha of 0.683 indicates acceptable reliability for Factor Enjoyment
  • Corrected item-total correlation is slightly lower than the recommended value for Enjoyment 2
  • Squared multiple correlation is lower than the recommended value for all items
  • However, Cronbach's alpha if item deleted indicates that removing any of the items will not improve the Cronbach's alpha
  • Thus, all three items are retained in the construct
  • Researcher may consider to add more items for Factor Enjoyment
  • Repeat the reliability test if there is an item removed from the construct
Internal Consistency – Step 4: Results Interpretation

For reliability of the questionnaire, internal consistency was assessed using Cronbach's alpha.

Cronbach's alpha for Factor Mastery and Enjoyment were 0.859 and 0.683 respectively which indicated acceptable internal consistency reliability.

No items were marked for deletion.

Internal Consistency – Step 5: Conclusion

Exercise Motivation Scale is a reliable questionnaire in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM.

How to Interpret Reliability Findings

▼

Cronbach's Alpha — Interpretation Scale

Alpha (α)InterpretationAcceptability
< 0.60Poor / Unacceptable❌ Do not use — revise items or scale
0.60 – 0.69Acceptable (marginal)⚠️ Use with caution in exploratory research
0.70 – 0.79Acceptable✅ Generally acceptable
0.80 – 0.89Good✅ Good reliability
0.90 – 0.94Excellent✅ Excellent
≥ 0.95Too high — possible item redundancy⚠️ Review items for overlap/duplication

Reference: Nunnally & Bernstein (1994); George & Mallery (2003); Hair et al. (2010)

Item-Level Statistics — What to Check

StatisticMeaningDecision Rule
Corrected Item-Total Correlation (CITC)Correlation of each item with the remaining items' total score. Low value = item measures something different from the rest.> 0.50 recommended; > 0.30 acceptable. Negative → item is reverse-coded or measuring opposite direction
Squared Multiple Correlation (SMC)R² from regressing the item on all other items. High value = item is well predicted by the other items (consistent).> 0.50 recommended (Hair et al., 2010). Low value → item may not belong to this factor
Alpha if Item DeletedWhat Cronbach's alpha would be if this item were removed from the scale.If > current alpha → removing this item IMPROVES reliability → consider deletion (but weigh against content validity)

Interpretation Decision Tree

Step 1 — Is alpha acceptable?
  → α ≥ 0.60: Proceed. Report alpha and its level (acceptable/good/excellent)
  → α < 0.60: Review items. Check CITC for low values and consider removing items.

Step 2 — Check individual items
  → CITC < 0.30: Flag the item — it may not belong to this construct
  → CITC negative: Item is reverse-coded or measuring the opposite — reverse score it
  → Alpha if deleted > current alpha: Consider removing item (check content validity first)

Step 3 — Final decision
  → Remove the WORST item first (lowest CITC or highest "alpha if deleted")
  → Re-run and check if alpha improves
  → Document all items removed and the reason

Example Write-Up Paragraph (Reliability)

"The internal consistency reliability of each subscale was assessed using Cronbach's alpha. For Factor Mastery (M1–M4), Cronbach's alpha was 0.859, indicating good internal consistency. For Factor Enjoyment (E1–E3), Cronbach's alpha was 0.683, indicating acceptable internal consistency. Corrected item-total correlations ranged from [min] to [max] for Mastery and [min] to [max] for Enjoyment, all exceeding the recommended threshold of 0.30. No items were flagged for deletion as removal of any item would not result in an improvement in Cronbach's alpha. These results indicate that the Exercise Motivation Scale demonstrates acceptable to good internal consistency reliability."

What to Report in a Thesis/Paper

  • Cronbach's alpha (α) for each factor/subscale with interpretation label
  • Number of items per factor
  • Range of corrected item-total correlations (CITC)
  • Any items removed (and reason — statistical + content justification)
  • Final number of items retained per factor
  • Conclusion: "The scale demonstrates [acceptable/good/excellent] reliability"

EFA Written Results (Exercise Motivation Example)

Validity

Two factors were extracted among the seven items in Exercise Motivation Scale. These factors were named as Mastery and Enjoyment. For Factor Mastery, items were found to have communalities ranging from 0.514 to 0.715. For Factor Enjoyment, items were found to have communalities ranging from 0.370 to 0.491. Factor loading ranging from 0.719 to 0.821 for Factor Mastery, and 0.638 to 0.688 for Factor Enjoyment. All items were retained in the EFA model as no deletion was required.

Conclusion: Exercise Motivation Scale is a valid questionnaire in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM.

Reliability

For reliability of the questionnaire, internal consistency was assessed using Cronbach's alpha. Cronbach's alpha for Factor Mastery and Enjoyment were 0.859 and 0.683 respectively which indicated acceptable internal consistency reliability. No items were marked for deletion.

Conclusion: Exercise Motivation Scale is a reliable questionnaire in measuring the motives of exercise among students in USM.

A new questionnaire on exercise self-efficacy is explored for its construct validity and internal consistency. The questionnaire consists of 22 questions rated on a Likert scale of 1 to 5 (dataset: data EFA for exercise.sav). Perform appropriate statistical analyses on the given data using SPSS.

Key Findings from Exercise (by Saufi)

  • KMO: 0.954 (Marvelous) — very high degree of common variance
  • Bartlett's Test: p <0.001 (significant)
  • Initial EFA: First three factors have eigenvalues >1; explain approximately 79.422% of total variance → fix nfactors = 3
  • Item Q21 showed loading >1 — deleted first; EFA rerun
  • Cross-loading items removed one by one: Q3, Q4, Q6, Q8, Q10, Q12, Q14, Q15, Q16, Q18, Q19, Q21, Q22
  • Final 10 items retained: Q1, Q2 (Factor 3); Q5, Q7, Q9, Q11, Q13 (Factor 1); Q17, Q18, Q20 (Factor 2) — no cross-loading
  • Factor correlations all <0.85 — good discriminant validity
  • Factor 1 Cronbach's alpha: 0.939 (high internal consistency)
  • Factor 2 Cronbach's alpha: 0.923

🗂️ DatasetAttitude_Statistics v3.sav

Dataset description: Attitude towards Statistics questionnaire — n=150 respondents, 12 items (Q1–Q12), 5-point Likert scale (1=Strongly Disagree, 5=Strongly Agree). No missing values. Goal: explore the factor structure of this new questionnaire.

📌 Practical Focus: Number of extracted factors · Factor loadings · Factor correlations · Cronbach's alpha
🧠

EFA Overview — What We Are Doing & Why

▼

Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA) is used to explore the latent factor structure of a questionnaire when there is no pre-specified theory. It identifies which items "hang together" (correlate) and form meaningful constructs.

PhaseStepsKey Decision
PreliminaryLoad, clean data; descriptives; normality checkChoose extraction method (ML vs PAF)
Step 1aKMO + BartlettIs data suitable for EFA?
Step 1bEigenvalues + Scree + Parallel AnalysisHow many factors to retain?
Step 2Factor extraction (PAF), rotation (Promax)Which items load on which factor? Remove poor items iteratively.
Step 3Cronbach's alpha, CITCAre factors reliable?

Extraction method choice (Dr Wan): If data are normally distributed → use Maximum Likelihood (ML). If not → use Principal Axis Factoring (PAF) because it does not assume normality (Brown, 2015).

⚙️

Preliminary — Load Libraries & Data

▼

R

library(lattice)  # easy multivariate plots (histograms)

Preliminary — Normality Check (Univariate & Multivariate)

▼

Why check normality? Determines which extraction method to use: ML (normal data) or PAF (non-normal data).

A) Univariate normality — Histograms

R
cat(names(data), sep = " + ")  # generate variable list for copy-paste
# Output: Q1 + Q2 + Q3 + Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q8 + Q9 + Q10 + Q11 + Q12

histogram(~ Q1 + Q2 + Q3 + Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q8 + Q9 + Q10 + Q11 + Q12,
          data = data)
# Graphically looks approximately normal

B) Univariate normality — Shapiro-Wilk test

R
mapply(shapiro.test, data)  # run Shapiro-Wilk for each item

Output (selected):

Q1:  statistic=0.9153, p=1.08e-07  ← p<0.05 → NOT normal
Q2:  statistic=0.8832, p=1.66e-09  ← p<0.05 → NOT normal
Q3:  statistic=0.9078, p=3.76e-08  ← p<0.05 → NOT normal
... (all Q1–Q12: p < 0.05)
❌ Decision: All p-values < 0.05 → All items are NOT normally distributed at univariate level.

C) Multivariate normality — Mardia's test

R
mardia(data)  # Mardia's test of multivariate skewness and kurtosis

Output:

Mardia tests of multivariate skew and kurtosis
n.obs = 150   num.vars = 12
b1p = 29.4    skew = 735.01      p ≤ 2.7e-27   ← significant
b2p = 200.33  kurtosis = 10.8    p ≤ 0          ← z-kurtosis > 5
❌ Decision: kurtosis = 10.8 (should be <5 for normality, Bentler 2006), p < 0.05 → Data are NOT multivariate normal. The Q–Q plot also shows deviation from the straight line at the extremes.
🔵 Conclusion & Decision: Data are NOT normal (both univariate and multivariate). Use Principal Axis Factoring (PAF) as extraction method — it does not require normality.
🔍

Step 1a — Suitability of Data for EFA (KMO & Bartlett)

▼

Before running EFA, verify that the correlation structure of the data is suitable for factor analysis.

1. Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) — Measure of Sampling Adequacy

KMO measures the proportion of variance among items that might be common variance (i.e., shared by factors). Higher KMO = more suitable for EFA.

R
KMO(data)

Output:

Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin factor adequacy
Overall MSA = 0.76
MSA for each item:
  Q1   Q2   Q3   Q4   Q5   Q6   Q7   Q8   Q9  Q10  Q11  Q12
0.34 0.83 0.64 0.75 0.83 0.81 0.82 0.81 0.68 0.70 0.82 0.68
KMO ValueInterpretation
< 0.5Unacceptable
0.5 – 0.59Miserable
0.6 – 0.69Mediocre
0.7 – 0.79Middling (generally acceptable)
0.8 – 0.89Meritorious
0.9 – 1.00Marvelous
⚠️ Interpretation: Overall KMO = 0.76 (Middling) → acceptable for EFA. However, Q1 MSA = 0.34 (unacceptable) → Q1 may be problematic and could be removed in later steps.

2. Bartlett's Test of Sphericity

Tests whether the correlation matrix is significantly different from an identity matrix (all correlations = 0). Significant result = items are sufficiently correlated for EFA.

R
cortest.bartlett(data)

Output:

$chisq  = 562.31
$p.value = 7.85e-80    ← p < 0.05 → significant
$df     = 66
✅ Decision: χ²(66)=562.31, p<0.001 → Statistically significant. Items are correlated → data is suitable for EFA. Proceed!
🔢

Step 1b — Determine Number of Factors

▼

Use multiple criteria — convergence of methods gives more confidence. Dr Wan recommends checking at least: eigenvalue >1, scree plot, and parallel analysis.

Method 1: Kaiser's Eigenvalue > 1 Rule

Eigenvalue = amount of variance in the dataset explained by a factor. Factors with eigenvalue > 1 contain more information than a single item → worth retaining.

R
scree_result = scree(data)
print(scree_result)

Output:

Eigen values of factors:
[1]  2.67  1.78  0.18  0.07  0.02 -0.05 -0.09 -0.15 -0.20 -0.41 -0.46 -0.70
     ^F1   ^F2   <1    <1   ...
✅ Decision: Factors with eigenvalue >1 = 2 factors (EV=2.67 and 1.78). Factor 3 onward (EV=0.18, etc.) <1 → not meaningful.

Method 2: Cattell's Scree Test

"Scree" = loose stones at the base of a cliff. Look for the "elbow" (point before the curve flattens). Retain factors before the elbow.

✅ Decision: Elbow occurs after 2 factors → retain 2 factors.

Method 3: Parallel Analysis (Most Rigorous)

Compares actual eigenvalues to eigenvalues from randomly generated data of same size. Retain only factors whose real eigenvalue exceeds the simulated eigenvalue.

R
parallel = fa.parallel(data, fm = "pa", fa = "fa")
print(parallel)

Output:

Parallel analysis suggests that the number of factors = 2
Eigen values of factors (real data):     2.67  1.78  0.18  0.07 ...
Eigen values of simulated factors:       0.65  0.38  0.29  0.20 ...
← F1: 2.67 > 0.65 ✓   F2: 1.78 > 0.38 ✓   F3: 0.18 < 0.29 ✗
✅ Decision: Parallel analysis confirms 2 factors. Factors 1 and 2 real eigenvalues exceed simulated; Factor 3 does not.
🔵 Final Decision: All three methods agree → retain 2 factors for extraction.
🔄

Step 2 — Extract Factors (PAF + Promax Rotation)

▼

Run EFA: PAF extraction with Promax oblique rotation, 2 factors

R
fa_result = fa(data,
               nfactors = 2,        # number of factors from Step 1b
               fm = "pa",           # Principal Axis Factoring (non-normal data)
               rotate = "promax")   # oblique rotation (factors may correlate)
print(fa_result, cut = 0.3, digits = 3)
# cut=0.3 hides loadings below 0.3 (reduces clutter)
🔵 Why Promax? Dr Wan recommends oblique rotation (Promax) for most social/health science questionnaires because constructs realistically correlate. Varimax assumes independence (often unrealistic). If factor correlations turn out to be <0.32, orthogonal rotation would also be acceptable.

What to examine in the output:

Output ElementWhat to CheckCut-off
Factor Loadings (PA1, PA2)Each item's loading on each factor≥0.5 = practically significant; ≥0.7 = well-defined
h2 (Communality)% item variance explained by all factors>0.25 (loading ≥0.5 ↔ h2 ≥0.25)
Cross-loadingItem loads comparably on 2+ factorsDifference <0.2 = cross-loading → remove item
Factor Correlation ($Phi)Correlation between factors<0.85 = discriminant validity OK

Factor Loading Interpretation (Hair Jr. et al., 2009):

Loading (absolute)Interpretation
0.3 – 0.4Minimally acceptable (keep only if communality is adequate)
≥ 0.5Practically significant ✓
≥ 0.7Well-defined structure ✓✓

Check factor correlations:

R
fa_result$Phi   # factor correlation matrix
✅ Interpretation: If |r| < 0.85 between factors → discriminant validity confirmed. If |r| > 0.85 → factors are too similar, consider merging them.

🔁 Iterative Item Removal Process:

  1. Run fa() → inspect pattern matrix
  2. Identify items with: (a) loading <0.5 on all factors, or (b) cross-loading (similar loading on 2 factors, difference <0.2), or (c) communality <0.25
  3. Remove one item at a time (worst item first)
  4. Re-run EFA and repeat until all remaining items have acceptable loadings
⚠️ Note about Q1: Q1 had KMO MSA=0.34 (unacceptable) in Step 1a — it is likely to show a low loading or cross-loading and may be a candidate for removal.
🎯

Step 3 — Reliability (Cronbach's Alpha)

▼

After finalising item groupings from EFA, test each factor's internal consistency reliability using Cronbach's alpha.

R
# After determining which items belong to each factor from Step 2:
F1_items = c("Q1","Q2","Q3","Q4","Q5","Q6")   # adjust based on your EFA results
F2_items = c("Q7","Q8","Q9","Q10","Q11","Q12")

alpha_F1 = alpha(data[F1_items])
print(alpha_F1, digits = 3)
# Key output: raw_alpha, item.total (CITC), alpha.if.drop

alpha_F2 = alpha(data[F2_items])
print(alpha_F2, digits = 3)

What to examine in alpha() output:

Output ElementWhat It MeansAction
raw_alphaOverall Cronbach's alpha for the factorTarget ≥0.70
item.total (CITC)Correlation of each item with sum of remaining itemsIdeally >0.5; negative CITC → reverse-code item
alpha.if.dropAlpha if that item is removedRemove item only if improvement is marked

Cronbach's alpha interpretation — DeVellis (2012):

α ValueInterpretation
< 0.60Unacceptable
0.60 – 0.65Undesirable
0.65 – 0.70Minimally acceptable
0.70 – 0.80Respectable ✓
0.80 – 0.90Very good ✓✓
> 0.90Consider shortening — items may be redundant

Also: Bland & Altman (1997): satisfactory = 0.7–0.8; clinical use: >0.9

💻

Complete R Code — Dr Wan's Practical (Copy-Ready)

▼
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Object required: data loaded from import step — must not contain ID or group columns
  • Remove non-items: Use data = data[-1] to drop the first column if it is an ID variable
  • Expected item range: Know your scale (e.g., 1–5 Likert) to spot out-of-range values
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Input: Subset of data with only the items belonging to that factor
  • Item directionality: Reverse-score negatively worded items BEFORE computing alpha
  • Acceptability: α ≥ 0.70 = acceptable; ≥ 0.80 = good; ≥ 0.90 = excellent (but >0.95 may indicate redundancy)
  • If α < 0.70: Check "alpha if item deleted" column — remove the item with biggest improvement
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Factor structure required: Must define the CFA model from EFA results or theory — assign items to factors first
  • Sample size: Minimum n ≥ 100; prefer n ≥ 200 for stable SEM/CFA estimates
  • Data type: Continuous indicators → use estimator="ML"; ordinal/Likert → use estimator="WLSMV"
  • Multivariate normality: Check with Mardia's test; if violated → use estimator="MLR" (robust ML)
  • Identification: Each factor needs ≥ 3 indicators (2 with fixed factor variance); model must be identified
  • Package: lavaan — install once, load each session; semPlot for path diagrams
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Model specification: Must define measurement model (CFA) AND structural paths before fitting
  • Sample size: Minimum n ≥ 200; SEM is sensitive to small samples (ML estimator needs n ≥ 200)
  • Estimator: "ML" for continuous, multivariate-normal data; "MLR" if non-normal; "WLSMV" for ordinal
  • Model identification: Check degrees of freedom ≥ 0; each latent variable needs ≥ 3 indicators or constraints
  • Modification indices: Use with caution — only modify model if theoretically justified; risk of capitalising on chance
  • Packages: lavaan (fitting), semPlot (diagram), semTools (reliability, AVE)
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Mediation (indirect effects): Define a, b paths; compute indirect := a*b in lavaan model string
  • Bootstrapping: Use se="bootstrap" and bootstrap=1000 for robust CI of indirect effect
  • Moderation: Requires creating an interaction term (X × Z) BEFORE specifying the model
  • Baron & Kenny steps: Deprecated — use bootstrapped indirect effect (Hayes PROCESS or lavaan with boot CI) instead
  • Effect size: Report proportion mediated = indirect / total; κ² (kappa squared) for indirect effect size
⚠️ Prerequisites / Criteria to Run This Code
  • Complete workflow: This is the full EFA practical script — run section by section, not all at once
  • Dataset: Attitude_Statistics v3.sav — 19 attitude items + 1 ID column; n = XXX
  • Packages: foreign, psych, GPArotation all loaded
  • ID column: Step 0 removes the first column (ID) — ensure column 1 is actually the ID, not an item
  • Step-by-step: Check output of each step before proceeding to the next
R
# ══ EFA Practical — Lecturer, USM (Jan 2024) ════════════════════════

# ── 0. Setup ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
library(foreign)   # read SPSS .sav
library(psych)     # KMO, fa, alpha, mardia, scree, fa.parallel
library(lattice)   # histograms

# ── 1. Load & prepare data ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
data = read.spss("Attitude_Statistics v3.sav",
                 use.value.labels = FALSE, to.data.frame = TRUE)
str(data); names(data); head(data)   # 150 obs, 13 vars (ID + Q1-Q12)
data = data[-1]                      # remove ID column → 12 items remain
str(data); names(data); head(data)

# ── 2. Descriptive statistics ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
describe(data)             # check n, min, max (all 150; 1–5 for all items)
response.frequencies(data) # % response per option; check for 0 missing

# ── 3. Normality check ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# A) Univariate: histograms
cat(names(data), sep = " + ")  # generate variable list
histogram(~ Q1 + Q2 + Q3 + Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q8 + Q9 + Q10 + Q11 + Q12,
          data = data)

# B) Univariate: Shapiro-Wilk (per item)
mapply(shapiro.test, data)   # all p < 0.05 → NOT normal → use PAF

# C) Multivariate: Mardia's test
mardia(data)
# kurtosis = 10.8, p < 0.001 → NOT multivariate normal → use PAF

# ── 4. Step 1a: Suitability ───────────────────────────────────────────────────
KMO(data)                 # Overall MSA = 0.76 (middling, acceptable); Q1 = 0.34 (poor)
cortest.bartlett(data)    # χ²(66)=562.31, p<0.001 → items are correlated → proceed

# ── 5. Step 1b: Number of factors ────────────────────────────────────────────
scree_result = scree(data)
print(scree_result)        # Eigenvalues of FA: 2.67, 1.78 >1 → 2 factors (Kaiser)
                           # Scree plot: elbow after 2 factors (Cattell)

parallel = fa.parallel(data, fm = "pa", fa = "fa")
print(parallel)            # Parallel analysis → 2 factors confirmed

# ── 6. Step 2: Extract factors (PAF + Promax) ─────────────────────────────────
fa_result = fa(data, nfactors = 2, fm = "pa", rotate = "promax")
print(fa_result, cut = 0.3, digits = 3)
# Examine: factor loadings (≥0.5), communalities h2 (>0.25), cross-loadings
fa_result$Phi   # factor correlation (should be <0.85)

# If items need removal (low loading, cross-loading, h2<0.25):
# Remove one item at a time (worst first), re-run fa(), repeat until clean

# ── 7. Step 3: Reliability (Cronbach's alpha) ─────────────────────────────────
# Adjust item lists based on your final EFA factor groupings:
F1 = c("Q2","Q3","Q4","Q5","Q6")    # example items — check your pattern matrix!
F2 = c("Q7","Q8","Q9","Q10","Q11","Q12")  # adjust accordingly

alpha_F1 = alpha(data[F1])
print(alpha_F1, digits = 3)   # raw_alpha (≥0.70?), CITC (>0.5?), alpha.if.drop

alpha_F2 = alpha(data[F2])
print(alpha_F2, digits = 3)

EFA Decision Flowchart & Common Problems

▼

Item removal decision (Step 2):

ProblemWhat to Look ForAction
Low loadingAll loadings <0.3 (or all <0.5)Remove item
Cross-loadingComparable loadings on ≥2 factors (difference <0.2)Remove item
Low communalityh2 < 0.25Remove item
Negative CITCNegative item-total correlationCheck item wording — may need reverse-coding
High factor correlation$Phi > 0.85Consider merging the two factors into one

Reporting EFA results (example sentence):

"The EFA using principal axis factoring with Promax rotation revealed a two-factor structure explaining XX% of total variance. The KMO value was 0.76 (acceptable) and Bartlett's test was significant (χ²(66)=562.31, p<0.001). Factor loadings ranged from 0.XX to 0.XX. Internal consistency was acceptable with Cronbach's alpha of 0.XX (Factor 1) and 0.XX (Factor 2)."

How to Interpret Dr Wan's EFA Output (R / psych)

▼

Reading the fa() Output in R

R Output ElementLabel in OutputWhat It MeansCut-off
Factor loadingsPA1, PA2, … columnsCorrelation of each item with each factor after rotation. Primary loading = the factor where the item has the highest value.≥ 0.30 (include); ≥ 0.50 preferred
Communalityh2% of item's variance explained by all extracted factors. Low h² = item is poorly captured by the factor structure.≥ 0.50 preferred; ≥ 0.25 acceptable
Uniquenessu21 − h². The proportion of variance unique to the item (not shared with any factor). u2 = 1 − h².Lower is better
ComplexitycomHow many factors each item loads on (1.0 = loads purely on one factor). Values > 1.5 suggest cross-loading.≈ 1.0 ideal; > 1.5 = cross-loading concern
Factor correlationsWith factor correlations / PhiCorrelation between the extracted factors. If high (≥ 0.85), factors may be indistinct.< 0.85 (discriminant validity)
EigenvaluesSS loadingsSum of squared loadings for each factor = factor's contribution to total variance> 1.0 (Kaiser rule)
Proportion VarianceProportion Var% of total variance explained by each factorSum ≥ 0.60 (60%) preferred
Cumulative VarianceCumulative VarCumulative % explained by all factors together≥ 0.60 (60%) preferred

Parallel Analysis (fa.parallel) — How to Interpret

Parallel Analysis generates random simulated data with the same n and p. It compares the observed eigenvalues against those from random data:

  → Observed eigenvalue > simulated eigenvalue: Factor is real — RETAIN it
  → Observed eigenvalue < simulated eigenvalue: Factor is due to chance — DO NOT retain

Decision: Count the number of factors where the blue line (observed) is ABOVE the red dashed line (simulated). This is the number of factors to retain.

Example: If parallel analysis suggests 3 factors, set nfactors = 3 in fa()

KMO() Output in R

R OutputWhat to Report
Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin factor adequacy — Overall MSAThe overall KMO value — apply the Kaiser (1974) label table (Miserable to Marvelous)
MSA for each itemIndividual item KMO. Items with MSA < 0.50 should be removed (they reduce overall KMO)

Item Removal Process (Iterative)

Remove ONE item at a time, then re-run EFA. Priority for removal:
  1. Items with individual KMO < 0.50
  2. Items with h² < 0.25 (low communality)
  3. Items with cross-loading (com > 1.5; loads ≥ 0.30 on ≥ 2 factors) — remove the item with the highest cross-factor loading first
  4. Items with loading < 0.30 on all factors

Example Write-Up — Dr Wan's EFA (Attitude towards Statistics)

"Exploratory factor analysis was conducted on the Attitude towards Statistics questionnaire (n = 150, 12 items). Prior to extraction, data suitability was confirmed: the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) measure of sampling adequacy was 0.XXX, and Bartlett's test of sphericity was statistically significant (χ²([df]) = [value], p < 0.001). Parallel analysis suggested retaining [n] factors. Principal axis factoring (PAF) with Promax rotation was applied. Items Q[x], Q[y] were removed sequentially due to [low communality / cross-loading]. The final model retained [n] items across [n] factors, with factor loadings ranging from [min] to [max] (Pattern Matrix, loadings < 0.30 suppressed) and communalities ranging from [min h²] to [max h²]. The inter-factor correlation was [value] (r < 0.85), confirming discriminant validity (Brown, 2015). Internal consistency for each factor was assessed using Cronbach's alpha: Factor 1 (α = 0.XXX) and Factor 2 (α = 0.XXX), both indicating [acceptable/good] reliability."
