🚀

CFA Summary — Simplified (Quick Reference)

▼
What is CFA? Confirmatory Factor Analysis confirms whether your survey items actually measure the constructs you think they measure. It tests a pre-specified factor structure — unlike EFA which explores one.

✅ Step 1: Check Data Quality

Before running CFA, check normality of each item:

Data DistributionUse This Estimator
Normally distributedML (Maximum Likelihood)
Mildly non-normalMLR (Robust ML)
Severely non-normal / ordinalWLSMV

Check: Mardia's test — skewness p < 0.05 → non-normal → use MLR/WLSMV

✏️ Step 2: Write the Model

Specify which items belong to which construct using lavaan syntax:

model = "
  depression =~ Q1 + Q2 + Q3
  anxiety    =~ Q4 + Q5 + Q6
"
fit = cfa(model, data = df,
          estimator = "ML")

=~ means "is measured by". Each line = one latent construct and its indicators.

🔍 Step 3: Check Results — Three Things

A. Model Fit — Does model match data?
IndexGood Fit
CFI> 0.95
TLI> 0.95
RMSEA< 0.08
SRMR< 0.08

✅ All good → Model fits. Proceed.
❌ Any fail → Check Modification Indices.

B. Factor Loadings (λ) — Do items measure the construct?
LoadingMeaning
> 0.70Excellent ✅
0.50 – 0.70Good ✅
< 0.50Weak — remove ❌
> 1.0Heywood case ⚠️

Simple formula:
Q1 = λ × Construct + Error
e.g. Q1 = 0.82 × Depression + e
→ 0.82 = how strongly Q1 measures depression

C. Factor Correlations — Are factors distinct?
r between factorsMeaning
< 0.85✅ Factors distinct
≥ 0.85⚠️ Too similar

If > 0.85 → factors may be measuring the same thing → consider merging them

🔧 If Model Doesn't Fit — Use Modification Indices (MI)

modificationIndices(fit, sort. = TRUE, maximum.number = 10)

MI valueAction
> 10 (large)High priority to fix
3.84 – 10Consider if theoretically justified
< 3.84Ignore
Two Common Fixes:
  1. Correlate error terms — for items with similar wording
    Q9 ~~ Q10 (add to model)
  2. Remove the item — if it loads poorly or causes misfit
⚠️ Each change must be theoretically justified. Change ONE at a time, then re-run. Watch for Heywood cases (λ > 1.0) after adding correlated errors.

📋 What to Report in Your Write-Up

  1. Estimation method (ML / MLR / WLSMV) + normality test result
  2. Fit indices table — CFI, TLI, RMSEA (90% CI), SRMR for each model version
  3. Factor loadings table — standardised λ + p-value for each item
  4. Model revision details — which items removed / errors correlated, and why
  5. Path diagram — semPaths(fit, ...)
  6. Reliability — McDonald's Omega (ω > 0.70) per factor
  7. Convergent validity — AVE ≥ 0.50 per factor
🔍

CFA Overview — What & Why

▼
Aspect EFA CFA
Purpose Explore factor structure (data-driven) Confirm a hypothesised structure (theory-driven)
Items per factor Determined by loadings Pre-specified by researcher
Method PAF, ML, etc. ML, MLR (robust), WLSMV
Model fit Not formally tested Evaluated via fit indices (CFI, TLI, RMSEA, SRMR)
Software (R) psych package lavaan + semTools + semPlot
CFA Workflow (3 Steps):
Step 1 → Specify model (lavaan syntax)
Step 2 → Fit model & evaluate fit indices + localized misfit
Step 3 → Revise model if needed → composite reliability (ω)
⚙

Preliminary — Setup & Data Preparation

▼

Libraries required:

# Install once if not installed:
# install.packages(c("foreign","psych","lavaan","semTools","semPlot"))

library(foreign)    # read SPSS .sav files
library(psych)      # descriptive stats, mardia test
library(lavaan)     # CFA model specification and fitting
library(semTools)   # composite reliability (compRelSEM)
library(semPlot)    # path diagram (semPaths)

Load dataset — use only items retained from EFA (Q4–Q11):

medical = read.spss("medical_devices.sav", to.data.frame = TRUE)

# Select only EFA-retained items: Q4 to Q11
# Q1, Q2, Q3 were removed during EFA
med_cfa = medical[, c("Q4","Q5","Q6","Q7","Q8","Q9","Q10","Q11")]
💡 Why Q4–Q11 only? EFA showed Q1 (KMO=0.34, poor), Q2, Q3 had low communality or cross-loadings. CFA starts from items that survived EFA screening.
📊

Preliminary — Descriptive Statistics & Normality Test

▼
# Descriptive statistics
describe(med_cfa)

# Mardia's multivariate normality test
mardia(med_cfa)

Descriptive output (n=150, 8 items, no missing values):

ItemnMeanSDMinMaxSkewKurtosis

Mardia's Multivariate Normality Result:
Mardia Kurtosis = 8.18, p < 0.001
→ NOT multivariate normal (kurtosis > 5 is problematic)
→ Must use estimator = "MLR" (Yuan-Bentler robust ML)
→ MLR provides robust standard errors and Satorra-Bentler scaled χ² statistic
📐

Step 1 — Model Specification (lavaan Syntax)

▼

Based on EFA results: PA1 = perceived advantage; PA2 = perceived ease of use

# Define the CFA model using lavaan syntax
# =~  means "is measured by" (factor to items)
# ~~  means "correlates with" (residual or factor correlation)

model1 = '
  PA1 =~ Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  PA2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
'
# PA1: 5 items (Q4, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q11)
# PA2: 3 items (Q8, Q9, Q10)
# By default lavaan freely estimates PA1~~PA2 (factor correlation)
lavaan OperatorMeaningExample
=~Measured by (factor loading)PA1 =~ Q4 + Q5
~~Covariance / correlationPA1 ~~ PA2 (factor); Q9 ~~ Q10 (residual)
~1Intercept / meanQ4 ~ 1
1*Fix parameter (e.g. marker var)1*Q4 (fix loading = 1)
💡 Identification rules:
• Each factor needs ≥ 3 indicators for identification
• First indicator of each factor is fixed to 1 (marker variable method, lavaan default)
• Model df = p(p+1)/2 − free parameters (must be ≥ 0 for identified model)
🔨

Step 2 — Fit Model & Evaluate Global Fit Indices

▼
# Fit CFA model using MLR estimator (robust to non-normality)
fit1 = cfa(model1, data = med_cfa, estimator = "MLR")

# Model summary: fit indices + standardized solution
summary(fit1, fit.measures = TRUE, standardized = TRUE)

Global Fit Indices — Initial Model (model1):

IndexCut-offInitial Model ValueDecision
χ² (Scaled) p-valuep > 0.05 χ²(19) = 27.373, p = 0.096 ✓ Acceptable
CFI≥ 0.95 0.972 ✓ Good
TLI (NNFI)≥ 0.95 0.958 ✓ Good
RMSEA≤ 0.08 0.054 (90% CI: 0.000–0.091) ⚠ Upper CI = 0.091 > 0.08
SRMR≤ 0.08 0.079 ⚠ Borderline (close to 0.08)
⚠ RMSEA note: Point estimate (0.054) is acceptable, but upper 90% CI = 0.091 > 0.08 threshold. Ideally the entire CI should be ≤ 0.08. This warrants checking for localized misfit.
💡 Why "Scaled" χ²? Because MLR estimator was used. The Satorra-Bentler scaled χ² adjusts for non-normality — it is more reliable than standard ML χ². Always report the scaled version with MLR.
📈

Step 2 — Factor Loadings & Factor Correlation

▼
# Standardized solution: key output for CFA interpretation
standardizedSolution(fit1)

# Full estimates with SE and 95% CI
parameterEstimates(fit1, standardized = TRUE)

Factor Loadings — Standardized (Std.all) column:

FactorItemStd.all (λ)Decision
PA1Q40.814✓ > 0.5 (Excellent)
PA1Q50.624✓ > 0.5 (Good)
PA1Q60.708✓ > 0.5 (Good)
PA1Q70.735✓ > 0.5 (Good)
PA1Q110.544⚠ Marginal (≥0.5)
PA2Q80.655✓ > 0.5 (Good)
PA2Q90.844✓ Excellent
PA2Q100.856✓ Excellent

Factor Correlation — PA1 ~~ PA2:

PairStd.allCut-offDecision
PA1 ~~ PA20.124 < 0.85✓ Discriminant validity confirmed
Interpretation criteria:
• Std.all ≥ 0.7 = excellent; ≥ 0.5 = acceptable; < 0.5 = weak (consider removal)
• Factor correlation < 0.85 = discriminant validity (factors measure distinct constructs)
• Factor correlation > 0.85 = factors too similar (convergent — consider merging)
🔎

Step 2 — Localized Misfit: Modification Indices & Standardized Residuals

▼
# Modification Indices (MI): expected chi-sq improvement if parameter freed
modindices(fit1, sort. = TRUE, maximum.number = 10)

# Standardized Residuals (SR): observed vs model-implied correlation discrepancy
residuals(fit1, type = "standardized")

Modification Indices (MI) — Threshold: MI > 3.84:

ParameterMIEPCStd.allAction
Q9 ~~ Q1010.2640.1480.356 ⚠ Flag — consider residual correlation
PA1 =~ Q810.264-0.349-0.349 ✓ Ignore — cross-loading, no content justification
PA2 =~ Q58.3590.2850.285 ✓ Ignore — cross-loading, no content justification
Q5 ~~ Q116.3010.0990.260 ✓ Ignore — not theoretically justifiable

Standardized Residuals (SR) — Threshold: |SR| > 2.58:

Item PairSRDecision
Q5 — Q83.455 ✗ |SR| > 2.58 — Problem pair flagged
All other pairs< |2.58| ✓ Acceptable
Decision Logic:
1. MI flagged: Q9~~Q10 (MI=10.264) → potential residual correlation → Revision 1: add Q9~~Q10
2. SR flagged: Q5–Q8 (SR=3.455) → Q5 appears in both SR flag and MI (PA2=~Q5)
3. Cross-loading MIs (PA1=~Q8, PA2=~Q5) → ignore (not content-justified)
4. Q5 is common problematic item → Revision 2: remove Q5
→ Try both revisions, choose the admissible one (no Heywood case)
⚠

Step 3 — Model Revision 1: Add Q9~~Q10 → REJECTED (Heywood Case)

▼
# Revision 1: Add residual correlation between Q9 and Q10
model1_r1 = '
  PA1 =~ Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  PA2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
  Q9 ~~ Q10    # Allow residual correlation (error covariance)
'

fit1_r1 = cfa(model1_r1, data = med_cfa, estimator = "MLR")
summary(fit1_r1, fit.measures = TRUE, standardized = TRUE)
# WARNING: Check for Heywood case!
⛔ HEYWOOD CASE DETECTED — Revision 1 REJECTED

Output showed:
• Q8 standardized loading = 1.522 (impossible — standardized loading cannot exceed 1.0!)
• Negative residual variance for Q8 (variance must be positive!)

What is a Heywood case?
An inadmissible solution where the CFA estimator produces mathematically impossible values:
• Standardized factor loading > 1.0
• Negative error (residual) variance
• Factor correlation > 1.0

Why does it occur?
• Model is misspecified (wrong structure)
• Too few indicators for a factor (PA2 only has 3 items — adding residual correlation over-parameterizes)
• Multicollinearity among Q9 and Q10 (both high loadings: 0.844, 0.856)
• Small sample relative to model complexity

Rule: Any Heywood case = inadmissible solution → ALWAYS reject
✔

Step 3 — Model Revision 2: Remove Q5 → ACCEPTED (Final Model)

▼
# Revision 2: Remove Q5 from PA1
# Q5 flagged in both SR (Q5-Q8 = 3.455) and MI (PA2=~Q5 = 8.359)
model2 = '
  PA1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11    # Q5 removed
  PA2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
'

fit2 = cfa(model2, data = med_cfa, estimator = "MLR")
summary(fit2, fit.measures = TRUE, standardized = TRUE)

# Verify no Heywood cases, no SR > 2.58
standardizedSolution(fit2)
residuals(fit2, type = "standardized")
modindices(fit2, sort. = TRUE, maximum.number = 10)

Fit Comparison — Initial (model1) vs Final (model2):

IndexCut-offModel 1 (initial)Model 2 (final)Verdict
χ² p-value> 0.05p=0.096 ✓p=0.342 ✓Improved
df—1913—
CFI≥ 0.950.972 ✓0.994 ✓Improved
TLI≥ 0.950.958 ✓0.990 ✓Improved
RMSEA≤ 0.080.054 ⚠ (CI: 0.000–0.091)0.027 (CI: 0.000–0.079) ✓All CI now ≤ 0.08
SRMR≤ 0.080.079 ⚠ (borderline)0.063 ✓Improved
HeywoodNoneNone ✓None ✓OK
|SR| > 2.58NoneQ5–Q8=3.455 ✗None ✓Resolved

Factor Loadings — Final Model (Std.all):

FactorItemStd.all (λ)Decision
PA1Q40.806✓ Excellent
PA1Q60.718✓ Good
PA1Q70.762✓ Good
PA1Q110.509⚠ Marginal (≥ 0.5)
PA2Q80.653✓ Good
PA2Q90.844✓ Excellent

Factor Correlation (Final): PA1 ~~ PA2 Std.all = 0.078 < 0.85 ✓ Discriminant validity confirmed

⚖

Model Comparison — AIC / BIC

▼
# Compare model1 (initial) vs model2 (final)
# anova() gives Satorra-Bentler chi-sq difference test for MLR models
anova(fit1, fit2)

# AIC / BIC comparison
fitMeasures(fit1, c("aic","bic"))   # model1: AIC=3166, BIC=3217
fitMeasures(fit2, c("aic","bic"))   # model2: AIC=2791, BIC=2836
# Lower AIC and BIC = better fitting model

AIC / BIC Results:

ModelAICBICDecision
model1 (initial, Q4-Q11)31663217—
model2 (final, Q4,Q6,Q7,Q8,Q9,Q10,Q11) 27912836 ✓ Lower AIC & BIC = Better
💡 AIC vs BIC interpretation:
• Lower AIC / BIC = better fitting model (always)
• AIC penalises complexity less → useful when prediction is priority
• BIC penalises complexity more → favours parsimony (preferred in confirmatory work)
• Both agree here: model2 is preferred
• For non-nested models, use AIC/BIC; for nested models (same items, different params), use χ² difference test (anova output with MLR)
🔗

Composite Reliability — McDonald's Omega (ω)

▼
# Composite reliability omega from semTools
# McDonald's omega is preferred over Cronbach's alpha for CFA
compRelSEM(fit2)

# Output:
#   PA1   PA2
# 0.809 0.836

Omega (ω) Results — Final Model:

FactorωCut-offDecision
PA1 (Q4, Q6, Q7, Q11)0.809≥ 0.70 acceptable; ≥ 0.80 good✓ Good
PA2 (Q8, Q9, Q10)0.836≥ 0.70 acceptable; ≥ 0.80 good✓ Good
💡 Why ω (omega) over Cronbach's α for CFA?
• Cronbach's α assumes equal factor loadings (tau-equivalence) — rarely met
• McDonald's ω accounts for unequal factor loadings — more accurate for CFA
• ω is model-based: computed directly from CFA parameter estimates
• Interpretation: ω ≥ 0.70 = acceptable; ≥ 0.80 = good; ≥ 0.90 = excellent
🗺

Complete Annotated R Script — Copy Ready

▼
# ============================================================
# CFA ANALYSIS - Lecturer Practical (medical_devices)
# Items: Q4-Q11 (retained from EFA); n=150
# ============================================================

# 0. LOAD LIBRARIES -------------------------------------------
library(foreign)    # read .sav
library(psych)      # describe(), mardia()
library(lavaan)     # cfa(), standardizedSolution()
library(semTools)   # compRelSEM()
library(semPlot)    # semPaths()

# 1. LOAD DATA ------------------------------------------------
medical = read.spss("medical_devices.sav", to.data.frame = TRUE)
med_cfa = medical[, c("Q4","Q5","Q6","Q7","Q8","Q9","Q10","Q11")]

# 2. PRELIMINARY: DESCRIPTIVE + NORMALITY ---------------------
describe(med_cfa)
# Look for: n (no missing), mean (3-5 scale), SD, skew, kurtosis

mardia(med_cfa)
# Mardia Kurtosis = 8.18, p < 0.001
# Kurtosis > 5 and p < 0.05 -> Use MLR (robust ML)

# 3. STEP 1: MODEL SPECIFICATION (lavaan) ---------------------
model1 = '
  PA1 =~ Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11   # Factor 1: 5 items
  PA2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10              # Factor 2: 3 items
'
# =~ : measured by (factor loading path)
# lavaan auto-estimates factor correlation PA1~~PA2

# 4. STEP 2a: FIT MODEL + GLOBAL FIT INDICES ------------------
fit1 = cfa(model1, data = med_cfa, estimator = "MLR")
summary(fit1, fit.measures = TRUE, standardized = TRUE)
# Acceptable: chi2 p>0.05, CFI>=0.95, TLI>=0.95, RMSEA<=0.08, SRMR<=0.08
# Initial: chi2(19)=27.373 p=0.096, CFI=0.972, TLI=0.958
#          RMSEA=0.054 (CI:0.000-0.091), SRMR=0.079
# RMSEA upper CI = 0.091 > 0.08 (borderline) -> check localized misfit

# 5. STEP 2b: FACTOR LOADINGS + FACTOR CORRELATION -----------
standardizedSolution(fit1)
# Std.all column = standardized factor loadings (lambda)
# All >= 0.5: Q4=0.814, Q5=0.624, Q6=0.708, Q7=0.735, Q11=0.544 (OK)
#             Q8=0.655, Q9=0.844, Q10=0.856 (OK)
# Factor correlation PA1~~PA2 = 0.124 < 0.85 (discriminant validity OK)

parameterEstimates(fit1, standardized = TRUE)
# Includes SE and 95% CI for each parameter

# 6. STEP 2c: LOCALIZED MISFIT --------------------------------
modindices(fit1, sort. = TRUE, maximum.number = 10)
# MI > 3.84 = flagged (chi-sq critical, df=1, p<0.05)
# Q9~~Q10 = 10.264 (flag), PA1=~Q8 = 10.264 (ignore, cross-loading)
# PA2=~Q5 = 8.359 (ignore, cross-loading), Q5~~Q11 = 6.301 (ignore)

residuals(fit1, type = "standardized")
# |SR| > 2.58 = flagged pair
# Q5-Q8 = 3.455 (flagged!)
# Q5 appears in both SR flag and MI cross-loading -> Q5 is problematic

# 7. STEP 3a: REVISION 1 - Add Q9~~Q10 -----------------------
model1_r1 = '
  PA1 =~ Q4 + Q5 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  PA2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
  Q9 ~~ Q10
'
fit1_r1 = cfa(model1_r1, data = med_cfa, estimator = "MLR")
summary(fit1_r1, fit.measures = TRUE, standardized = TRUE)
# HEYWOOD CASE: Q8 loading = 1.522, negative variance for Q8
# -> REJECT Revision 1 (inadmissible solution)

# 8. STEP 3b: REVISION 2 - Remove Q5 -------------------------
model2 = '
  PA1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  PA2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
'
fit2 = cfa(model2, data = med_cfa, estimator = "MLR")
summary(fit2, fit.measures = TRUE, standardized = TRUE)
# Final: chi2(13)=14.467 p=0.342, CFI=0.994, TLI=0.990
#        RMSEA=0.027 (CI:0.000-0.079), SRMR=0.063 -> ALL GOOD!

standardizedSolution(fit2)
# PA1: Q4=0.806, Q6=0.718, Q7=0.762, Q11=0.509 (all >= 0.5)
# PA2: Q8=0.653, Q9=0.844, Q10=0.858 (all >= 0.5)
# Factor correlation: 0.078 < 0.85 (OK)

residuals(fit2, type = "standardized")
# No |SR| > 2.58 -> no localized misfit remaining

modindices(fit2, sort. = TRUE, maximum.number = 10)
# No MI > 3.84 -> model fits well

# 9. MODEL COMPARISON -----------------------------------------
anova(fit1, fit2)           # Satorra-Bentler chi-sq difference test
fitMeasures(fit1, c("aic","bic"))  # AIC=3166, BIC=3217
fitMeasures(fit2, c("aic","bic"))  # AIC=2791, BIC=2836
# Lower AIC & BIC -> model2 is preferred

# 10. COMPOSITE RELIABILITY OMEGA -----------------------------
compRelSEM(fit2)
# PA1: omega = 0.809 (Good, >= 0.80)
# PA2: omega = 0.836 (Good, >= 0.80)
# Cut-off: omega >= 0.70 acceptable, >= 0.80 good, >= 0.90 excellent

# 11. PATH DIAGRAM -------------------------------------------
semPaths(fit2,
         what = "std",           # standardized estimates
         layout = "tree",        # tree layout
         rotation = 2,           # left to right
         edge.label.cex = 0.8,
         sizeMan = 6,
         sizeLat = 8,
         nCharNodes = 0,
         color = list(lat = "#00897b", man = "#e0f4f1"),
         style = "ram")
📝

Results Presentation — 7 Required Elements

▼

According to Dr Wan's practical, a complete CFA results report must include all 7 elements:

#ElementExample from Study
1 Normality test + estimator chosen Mardia kurtosis=8.18 (p<0.001) → MLR estimator used
2 Initial model specification PA1: Q4,Q5,Q6,Q7,Q11; PA2: Q8,Q9,Q10
3 Global fit indices (initial model) χ²(19)=27.37, p=0.096; CFI=0.972; RMSEA=0.054 (0.000–0.091); SRMR=0.079
4 Localized misfit (MI & SR) MI: Q9~~Q10=10.26; SR: Q5–Q8=3.455 → Q5 problematic
5 Model revision process & rationale Rev 1 (Q9~~Q10) → Heywood rejected. Rev 2 (remove Q5) → accepted
6 Final model fit + factor loadings χ²(13)=14.47, p=0.342; CFI=0.994; RMSEA=0.027; SRMR=0.063; all FL≥0.5
7 Composite reliability omega PA1: ω=0.809; PA2: ω=0.836 — both ≥ 0.70 ✓
Example Academic Paragraph:

A confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) was conducted using the maximum likelihood with robust standard errors (MLR) estimator, as Mardia's test indicated multivariate non-normality (kurtosis = 8.18, p < 0.001). The initial two-factor model comprising eight items (PA1: Q4, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q11; PA2: Q8, Q9, Q10) showed borderline acceptable fit: χ²(19) = 27.37, p = 0.096; CFI = 0.972; TLI = 0.958; RMSEA = 0.054 (90% CI: 0.000–0.091); SRMR = 0.079. Examination of localized misfit revealed a modification index of 10.26 for Q9~~Q10 and a standardized residual of 3.46 for the Q5–Q8 pair. Model revision by adding a residual correlation between Q9 and Q10 produced a Heywood case (Q8 standardized loading = 1.52) and was rejected. Removal of Q5 resulted in a well-fitting final model: χ²(13) = 14.47, p = 0.342; CFI = 0.994; TLI = 0.990; RMSEA = 0.027 (90% CI: 0.000–0.079); SRMR = 0.063. All standardized factor loadings ranged from 0.509 to 0.858, and the factor correlation was 0.078 (< 0.85), confirming discriminant validity. McDonald's omega indicated good composite reliability for both factors (PA1: ω = 0.809; PA2: ω = 0.836).
🗺

CFA Decision Flowchart & Quick Reference

▼
StageWhat to CheckCut-offIf Failed
PrelimMardia kurtosis< 5: use ML; ≥ 5: use MLRSwitch to MLR estimator
Step 1lavaan model syntax=~, ~~; each factor ≥ 3 itemsRevise specification
Step 2aGlobal fit: χ², CFI, TLI, RMSEA, SRMRAll 5 meet cut-offsProceed to localized misfit
Step 2bFactor loadings Std.allAll ≥ 0.5Consider removing weak item
Step 2cFactor correlation< 0.85Check discriminant validity
Step 2dMI & SRMI < 3.84; |SR| < 2.58Consider content-justified revision
Step 3Revised model: Heywood case?No loading >1, no neg varianceReject revision, try alternative
ComparisonAIC / BICLower = betterChoose model with lower values
ReliabilityMcDonald's omega (ω)≥ 0.70Consider revising factor structure
💡 Key Principles for CFA:
• Never revise model based on statistics alone — must have theoretical/content justification
• Heywood case = inadmissible solution — always reject without exception
• Maximum 2–3 revisions — excessive modification = overfitting (capitalizing on chance)
• CFA is confirmatory — do not add items not present in original EFA solution
• Always report both initial and final model for transparency and reproducibility
• Cross-loading MIs are generally not added unless strongly content-justified
📝

How to Interpret CFA Findings (lavaan / R)

▼

Step 1 — Global Model Fit Indices

Always check global fit FIRST. If the model does not fit, do not interpret individual parameter estimates — modify the model first.
IndexAcceptableGood FitPoor Fit
χ² p-value> 0.05> 0.05p < 0.05 (sensitive to n)
CFI≥ 0.95≥ 0.97< 0.90
TLI≥ 0.95≥ 0.97< 0.90
RMSEA≤ 0.08 (CI upper < 0.10)≤ 0.05> 0.10
SRMR≤ 0.08≤ 0.05> 0.10

Reference: Hu & Bentler (1999); Browne & Cudeck (1993); Marsh et al. (2004)

Step 2 — Factor Loadings (λ)

Standardised Loading (λ) — Std.allInterpretationAction
≥ 0.70Excellent convergent validity — item strongly reflects the factor✅ Retain
0.50 – 0.69Good — acceptable indicator✅ Retain
0.30 – 0.49Borderline / Weak indicator⚠️ Consider removing if model fit is poor
< 0.30Poor — does not load sufficiently on the factor❌ Remove

All loadings should also be statistically significant (p < 0.05) — check z-value or p-value in output.

Step 3 — Convergent Validity

CriterionHow to CalculateThreshold
Average Variance Extracted (AVE)AVE = Σλ² / n (mean of squared standardised loadings per factor)≥ 0.50 — indicates convergent validity (Fornell & Larcker, 1981)
All loadings significantp < 0.05 for each λRequired — non-significant loadings indicate the item doesn't measure the factor
Composite Reliability (CR / ω)omega() or compRelSEM() in R≥ 0.70 (Hair et al., 2010)
AVE = mean of (Std.all)² for each item in the factor. Example: if 3 items have λ = 0.70, 0.80, 0.75 → AVE = (0.49 + 0.64 + 0.56)/3 = 0.563 ≥ 0.50 ✅

Step 4 — Discriminant Validity

TestRuleMeaning
Factor correlationr < 0.85 between any two factorsFactors are sufficiently distinct (Brown, 2015)
Fornell-Larcker criterion√AVE of each factor > correlation with any other factorEach factor shares more variance with its own items than with other factors

Step 5 — Modification Indices (MI)

Modification indices suggest which parameters, if freed, would most improve model fit (lower χ²).

  → MI > 10 (or > 3.84 at α=0.05): Consider freeing this parameter
  → Common modification: Allow residuals of similar items to correlate (e.g., items with similar wording)
  → Caution: Each modification must be theoretically justifiable — never modify purely for statistical reasons
  → Modify one parameter at a time, then re-run the model
  → Report all modifications made and the theoretical rationale

CFA Interpretation Decision Tree

Step 1: Check global fit → CFI ≥ 0.95, RMSEA ≤ 0.08, SRMR ≤ 0.08
  → If good fit: proceed to Step 2
  → If poor fit: inspect modification indices → make 1 justified change → re-run

Step 2: Check all factor loadings λ ≥ 0.50 and p < 0.05
  → Remove items with λ < 0.30 or p ≥ 0.05 → re-run

Step 3: Calculate AVE per factor (≥ 0.50) and CR/ω (≥ 0.70)

Step 4: Check factor correlations < 0.85 for discriminant validity

Step 5: Report final model with all fit indices, loadings, AVE, CR

Example Write-Up — CFA

"Confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) was conducted using the lavaan package in R to evaluate the factor structure identified during EFA. Maximum likelihood estimation was used. The initial CFA model with [n] factors and [n] items demonstrated [acceptable / poor] fit: χ²([df]) = [value], p = [value]; CFI = [value]; TLI = [value]; RMSEA = [value] (90% CI: [LL]–[UL]); SRMR = [value]. [If modifications were made: Based on modification indices, residual covariances between [items] were allowed as they share similar wording/content, yielding improved fit: χ²([df]) = [value]; CFI = [value]; RMSEA = [value].] All standardised factor loadings were statistically significant (p < 0.05) and ranged from [min] to [max], all exceeding 0.50, confirming convergent validity. Average variance extracted (AVE) ranged from [min] to [max] for each factor, all meeting the ≥ 0.50 criterion (Fornell & Larcker, 1981). Composite reliability (ω) ranged from [min] to [max], exceeding the recommended threshold of 0.70. Factor correlations were all below 0.85, confirming discriminant validity."

What to Report in a Thesis/Paper

  • Global fit: χ²(df), p, CFI, TLI, RMSEA (90% CI), SRMR
  • Factor loadings: Standardised λ, SE, z, p for each item
  • Convergent validity: AVE per factor (≥ 0.50)
  • Composite reliability: CR or ω per factor (≥ 0.70)
  • Discriminant validity: Factor correlations < 0.85; Fornell-Larcker criterion
  • Modifications: List all changes made + theoretical justification
  • Conclusion: State whether the hypothesised factor structure was confirmed

Note: SEM uses the same dataset as EFA/CFA (n=150, Q1–Q12, 5-point Likert). The SEM practical builds on the CFA final model (PA1: Q4,Q6,Q7,Q11; PA2: Q8,Q9,Q10 → renamed F1, F2 in SEM).
🏗

What is SEM?

▼

Structural Equation Modeling (SEM) is a multivariate statistical modeling technique that aims to explain the structure of relationships among multiple variables (Hair, Black, Babin & Anderson, 2010).

Key Requirement: SEM needs strong theoretical specification of the model ahead of the analysis — to verify (confirm) our theory on the relationships. It is a confirmatory, not exploratory, technique.

SEM consists of TWO components (Bartholomew et al., 2008):

ComponentAlso CalledWhat It Does
Measurement ModelCFA partDeals with latent variables (factors) and the relationships between observed items and the factors. Defines how constructs are measured.
Structural ModelPath analysis partDeals with how latent variables relate to each other. Specifies directional (causal) or non-directional (correlational) paths between constructs.

Similar to CFA — SEM examines how well the variance-covariance matrix produced from the model fits the variance-covariance matrix of the observed data → Goodness of Fit.

📝

Key Terms in SEM

▼
TermDescriptionSymbol
Exogenous variableIndependent / predictor variable. Can be observed (manifest) or unobserved (latent).X or ξ (xi)
Endogenous variableDependent / outcome variable. Can be observed (manifest) or unobserved (latent).Y or η (eta)
Latent variableUnobserved construct measured indirectly by observed indicators.Circle / Ellipse
Observed variableDirectly measured item or indicator (e.g., Q4, Q6).Rectangle / Square
Path diagramVisual representation of the SEM model showing all variables and their relationships.—
Path coefficientStandardised regression weight between variables in SEM.β (beta)
Error / ResidualUnexplained variance in each observed or endogenous variable.ε or δ (epsilon / delta)
📊

Path Diagram Conventions

▼
ConceptDiagram Symbollavaan SyntaxMeaning
Latent variable◯ Circle / EllipseF1 =~ Q4 + Q6Factor F1 is measured by Q4, Q6
Observed variable□ RectangleVariable name onlyDirectly measured item
Correlation / Covariance⇔ Double-headed arrowF1 ~~ F2Bidirectional relationship (no direction implied)
Causal / Regression→ Single-headed arrowY ~ XX predicts Y (X → Y)
Residual / Error variance→ arrow to observedAuto-estimatedUnexplained variance in indicator
ASCII Path Diagram Convention:
Correlation (no direction):
   [F1] <---> [F2]
   lavaan: F1 ~~ F2

Causal (directional):
   [F1] ------> [F2]
   lavaan: F2 ~ F1

Measurement (factor to item):
   [F1] ------> [Q4]
   lavaan: F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
⚖

SEM vs Conventional Regression

▼
AspectConventional RegressionSEM
VariablesOnly observed variablesObserved AND latent variables
Measurement errorIgnored (assumes no error)Explicitly modelled for each indicator
Multiple outcomesOne DV at a timeMultiple DVs simultaneously
MediationRequires multiple regression stepsDirect, indirect, total effects in one model
Model fitR², F-test onlyGlobal fit indices (CFI, TLI, RMSEA, SRMR)
Sample requirement10–20 obs per predictorMinimum 100–200, ideally ≥200
TheoryLess theory-drivenRequires strong a priori theory
📈

Types of Relationships in SEM

▼
RelationshipDescriptionlavaan OperatorExample
CorrelationalBidirectional, no direction implied. Between observed or latent variables.~~Q4 ~~ Q11 or F1 ~~ F2
CausalDirectional (unidirectional). X causes Y. Between observed or latent.~Q4 ~ Q11 or F2 ~ F1
MediationIndirect effect of X on Y through mediator Z. Examines path X→Z→Y.~ (two paths)Z ~ X, Y ~ Z + X
ModerationA third variable M changes the X→Y relationship. Interaction effect.~ (with product)Y ~ X + M + X.M
SEM 8-Step Process: SEM textbooks (Diamantopoulos & Siguaw, 2000; Bollen & Long, 1993; Kline, 2011) describe up to 8 steps: (1) Model Conceptualisation, (2) Path Diagram, (3) Model Specification, (4) Parameter Identification, (5) Sample Size & Data Collection, (6) Parameter Estimation, (7) Model Fit, (8) Model Respecification.
🔄

SEM 8-Step Process — Detailed Guide

▼

SEM textbooks (Diamantopoulos & Siguaw, 2000; Bollen & Long, 1993; Hoyle, 1995; Kline, 2011; Wang & Wang, 2011; Raykov & Marcoulides, 2000; Schumacker & Lomax, 2004) describe up to eight steps in the modern application of SEM. These steps ensure the analysis is theory-driven, statistically sound, and reproducible.

Step 1 — Model Conceptualisation
  • Define the theoretical framework for the model before looking at the data.
  • Identify all latent constructs (factors) and their observed indicators (items).
  • Specify the direction and nature of relationships between constructs (correlational, causal, mediating, moderating).
  • Clearly distinguish exogenous variables (independent/predictor — no incoming paths from other latent variables) from endogenous variables (dependent/outcome — have at least one incoming path).
  • Justify every path based on theory or prior empirical evidence — SEM is confirmatory, not exploratory.
  • Common frameworks used: literature review, conceptual/theoretical framework (e.g., Theory of Planned Behaviour, Health Belief Model).
Step 2 — Path Diagram Construction
  • Draw a visual representation of the model using standard SEM notation.
  • Circles/Ellipses = latent (unobserved) variables; Rectangles/Squares = observed (manifest) variables.
  • Single-headed arrows (→) = causal/directional paths; Double-headed arrows (⇔) = correlations/covariances.
  • Small circles with arrows pointing to endogenous variables = error terms (residuals).
  • Each indicator/observed variable gets an error term. Each endogenous latent variable gets a disturbance (residual) term.
  • The path diagram helps detect identification problems before running the model and communicates the model clearly to reviewers.
Example path diagram (SEM with 2 factors):

  [Q4]◤           ◦[Q8]
  [Q6]◤▶(F1)↔(F2)▶[Q9]
  [Q7]◤           ◦[Q10]
  [Q11]◤

  F1 =~ Q4+Q6+Q7+Q11 (measurement model)
  F2 =~ Q8+Q9+Q10    (measurement model)
  F2 ~ F1            (structural path)
Step 3 — Model Specification (lavaan Syntax)
  • Translate the path diagram into mathematical model equations using lavaan syntax.
  • Specify the measurement model (=~) for each latent variable and its indicators.
  • Specify the structural model (~ for regression, ~~ for covariance) for paths between constructs.
  • Choose the estimator: ML (default, multivariate normality assumed), MLR (robust ML, Yuan-Bentler, for non-normal data — use when Mardia kurtosis p < 0.05), WLSMV (for ordinal/categorical indicators).
  • Decide on scaling method: lavaan defaults to fixing the first indicator loading to 1.0 (marker variable method).
# lavaan operator reference
# =~   "is measured by"  → measurement model (factor → items)
# ~    "is regressed on" → structural path (Y ~ X)
# ~~   "is correlated with" → covariance/correlation
# :=   "is defined as"  → computed parameter (e.g., indirect effect)

model = "
  F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11   # measurement
  F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
  F2 ~ F1                     # structural path
"
fit = sem(model, data = data, estimator = 'ML')
Step 4 — Model Identification
  • A model is identified if it is theoretically possible to obtain a unique solution for each parameter.
  • Identification is determined by the relationship between known information (observed variances and covariances) and unknown parameters (to be estimated).
TypeConditiondfImplication
Under-identifiedParameters > knownsdf < 0✘ Cannot be estimated — need to constrain parameters or add indicators
Just-identifiedParameters = knownsdf = 0⚠ Saturated model — perfect fit by definition, no model test possible
Over-identifiedParameters < knownsdf > 0✓ Testable — degrees of freedom allow chi-square test of fit
Rule of thumb: Each latent variable needs at least 3 indicators for identification (2 is possible with constraints; 1 is not identifiable without fixing parameters). Aim for over-identified models with df > 0.
Step 5 — Sample Size & Data Collection
  • SEM requires larger samples than conventional regression due to the number of parameters estimated.
  • Rules of thumb:
    • Minimum n = 100–200 (Hair et al., 2010)
    • 10–20 observations per free parameter
    • 5–10 observations per indicator
    • Kyriazos (2018): n ≥200 for stable CFA/SEM estimates
  • Monte Carlo simulation (via Mplus or simsem in R): increasingly used to estimate required power and sample size for a specific SEM model. Requires hypothesised population values for each parameter from theory or prior empirical findings.
  • Data requirements: continuous indicators (Likert with ≥5 categories often treated as continuous), check normality (Mardia's kurtosis), no severe outliers, missing data handled (FIML preferred over listwise deletion in lavaan).
  • Missing data: use missing = 'fiml' in sem() for Full Information Maximum Likelihood estimation rather than listwise deletion.
Step 6 — Parameter Estimation
  • lavaan uses iterative optimisation to find parameter values that minimise the discrepancy between the model-implied covariance matrix and the observed covariance matrix.
  • Default estimator: ML (Maximum Likelihood) — assumes multivariate normality. Produces ML chi-square test statistic.
  • MLR (Robust ML, Yuan-Bentler) — use when normality assumption violated (Mardia kurtosis p < 0.05). Produces Satorra-Bentler scaled chi-square. Specify: estimator = 'MLR'.
  • WLSMV — for ordinal/binary indicators using polychoric correlations. Specify: estimator = 'WLSMV'.
  • Convergence: lavaan reports "ended normally after X iterations." If it does not converge, the model may be misspecified or under-identified.
  • Parameters estimated per factor: loadings (λ), residual variances (θ), factor variances (Φ), factor covariances/correlations.
EstimatorWhen to Uselavaan Code
MLContinuous, multivariate normal dataestimator = 'ML' (default)
MLRNon-normal continuous data (Mardia kurtosis p < 0.05)estimator = 'MLR'
WLSMVOrdinal or binary indicatorsestimator = 'WLSMV'
GLSGeneralised Least Squares (less common)estimator = 'GLS'
Step 7 — Model Fit Evaluation
  • Evaluate global fit using multiple fit indices (no single index is sufficient alone):
IndexGood FitAcceptable
χ² p-value>0.05>0.05
CFI≥0.95≥0.90
TLI≥0.95≥0.90
RMSEA (+ 90% CI)≤0.05 (CI <0.08)≤0.08 (CI <0.10)
SRMR≤0.05≤0.08
  • If global fit is inadequate, inspect localised misfit:
    • Modification Indices (MI): MI > 3.84 suggests adding a path may improve fit (χ² reduction ≈ MI value). Prioritise theoretically justified MIs only.
    • Standardised Residuals (SR): |SR| > 2.58 indicates a pair of variables is poorly fitted by the model.
    • Parameter estimates: check all loadings are significant (p < 0.05) and λ > 0.50; check for Heywood cases (loading > 1 or negative variance — always reject model if present).
summary(fit, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
modindices(fit, sort = T, maximum.number = 10)  # top MIs
residuals(fit, type = "standardized")           # SRs
Step 8 — Model Respecification (if needed)
  • If fit is inadequate, respecify the model guided by MIs, SRs, and theory — NOT purely statistically driven.
  • Permissible modifications:
    • Add correlated error terms (Q9 ~~ Q10) — only if theoretically justified (e.g., same wording, same method)
    • Remove poorly-fitting indicators (low loading or high SR) — requires theoretical justification
    • Add or remove structural paths based on theory
    • Move an indicator to a different factor if content warrants it
  • Not permissible: Making modifications purely to improve fit without theoretical basis (capitalisation on chance — leads to overfitting).
  • Model comparison:
    • For nested models: use chi-square difference test (Satorra-Bentler scaled χ² difference for MLR estimator)
    • For non-nested models: compare AIC/BIC (lower = better)
  • Cross-validation: Ideally validate the respecified model on a new/holdout sample to confirm it is not sample-specific.
  • Report all models: Report both the initial and final model with justification for each change.
# Model comparison (nested models with ML estimator)
anova(fit.model1, fit.model2)

# AIC / BIC comparison (any models)
AIC(fit.model1, fit.model2)
BIC(fit.model1, fit.model2)

# For MLR estimator — Satorra-Bentler scaled chi-sq difference
lavTestLRT(fit.model1, fit.model2, method = "satorra.bentler.2010")
Summary Table: 8 Steps at a Glance
StepActionKey Decision / Output
1Model ConceptualisationTheory-driven; identify constructs, indicators, relationships
2Path DiagramVisual model; circles, rectangles, arrows, error terms
3Model Specificationlavaan syntax; choose estimator (ML/MLR/WLSMV)
4Identificationdf > 0 (over-identified); ≥3 indicators per factor
5Sample Size & Datan ≥ 200; check normality; handle missing data
6Parameter EstimationRun model; check convergence; inspect loadings
7Model Fit EvaluationGlobal (CFI/TLI/RMSEA/SRMR) + localised (MI/SR)
8Model RespecificationTheory-guided modifications; compare models (AIC/BIC/χ² diff)
🔧

Step 1 — Correlational: Observed Variables (Q4 ~~ Q11)

▼

A correlational model between two observed variables estimates the covariance/correlation. Using ~~ (double tilde) with meanstructure=T to display mean.

# Model syntax: ~~ = covariance/correlation
model.c = "
Q4 ~~ Q11
"
corr.c = sem(model.c, data = data, meanstructure = T)
summary(corr.c, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)

Output — Covariances:

ParameterEstimateStd.Errz-valueP(>|z|)Std.lvStd.all (r)
Q4 ~~ Q110.4790.0974.934<0.0010.4790.440

Verify with cor(data$Q4, data$Q11) → r = 0.440 ✓ (matches Std.all)

# Path diagram for correlational model
semPaths(corr.c, what = "path", whatLabels = "par", edge.color = "black",
         edge.label.cex = 1, residuals = F, sizeInt = 4)
Interpretation: Q4 and Q11 have a statistically significant positive correlation (r = 0.440, p < 0.001). The ~~ operator in lavaan represents a bidirectional covariance/correlation path in the path diagram.
📈

Step 2 — Correlational: Latent Variables (F1 ~~ F2)

▼

A correlational model between two latent variables is essentially a CFA model. lavaan automatically estimates the covariance between factors. The ~~ is implicit in CFA — no need to specify explicitly unless constraining to zero.

# Model: CFA with two correlated factors
model.cl = "
F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11   # F1: Affective subscale
F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10         # F2: Behavioural subscale
"
corr.cl = sem(model.cl, data = data)
summary(corr.cl, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
