How to Interpret Correlational SEM Findings

▼

Step-by-Step Interpretation Framework

#What to Look AtWhere to Find ItWhat It Means
1Estimate (unstandardised covariance)Covariances sectionRaw covariance between the two variables — unit-dependent, not directly comparable across studies
2Std.all (standardised = r)Covariances sectionPearson correlation coefficient for observed variables; factor correlation for latent variables. This is the main effect size.
3p-value (P(>|z|))Covariances sectionStatistical significance — is the correlation significantly different from zero?
4Global fit indices (for latent models)Model Test / Fit Indices sectionDoes the overall model fit the data well? (CFI ≥ 0.95, RMSEA ≤ 0.08, SRMR ≤ 0.08, p > 0.05)

Effect Size for Correlation (Cohen, 1988)

|r| valueInterpretationPractical Significance
< 0.10Negligible / trivialNo meaningful association
0.10 – 0.29SmallWeak association, but may be meaningful in context
0.30 – 0.49MediumModerate association — practically meaningful
≥ 0.50LargeStrong association
≥ 0.85⚠️ Discriminant validity concernVariables may be measuring the same construct (too similar)

Interpretation Decision Tree

Step 1 — Is the correlation significant?
   → p < 0.05: YES — proceed to interpret direction and magnitude
   → p ≥ 0.05: NOT significant — the two variables are not significantly associated (report as: "no significant correlation was found")

Step 2 — What is the direction?
   → Std.all > 0: Positive correlation (when X increases, Y increases)
   → Std.all < 0: Negative correlation (when X increases, Y decreases)

Step 3 — How strong is it?
   → Apply Cohen's (1988) criteria above

Step 4 (latent variables) — Does the model fit?
   → Check CFI ≥ 0.95, TLI ≥ 0.95, RMSEA ≤ 0.08, SRMR ≤ 0.08, p > 0.05
   → Also confirm factor loadings λ ≥ 0.50 for all indicators

Example Write-Up Paragraph

Observed variables (Q4 ~~ Q11):
The correlational SEM model revealed a statistically significant positive correlation between Q4 and Q11 (r = 0.440, z = 4.934, p < 0.001). The effect size was medium (Cohen, 1988), indicating a meaningful but not excessive association between the two items.

Latent variables (F1 ~~ F2):
The two-factor CFA model demonstrated acceptable overall fit (χ²(13) = 20.451, p = 0.085; CFI = 0.979; TLI = 0.966; RMSEA = 0.062 [90% CI: 0.000–0.111]; SRMR = 0.063). All factor loadings exceeded 0.50 (λ range = 0.509–0.858), confirming convergent validity. The correlation between the Affective subscale (F1) and the Behavioural subscale (F2) was not statistically significant (r = 0.078, p = 0.424), indicating that the two factors are largely independent. As r < 0.85, discriminant validity was confirmed (Fornell & Larcker, 1981).

What to Report in a Thesis/Paper

  • Model fit indices (for latent models): χ², df, p, CFI, TLI, RMSEA (+ 90% CI), SRMR
  • Factor loadings (λ) for all indicators — confirm λ ≥ 0.50
  • Correlation estimate (r = Std.all), z-value, p-value
  • Effect size label (Cohen, 1988)
  • Discriminant validity statement (r < 0.85) if comparing latent factors
📊

Causal Relationships in SEM

▼

A causal (regression) relationship assigns a directional path from an independent (exogenous) variable to a dependent (endogenous) variable. In lavaan, use the ~ operator (single tilde).

Relationship Typelavaan SyntaxInterpretation
Observed → ObservedQ4 ~ Q11Q11 (IV) predicts Q4 (DV) — simple regression
Latent → LatentF2 ~ F1F1 (exogenous) causes F2 (endogenous) — SEM path
Observed → LatentF2 ~ Q1Observed predictor on latent outcome
Latent → ObservedQ4 ~ F1Latent predictor on observed outcome
Key principle: The direction of the arrow in SEM is based on theory, not statistical analysis. Always specify the causal direction based on your research hypothesis before running the model.
➡

Step 3 — Causal: Observed Variables (Q4 ~ Q11)

▼

Directional path from Q11 to Q4. Compare with simple linear regression (SLR) to understand how SEM and SLR relate for observed variables.

# Causal model: Q11 predicts Q4
model.cs = "
Q4 ~ Q11
"
cause.cs = sem(model.cs, data = data)
summary(cause.cs, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)

# Compare with simple linear regression
summary(lm(formula = Q4 ~ Q11, data = data))

SEM Regression Output:

ParameterEstimate (b)Std.Errz-valueP(>|z|)Std.all (β)
Q4 ~ Q110.5520.0926.004<0.0010.440

SLR Output (for comparison):

ParameterEstimateStd. Errort valuePr(>|t|)
(Intercept)0.9630.3222.9890.003
Q110.5520.0935.964<0.001
Key Insight: For observed variable regression, SEM and SLR give virtually identical estimates (b = 0.552 in both). The standardised coefficient (β = 0.440) = Pearson correlation r for simple regression. SEM adds path diagram visualisation and fit index evaluation. 
semPaths(cause.cs, what = "path", whatLabels = "par", edge.color = "black",
         rotation = 2, edge.label.cex = 1, residuals = F)
🏗

Step 4 — Causal: Latent Variables (F2 ~ F1) — Full SEM

▼

This is the full SEM model: combines the measurement model (CFA) with a structural path (F1 → F2). F1 is the exogenous (independent) latent factor; F2 is the endogenous (dependent) latent factor.

# Full SEM: measurement + structural path
model.csl = "
  # Measurement model (CFA)
  F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
  # Structural model: F1 predicts F2
  F2 ~ F1
"
cause.csl = sem(model.csl, data = data)
summary(cause.csl, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)

Global Fit Indices:

IndexValueThresholdStatus
χ²(df)20.451 (13)——
p-value0.085>0.05✓ PASS
CFI0.979≥0.95✓ PASS
TLI0.966≥0.95✓ PASS
RMSEA0.062 (CI: 0.000–0.111)≤0.08⚠ Marginal
SRMR0.063≤0.08✓ PASS
AIC2791.020Lower = better—
BIC2836.179Lower = better—

Structural Path (Regressions):

PathEstimate (b)Std.Errz-valueP(>|z|)Std.all (β)
F2 ~ F10.0540.0670.8030.4220.078

Factor Loadings (same as CFA model):

FactorItemStd.all (λ)
F1Q40.806
Q60.718
Q70.762
Q110.509
F2Q80.653
Q90.844
Q100.858
Interpretation: The structural path F1 → F2 is not statistically significant (β = 0.078, p = 0.422). The Affective subscale (F1) does NOT significantly predict the Behavioural subscale (F2). This means the causal SEM model and the correlational CFA model give identical fit — changing from ~~ to ~ does not affect fit when variables have only a weak relationship.
Note: The fit indices for the causal latent model (F2 ~ F1) are identical to the correlational latent model (F1 ~~ F2). This is because with 2 factors, a causal path and a correlation are equivalent in terms of degrees of freedom — they use the same number of parameters. The path coefficient (β = 0.078) equals the correlation (r = 0.078). 
# Path diagram for full SEM
semPaths(cause.csl, what = "path", whatLabels = "par", edge.color = "black",
         layout = "tree2", edge.label.cex = 1, residuals = F)
📌

Correlational vs Causal: Key Differences

▼
AspectCorrelational (~~)Causal (~)
DirectionBidirectional (no direction)Unidirectional (X → Y)
Theoretical basisAssociation onlyCausal theory required
lavaan syntaxF1 ~~ F2F2 ~ F1
Path diagramDouble-headed arrow ⇔Single-headed arrow →
Parameter estimatedCovariance / correlationRegression coefficient
Std.all interpretationPearson rStandardised β (path coefficient)
For observed variablesSame as Pearson rSame as SLR β
For latent variablesFactor correlationStructural path coefficient
📝

How to Interpret Causal SEM Findings

▼

Step-by-Step Interpretation Framework

#What to Look AtWhere to Find ItWhat It Means
1Global model fitFit Indices sectionFirst confirm the model fits data well before interpreting paths. A poor-fitting model — any path coefficients are meaningless.
2Estimate (b) — unstandardised path coefficientRegressions sectionFor every 1-unit increase in X, Y changes by b units (same scale as variables)
3Std.all (β) — standardised path coefficientRegressions sectionEffect size: for every 1 SD increase in X, Y changes by β SD. Comparable across different studies and variables.
4p-value / z-valueRegressions sectionIs the path statistically significant? (p < 0.05 = significant)
5R² (R-square) — for endogenous variablesR-squared sectionProportion of variance in Y explained by the predictor(s) in the model

Effect Size for Standardised Path Coefficient β (Cohen, 1988)

|β| valueInterpretationPractical Significance
< 0.10NegligibleNo meaningful predictive effect
0.10 – 0.29SmallWeak but potentially meaningful
0.30 – 0.49MediumModerate practical significance
≥ 0.50LargeStrong predictive relationship

Model Fit Criteria (Standard SEM Thresholds)

IndexAcceptable ThresholdGood Fit
χ² p-value> 0.05Non-significant (model-data consistent)
CFI≥ 0.95≥ 0.97
TLI≥ 0.95≥ 0.97
RMSEA≤ 0.08 (CI upper < 0.10)≤ 0.05
SRMR≤ 0.08≤ 0.05

Interpretation Decision Tree

Step 1 — Check global model fit
   → If GOOD fit: proceed to interpret structural paths
   → If POOR fit: modify model (inspect modification indices) before interpreting paths

Step 2 — Is the structural path significant?
   → p < 0.05: Significant — X significantly predicts Y
   → p ≥ 0.05: Not significant — X does not significantly predict Y (reject hypothesis)

Step 3 — What is the direction and magnitude?
   → β > 0: Positive path — X and Y move in the same direction
   → β < 0: Negative path — X and Y move in opposite directions
   → Apply Cohen's criteria for magnitude

Step 4 — How much variance is explained?
   → Report R² for endogenous variable Y
   → e.g., R² = 0.35: "35% of the variance in Y is explained by X"

Example Write-Up Paragraph

Observed variable path (Q4 ~ Q11):
A simple regression path was specified in the SEM framework with Q11 as the predictor and Q4 as the outcome. The model yielded a statistically significant positive path coefficient (b = 0.552, β = 0.440, z = 6.004, p < 0.001), indicating that for every one-unit increase in Q11, Q4 increased by 0.552 units on average. The standardised coefficient (β = 0.440) indicated a medium effect size. These results are equivalent to simple linear regression (b = 0.552, t = 5.964, p < 0.001).

Latent variable path — Full SEM (F2 ~ F1):
The full SEM model combining the CFA measurement model with the structural path F1 → F2 demonstrated acceptable model fit (χ²(13) = 20.451, p = 0.085; CFI = 0.979; TLI = 0.966; RMSEA = 0.062 [90% CI: 0.000–0.111]; SRMR = 0.063). However, the structural path from the Affective subscale (F1) to the Behavioural subscale (F2) was not statistically significant (β = 0.078, b = 0.054, z = 0.803, p = 0.422). This indicates that F1 did not significantly predict F2, and the research hypothesis that Affective attitude predicts Behavioural attitude was not supported by the data.

What to Report in a Thesis/Paper

  • Model fit: χ²(df), p, CFI, TLI, RMSEA (90% CI), SRMR
  • Factor loadings: λ for all indicators (confirm ≥ 0.50)
  • Structural path: unstandardised b, standardised β, SE, z, p-value
  • R² for each endogenous variable
  • Effect size label (small/medium/large, Cohen, 1988)
  • State whether research hypothesis was supported or rejected
🤖

Mediation — Concept & Theory

▼

A mediator Z is a third variable that intervenes between two related variables X and Y (Hair et al., 2010). Z has two roles: it is a DV in one equation and an IV in another (Awang, 2012). Z mediates the relationship of X to Y.

WITHOUT mediator Z:          X ─────────────────────────────────────> Y
                                          (direct effect: c)

WITH mediator Z:             X ──────────> Z ──────────> Y     (indirect: a × b)
                              └─────────────────────────> Y     (direct: c’)
                                   (total = c’ + a×b)
PathDescriptionlavaan code
aX → Z (IV to mediator)Z ~ a*X
bZ → Y (mediator to DV)Y ~ b*Z + c*X
c’X → Y direct effect (with Z in model)c in above
a×bIndirect effect (mediation)ind := a*b
c’+a×bTotal effecttotal := c + a*b
📏

Types of Mediation (Awang, 2012)

▼
TypeConditionInterpretationX→Y direct (c’)X→Z (a)Z→Y (b)
Complete Mediationc’ becomes non-significant after adding ZZ completely mediates X→Y; X only indirectly causes YNot sig.Sig.Sig.
Partial Mediationc’ reduced but still significant; c’ < c (without Z)Z partially mediates; X has both direct and indirect effects on YSig.Sig.Sig.
No MediationZ does not affect X→Y; either a or b non-sigZ is not a mediator; X directly causes Y onlySig.Either a or b not sig.
Bootstrap for indirect effect: Use bootstrap CI (n=1000–5000) to test indirect effect (a×b). If 95% CI does NOT include zero → significant mediation.
📄

Mediation in lavaan: Code Template

▼
# Mediation model in lavaan
# X = exogenous (IV), Z = mediator, Y = endogenous (DV)

model.med = "
  # Measurement model (if latent variables)
  F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11  # F1 = X (exogenous)
  F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10        # F2 = Y (endogenous)

  # Structural model with labels for indirect effect
  Z  ~ a*F1              # a path: X → mediator Z
  F2 ~ b*Z + c*F1        # b path: Z → Y; c = direct effect X → Y

  # Define indirect and total effects
  ind   := a*b           # indirect effect
  total := c + a*b       # total effect
"

fit.med = sem(model.med, data = data,
              se = 'bootstrap', bootstrap = 1000)
summary(fit.med, fit.measures = T, standardized = T, ci = T)

# Check indirect effect confidence interval
parameterEstimates(fit.med, boot.ci.type = 'bca.simple')[
  parameterEstimates(fit.med)$label %in% c('ind','total'), ]
Report: Report the indirect effect (a×b), its 95% bootstrap CI, and whether the CI excludes zero. Also report the direct effect (c') and total effect. State whether mediation is complete, partial, or none.
🎯

Moderation — Concept & Theory

▼

A moderator M is a third variable that changes the relationship between two related variables X and Y (Hair et al., 2010). M moderates the causal effect that an IV has on a DV (Awang, 2012). Also commonly known as interaction.

WITHOUT moderator:     X ─────────────────────────────────────────> Y

WITH moderator M:      X ────────────────────────────────────────> Y
                       M ────────────────────────────────────────> Y
                      X*M (interaction) ─────────────────────────> Y
TypeConditionInterpretation
Complete ModerationX*M significant; X alone NOT significantM completely changes the X→Y relationship. X only has an effect in the presence of M.
Partial ModerationX*M significant; X alone also significant; a (with X*M) < a (without X*M)M partially changes the X→Y relationship. Both X and X*M contribute.
No ModerationX*M NOT significantM does not change the X→Y relationship.
Note (Dr Wan): Moderation is typically applied to observed variables only. For latent variable moderation: (a) use multi-group CFA for categorical moderators; (b) create interaction latent variable (items of X × items of M) for numerical moderators. This is complex and beyond basic SEM scope.
📄

Moderation in lavaan: Code Template

▼
# Moderation (interaction) — observed variables only
# Create interaction term first
data$X.M = data$X * data$M   # product term

model.mod = "
  Y ~ a*X + b*M + c*X.M   # c = moderation effect (X*M)
"
fit.mod = sem(model.mod, data = data)
summary(fit.mod, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)

# If c (X.M) is significant → moderation exists
# If c NOT significant → no moderation
Interpretation: If X*M is significant: (1) centre X and M before creating product (mean centring reduces multicollinearity); (2) plot interaction using simple slope analysis; (3) report: "M significantly moderated the effect of X on Y (β = ..., p = ...). The effect of X on Y was stronger/weaker at high vs low levels of M."
📌

Mediation vs Moderation: Quick Reference

▼
AspectMediationModeration
QuestionHOW does X affect Y? (through Z)WHEN/WHO does X affect Y? (for whom)
Third variableMediator Z: lies on the causal path X→Z→YModerator M: changes strength/direction of X→Y
MechanismIndirect causal chainInteraction/conditional effect
lavaan approachZ ~ a*X; Y ~ b*Z + c*XY ~ X + M + X*M
Key statisticIndirect effect a×b & its CIInteraction term X*M coefficient
TestBootstrap CI for a×bSignificance of X*M term
Latent variablesCan be used with latent variablesComplex; prefer observed variables
📝

How to Interpret Mediation & Moderation Findings

▼

🔗 MEDIATION — Interpretation Guide

Step 1 — Check Each Path for Significance
PathLabelSignificance Required?What It Means If Significant
X → Z (mediator)a✅ YesThe IV has a significant effect on the mediator
Z → Y (mediator to DV)b✅ YesThe mediator has a significant effect on the DV (controlling for X)
X → Y (direct, with Z)c'Depends on typeDirect effect of IV on DV after accounting for mediator
a × b (indirect)ind✅ Yes (via Bootstrap CI)The mediated (indirect) effect — key evidence for mediation
Step 2 — Determine Mediation Type
Typea pathb pathc' (direct)a×b Bootstrap CIConclusion
Complete MediationSig.Sig.NOT Sig.Does NOT include 0Z fully explains X→Y; X has no direct effect on Y
Partial MediationSig.Sig.Sig. (c' < c)Does NOT include 0Z partially explains X→Y; X retains direct effect
No MediationEither a or b NOT Sig.May be Sig.Includes 0Z does not mediate X→Y
Step 3 — Bootstrap CI for Indirect Effect (a × b)
  → 95% CI does NOT include zero: Indirect effect is significant → mediation is supported
  → 95% CI includes zero: Indirect effect is NOT significant → mediation not supported
     (Use BCa bootstrap with n ≥ 1000 resamples for robust CI)
Example Write-Up — Mediation
Template — fill in your own values:

"A mediation analysis was conducted using structural equation modelling (lavaan) with 1,000 bootstrap resamples to estimate the indirect effect. The path from X to Z (a = [value], β = [value], p = [value]) and from Z to Y (b = [value], β = [value], p = [value]) were both statistically significant. The indirect effect (a × b = [value]) was significant, with a 95% BCa bootstrap confidence interval that did not include zero [LL, UL]. The direct effect of X on Y after controlling for Z was [significant / not significant] (c' = [value], β = [value], p = [value]). These findings indicate [complete / partial] mediation: Z [fully / partially] mediates the relationship between X and Y."

Report: indirect effect (a×b), 95% bootstrap CI, direct effect (c'), total effect, and mediation type.

⚡ MODERATION — Interpretation Guide

Step 1 — Is the Interaction Term Significant?
Interaction (X × M)ConclusionNext Step
p < 0.05 (significant)Moderation exists — M changes the X→Y relationshipProceed to determine type (complete vs partial) and plot simple slopes
p ≥ 0.05 (not significant)No moderation — M does NOT change the X→Y relationshipReport as "no significant moderation effect was found"
Step 2 — Determine Moderation Type
X alone (without X×M)X×M termTypeInterpretation
NOT significantSignificantComplete ModerationX only affects Y in the presence of M; M completely controls the relationship
Significant (β reduced)SignificantPartial ModerationBoth X and X×M contribute; M strengthens or weakens X→Y
AnyNOT SignificantNo ModerationM does not moderate the effect of X on Y
Step 3 — Describe the Nature of the Interaction
After confirming moderation is significant:
  → Centre X and M (subtract mean) to reduce multicollinearity before creating product term
  → Compute simple slopes at M = mean ± 1 SD (low, medium, high levels of M)
  → Plot interaction (X on x-axis, Y on y-axis, separate lines for high/low M)
  → Describe: "The effect of X on Y was stronger [at high / at low] levels of M"
Example Write-Up — Moderation
Template — fill in your own values:

"Moderation analysis was conducted to examine whether M moderated the effect of X on Y. Prior to analysis, X and M were mean-centred to reduce multicollinearity. The main effect of X on Y was [significant / not significant] (β = [value], p = [value]), and the main effect of M on Y was [significant / not significant] (β = [value], p = [value]). The interaction term (X × M) was [significant / not significant] (β = [value], p = [value]), indicating [complete / partial / no] moderation. Simple slope analysis revealed that the effect of X on Y was significant at high levels of M (β = [value], p = [value]) but not at low levels (β = [value], p = [value]), suggesting that M strengthened the relationship between X and Y."

Mediation vs Moderation — Reporting Checklist

Item to ReportMediationModeration
Individual path coefficients (a, b, c')✅ b, SE, β, p for each✅ b, SE, β, p for X, M, X×M
Indirect effect✅ a×b value + 95% Bootstrap CI❌ Not applicable
Total effect✅ c' + a×b❌ Not applicable
Mediation type✅ Complete / Partial / None❌ Not applicable
Moderation type❌ Not applicable✅ Complete / Partial / None
Simple slope analysis❌ Not applicable✅ At high and low levels of M
Interaction plot❌ Not applicable✅ Strongly recommended
Mean centringNot required✅ Required for product term
Bootstrap✅ Required (n ≥ 1000)Optional
Model fit indices✅ χ², CFI, TLI, RMSEA, SRMR✅ χ², CFI, TLI, RMSEA, SRMR
📋

SEM Fit Indices — Complete Guide

▼
IndexFull NameGood FitAcceptableRationale
χ²Chi-square testp > 0.05p > 0.05Tests exact fit. Sensitive to n. Non-significant = good model (not sig. = data fits model)
CFIComparative Fit Index≥0.95≥0.90Compares model to null (baseline) model. Range 0–1. Less sensitive to n than χ².
TLITucker-Lewis Index≥0.95≥0.90Penalises for model complexity. Can exceed 1.0 (acceptable). Similar to CFI.
RMSEARoot Mean Square Error of Approximation≤0.05≤0.08Measures misfit per df. 90% CI should entirely be below 0.10. <0.05 = close fit.
SRMRStandardised Root Mean Square Residual≤0.05≤0.08Average standardised residual. Sensitive to number of items. <0.08 acceptable.
AICAkaike Information CriterionLower = better—Model comparison only (not absolute). Penalises for parameters. Use when comparing models.
BICBayesian Information CriterionLower = better—Penalises more for parameters than AIC. Prefers parsimonious models.
Rule (Dr Wan): A model has acceptable fit if it meets most (not necessarily all) criteria. RMSEA 90% CI upper bound <0.10 is acceptable. Report all indices together, not just those that look good.
📄

Results Presentation — 6 Required Elements

▼

When reporting SEM results in manuscripts (MJMS/USM DrPH standard), include:

#ElementWhat to Report
1Model descriptionState the SEM model type (correlational/causal/mediation), variables, software (R lavaan 0.6.15)
2Global fit indicesχ², df, p; CFI; TLI; RMSEA (90% CI); SRMR
3Factor loadingsStandardised loadings (λ) for each indicator; all should be >0.50
4Structural pathsStandardised path coefficients (β), SE, z, p for each structural path
5Factor correlationsReport r between factors (if correlational model)
6Indirect effectsIf mediation: report a×b, 95% bootstrap CI, whether CI excludes zero
📝

Academic Paragraph Template (Dr Wan's Dataset)

▼

Structural equation modelling (SEM) was conducted using the lavaan package (version 0.6.15) in R to examine the relationships between the Affective subscale (F1) and the Behavioural subscale (F2) of the Attitude towards Statistics scale.

The correlational SEM model, specifying F1 and F2 as correlated factors with their respective indicators (F1: Q4, Q6, Q7, Q11; F2: Q8, Q9, Q10), demonstrated acceptable model fit: χ²(13) = 20.451, p = 0.085; CFI = 0.979; TLI = 0.966; RMSEA = 0.062 (90% CI: 0.000–0.111); SRMR = 0.063. All factor loadings were statistically significant and exceeded the recommended threshold of 0.50, ranging from 0.509 to 0.858 for F1 and 0.653 to 0.858 for F2. The correlation between F1 and F2 was weak and non-significant (r = 0.078, p = 0.424), suggesting that the affective and behavioural components of attitude towards statistics are largely independent constructs.

The causal SEM model, specifying F1 as the exogenous predictor of F2, produced identical model fit indices and a non-significant structural path coefficient (β = 0.078, p = 0.422), confirming that the Affective subscale did not significantly predict the Behavioural subscale of the attitude towards statistics scale.

📍

SEM Decision Flowchart

▼
StageQuestionIf YESIf NO
1. TheoryDo you have strong theoretical justification for the model?Proceed to model specificationDo not use SEM yet — review literature
2. Samplen ≥ 200 (or min 100)?ProceedCaution — may have power issues
3. Model typeAre you testing direction of effect?Use causal model (~)Use correlational model (~~)
4. Global fitCFI≥0.95, TLI≥0.95, RMSEA≤0.08, SRMR≤0.08?Model fits wellInspect MIs, consider respecification
5. LoadingsAll λ > 0.50?Acceptable measurement modelConsider removing item or checking theory
6. Structural pathPath coefficient (β) significant?Relationship supportedRelationship not supported by data
7. MediationIs indirect effect a×b significant (CI excludes 0)?Mediation supportedNo mediation
📄

Complete Annotated R Script — Dr Wan's SEM Practical

▼
########################################################
# SEM in R using lavaan
# Dr. Lecturer — USM, May 2023
# Dataset: Attitude_Statistics v3.sav (n=150)
########################################################

## 1. LIBRARIES ─────────────────────────────────────────
library(foreign)   # read SPSS .sav files
library(psych)     # describe(), mardia()
library(lavaan)    # core SEM: sem(), summary()
library(semTools)  # additional tools
library(semPlot)   # semPaths() — path diagrams

## 2. LOAD DATA ──────────────────────────────────────────
data = read.spss("Attitude_Statistics v3.sav", F, T)
dim(data)     # [1] 150  13
names(data)   # "ID" "Q1"..."Q12"
describe(data[-1])  # descriptive stats for all items

## 3. CORRELATIONAL MODELS ───────────────────────────────
### 3.1 Observed variables: Q4 ~~ Q11
model.c = "
Q4 ~~ Q11
"
corr.c = sem(model.c, data = data, meanstructure = T)
summary(corr.c, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
# Std.all = 0.440 = Pearson r

# Verify with Pearson correlation
cor(data$Q4, data$Q11)  # [1] 0.4401738

# Path diagram
semPaths(corr.c, what = "path", whatLabels = "par",
         edge.color = "black", edge.label.cex = 1,
         residuals = F, sizeInt = 4)

### 3.2 Latent variables: F1 ~~ F2 (CFA model)
model.cl = "
F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11   # Affective subscale
F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10         # Behavioural subscale
"
corr.cl = sem(model.cl, data = data)
summary(corr.cl, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
# Key results:
# chi2(13) = 20.451, p = 0.085
# CFI = 0.979, TLI = 0.966
# RMSEA = 0.062 [0.000, 0.111]
# SRMR = 0.063
# F1~~F2 r = 0.078, p = 0.424 (not sig)

semPaths(corr.cl, what = "path", whatLabels = "par",
         edge.color = "black", layout = "tree2",
         edge.label.cex = 1, residuals = F)

## 4. CAUSAL MODELS ──────────────────────────────────────
### 4.1 Observed variables: Q4 ~ Q11
model.cs = "
Q4 ~ Q11
"
cause.cs = sem(model.cs, data = data)
summary(cause.cs, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
# Std.all = 0.440 = standardised beta

# Compare with simple linear regression
summary(lm(formula = Q4 ~ Q11, data = data))
# SEM and SLR give identical estimates for observed vars

semPaths(cause.cs, what = "path", whatLabels = "par",
         edge.color = "black", rotation = 2,
         edge.label.cex = 1, residuals = F)

### 4.2 Latent variables: F2 ~ F1 (Full SEM)
model.csl = "
  # Measurement model
  F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
  # Structural path: F1 predicts F2
  F2 ~ F1
"
cause.csl = sem(model.csl, data = data)
summary(cause.csl, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
# Structural path: F2 ~ F1, beta = 0.078, p = 0.422
# Fit identical to correlational latent model

semPaths(cause.csl, what = "path", whatLabels = "par",
         edge.color = "black", layout = "tree2",
         edge.label.cex = 1, residuals = F)

## 5. MEDIATION (General Template) ───────────────────────
# Replace X, Z, Y with your variables
model.med = "
  # Measurement model (if latent)
  F1 =~ Q4 + Q6 + Q7 + Q11
  F2 =~ Q8 + Q9 + Q10
  # Structural with labels
  Z  ~ a*F1
  F2 ~ b*Z + c*F1
  # Indirect and total effects
  ind   := a*b
  total := c + a*b
"
fit.med = sem(model.med, data = data,
              se = 'bootstrap', bootstrap = 1000)
summary(fit.med, fit.measures = T, standardized = T, ci = T)
parameterEstimates(fit.med, boot.ci.type = 'bca.simple')

## 6. MODERATION (Observed variables only) ───────────────
# Centre variables first (reduce multicollinearity)
data$X.c = scale(data$X, scale = FALSE)
data$M.c = scale(data$M, scale = FALSE)
data$XM  = data$X.c * data$M.c   # interaction term

model.mod = "
  Y ~ a*X.c + b*M.c + c*XM
"
fit.mod = sem(model.mod, data = data)
summary(fit.mod, fit.measures = T, standardized = T)
# If c (XM) significant → moderation exists
📖

Interpretation Guide — All Topics

GET503 Advanced Numerical Data Analysis · DrPH · USM · Based on lecturer notes & textbook references

Universal Templates All 8 Topics Interaction Effects
🔑

Universal Interpretation Framework — Overview

▼

All statistical interpretations in GET503 follow a structured template based on the REAAAPP framework (Prof Lecturer, USM DrPH). Every coefficient, test statistic, or model output must be interpreted in the context of: (1) direction, (2) magnitude, (3) precision (CI), (4) significance, and (5) practical meaning.

The Five Elements of Every Interpretation:
  1. Direction — positive/negative association, increase/decrease
  2. Magnitude — the numeric value of the coefficient/statistic
  3. Precision — 95% confidence interval
  4. Significance — p-value relative to α = 0.05
  5. Practical meaning — what this means in the real-world context of the study

Reference: Lecturer. DrPH Advanced Numerical Analysis Lecture Notes, USM, 2022–2024; Hair JF et al. (2019). Multivariate Data Analysis, 8th ed. Cengage.

📊

Interpreting Numerical (Continuous) Predictors

▼
Template sentence:
"For every one-unit increase in [predictor], [outcome] [increased/decreased] by [|β|] units (95% CI: [lower] to [upper]), holding all other variables constant (β = [value], p [</>] 0.05)."
ContextTemplateExample
Linear Regression (OLS/LMM) β = change in Y per 1-unit increase in X "For each additional year of age, stress score increased by 0.35 points (95% CI: 0.21–0.49, p<0.001)."
Logistic Regression OR = multiplicative change in odds per 1-unit increase "Each year of age increased the odds of hypertension by 8% (OR=1.08, 95% CI: 1.04–1.12, p<0.001)."
GEE (population-averaged) β = population-averaged change in mean Y "In the population, a 1-unit increase in time was associated with a mean decrease of 2.4 mmHg in SBP (95% CI: −3.1 to −1.7, p<0.001)."
Standardised (β*) β* = change in SD units of Y per 1-SD increase in X "A one standard deviation increase in age was associated with a 0.42 SD increase in stress score (β*=0.42)."

Reference: Field A (2018). Discovering Statistics Using IBM SPSS Statistics, 5th ed. Sage; Hair et al. (2019).

📌 PYQ Worked Example — stress.csv (2023/24 Q3 & 2020/21 Q2)
Dataset: stress.csv | Outcome: stress score | Predictors: time, sex | Clustering: districtid
  • time (continuous): "For a given district, each one-unit increase in time was associated with a decrease of 2.14 points in stress score, holding sex constant (β = −2.14, 95% CI: −2.68 to −1.60, p < 0.001)."
  • BMI (continuous, visualize.csv): "For every 1 kg/m² increase in BMI, SBP increased by 1.23 mmHg, holding sex and state constant (β = 1.23, 95% CI: 0.87–1.59, p < 0.001)."
🏷️

Interpreting Categorical Predictors (Dummy Coding)

▼
Template sentence:
"Compared to [reference category], [comparison group] had [significantly/no significantly] [higher/lower] [outcome] by [|β|] units (95% CI: [lower] to [upper]; β = [value], p [</>] 0.05)."
⚠ ALWAYS state the reference category — the interpretation is meaningless without it. In SPSS/R, the first (or last) alphabetical level is the default reference unless specified.
ScenarioTemplateExample
Binary categorical (2 groups) β = difference from reference group "Females scored 1.94 points higher than males in attainment (β=1.94, 95% CI: 1.62–2.27, p<0.001)."
Polytomous (3+ groups, OLS) Each dummy β vs. reference "Compared to mild stage (ref), moderate stage had 3.12 points higher pain (β=3.12, p<0.001) and severe stage had 7.45 points higher pain (β=7.45, p<0.001)."
Polytomous (logistic OR) OR vs. reference for each category "Compared to non-smokers (ref), current smokers had 2.8 times higher odds of disease (OR=2.8, 95% CI: 1.9–4.1, p<0.001)."
Non-significant State no evidence of difference "There was no significant difference in outcome between Group A and Group B (β=0.23, 95% CI: −0.45 to 0.91, p=0.512)."

Reference: Field A (2018); Lecturer, USM DrPH Lecture Notes.

📌 PYQ Worked Examples — sex & group variables
  • sex — stress.csv (binary, ref=Male): "Compared to males (reference), female participants had a significantly higher mean stress score of 3.62 points (β = 3.62, 95% CI: 2.89–4.35, p < 0.001), after adjusting for time."
  • Group — deaf children MANCOVA 2023/24 Q1 (ref=Control): "Compared to the control group (reference), the experimental group had significantly higher adjusted post-test auditory perception scores (mean difference = 1.57, 95% CI: 0.98–2.16, p < 0.001) and verbal intelligibility scores (mean difference = 1.40, 95% CI: 0.89–1.91, p < 0.001), after controlling for pre-test scores."
  • SEX — visualize.csv (ref=Male): "Females had a population-averaged SBP 4.21 mmHg lower than males (β = −4.21, 95% CI: −5.88 to −2.54, p < 0.001) after adjusting for BMI and state."
⚡

Interpreting Interaction Effects

▼
Core principle: When an interaction (X×M) is significant, the effect of X on Y depends on the level of M — main effects CANNOT be interpreted in isolation.

Template: "The effect of [X] on [Y] was significantly moderated by [M] (βX×M = [value], 95% CI: [lower] to [upper], p [</>] 0.05). Specifically, [describe direction of interaction]."
TypeInterpretation ruleExample
Continuous × Continuous Simple slopes at M = mean ±1 SD "At high stress (M+1SD), age had a stronger positive effect on BP (b=0.45, p<0.001) than at low stress (b=0.12, p=0.08)."
Continuous × Categorical Report simple slopes for each level of categorical moderator "Among females, age predicted BP more strongly (b=0.52, p<0.001) compared to males (b=0.18, p=0.12)."
Categorical × Categorical Cell means for each combination "The effect of treatment was significant in urban settings (Δ=5.2 points, p<0.001) but not in rural settings (Δ=0.8 points, p=0.42)."
MANCOVA interaction Multivariate F for interaction, then univariate breakdown per DV "The Group × Time interaction was statistically significant (Wilks' Λ=0.79, F(2,97)=12.9, p<0.001, η²=0.21), indicating differential change in DVs across groups over time."
SEM Moderation (interaction term) Report conditional effect at specific values of moderator "The indirect effect of stress on outcome via coping was significant when social support was high (β=0.31, 95% BCa CI: 0.14–0.49) but not when low (β=0.08, 95% BCa CI: −0.06 to 0.23)."
Non-significant interaction State no evidence of moderation — main effects can be interpreted "The interaction between age and sex was not statistically significant (β=0.03, p=0.67), indicating that the effect of age on BP did not differ by sex."
⚠ Common mistakes:
  • Interpreting main effects when interaction is significant — always interpret the conditional effect instead
  • Forgetting to mean-centre continuous predictors before computing interaction terms
  • Confusing statistical with practical significance in interactions

Reference: Aiken LS, West SG (1991).

📌 PYQ Worked Examples — Interaction effects
  • Group × Time (MANCOVA 2023/24 Q1 — homogeneity of slopes): "The Group × Pre-test score interaction was non-significant (Wilks' Λ = 0.97, F(2,17) = 0.31, p = 0.74), confirming the homogeneity of regression slopes assumption — the covariate-DV relationship was the same for both experimental and control groups."
  • Sex × Time (stress.csv LMM — hypothetical moderation): "To test whether the effect of time on stress differed by sex, an interaction term sex×time was added. The interaction was non-significant (β = 0.18, 95% CI: −0.35 to 0.71, p = 0.51), indicating that stress declined at the same rate over time for both males and females."
  • BMI × SEX (visualize.csv — SBP moderation): "If the interaction BMI×SEX were significant (e.g., β = 0.42, p = 0.03), interpretation would be: 'Among males, each kg/m² increase in BMI raised SBP by 1.65 mmHg, whereas among females the increase was only 1.23 mmHg, reflecting a stronger BMI-SBP association in males.'"
Multiple Regression: Testing and Interpreting Interactions. Sage; Hayes AF (2018). Introduction to Mediation, Moderation, and Conditional Process Analysis, 2nd ed. Guilford; Tabachnick BG, Fidell LS (2019). Using Multivariate Statistics, 7th ed.

📋

Reporting Checklist — Every Analysis

▼
Minimum reporting elements:
  • Test statistic with df (e.g., F(2,97)=12.3, t(45)=3.2)
  • Exact p-value (p=0.031, not just p<0.05)
  • Effect size + 95% CI
  • Direction of effect
  • Reference/comparison group for categorical
Effect size benchmarks (Cohen, 1988):
StatisticSmallMediumLarge
η² (MANOVA)0.010.060.14
Cohen's d0.20.50.8
R² (regression)0.020.130.26
CFI/TLI (SEM)—≥0.95

Cohen J (1988). Statistical Power Analysis for the Behavioral Sciences, 2nd ed. Lawrence Erlbaum.

📊

MANOVA — Step-by-Step Interpretation

▼

Follow this sequence for every MANOVA output. Ref: Tabachnick & Fidell (2019); Stevens JP (2009). Applied Multivariate Statistics for the Social Sciences, 5th ed. Routledge.

  1. Step 1 — Check multivariate normality and outliers: Box's M (p>0.001 acceptable), Mahalanobis distance D² (no extreme outliers). Report: "Box's M test was non-significant (F=2.14, p=0.09), indicating homogeneity of covariance matrices was met."
  2. Step 2 — Multivariate omnibus test (Wilks' Λ): Report Wilks' Λ, F, df1, df2, p, η². Template: "The multivariate test was statistically significant (Wilks' Λ = 0.72, F(4, 192) = 8.91, p < 0.001, η² = 0.157), indicating that the groups differed significantly on the combined DVs."
  3. Step 3 — Univariate follow-up (with Bonferroni correction): For each DV, report F, df, p, η². Adjust α = 0.05/number of DVs. Template: "Univariate ANOVAs indicated significant group differences on [DV1] (F(2,97)=12.3, p<0.001, η²=0.20) and [DV2] (F(2,97)=7.8, p=0.001, η²=0.14)."
  4. Step 4 — Post-hoc tests: Report pairwise comparisons (Tukey/Games-Howell). Template: "Post-hoc comparisons indicated that Group A scored significantly higher than Group B (mean difference = 2.3, 95% CI: 0.8–3.8, p=0.003) and Group C (mean difference = 3.1, 95% CI: 1.5–4.7, p<0.001)."
📈

MANCOVA — Additional Covariate Steps

▼

MANCOVA adds covariate control. Ref: Stevens (2009); Tabachnick & Fidell (2019). Ch. 8.

  1. Homogeneity of regression slopes: Group × Covariate interaction must be non-significant (p>0.05). Template: "The assumption of homogeneity of regression slopes was satisfied, as the Group × Covariate interaction was non-significant (Wilks' Λ=0.98, F(4,186)=0.51, p=0.73)."
  2. Multivariate test with covariate: Same as MANOVA but now 'after controlling for [covariate]'. Template: "After controlling for pre-test scores, the multivariate test remained significant (Wilks' Λ=0.68, F(4,190)=10.2, p<0.001, η²=0.18)."
  3. Adjusted means: Report estimated marginal means (EMM), not raw means. Template: "Adjusted group means (controlling for pre-test scores) were: Group A = 24.3 (SE=0.4), Group B = 21.1 (SE=0.4), Group C = 18.7 (SE=0.4)."
  4. Covariate effect: Report whether covariate itself was significant. Template: "The covariate (pre-test score) was significantly associated with post-test outcome (F(1,96)=34.2, p<0.001, η²=0.26), confirming the appropriateness of its inclusion."
Full reporting template:
"A one-way MANCOVA was conducted to examine the effect of [IV] on [DV1] and [DV2], controlling for [covariate]. The assumption of homogeneity of regression slopes was met (p=0.73). After controlling for [covariate], a significant multivariate effect was found (Wilks' Λ=0.68, F(4,190)=10.2, p<0.001, η²=0.18). Univariate ANCOVAs showed that [IV] significantly affected [DV1] (F(2,96)=8.4, p<0.001, η²=0.15) and [DV2] (F(2,96)=5.1, p=0.008, η²=0.10) after Bonferroni correction (α=0.025)."

Ref: Tabachnick BG, Fidell LS (2019). Using Multivariate Statistics, 7th ed. Pearson. Ch. 7–8; Stevens JP (2009). Applied Multivariate Statistics for the Social Sciences, 5th ed. Routledge.

📌 Fully Worked Example — PYQ 2023/24 Q1: Deaf Children MANCOVA
n=24 deaf children (Experimental n=12 vs Control n=12); DVs: auditory perception + verbal intelligibility; Covariate: pre-test scores
  1. Assumption check: "Box's M test was non-significant (p > 0.001), indicating homogeneity of covariance matrices. The Group × Pre-test interaction was non-significant (Wilks' Λ = 0.97, F(2,17) = 0.31, p = 0.74), confirming homogeneity of regression slopes."
  2. Multivariate omnibus: "After controlling for pre-test scores, a statistically significant overall group effect was found on the combined DVs (Wilks' Λ, F(2,19) = 4.03, p < 0.001). Both pre-test covariates were significant: auditory perception pre-test (F(1,19) = 4.90, p = 0.002) and verbal intelligibility pre-test (F(1,19) = 6.05, p < 0.001)."
  3. Univariate follow-up (Bonferroni α = 0.025):
    — "Auditory perception: The experimental group scored significantly higher than controls after adjusting for pre-test (F(1,20) = 24.54, p < 0.001, η² = 0.56 — large effect)."
    — "Verbal intelligibility: Similarly, the experimental group had significantly higher adjusted post-test verbal intelligibility (F(1,20) = 27.56, p < 0.001, η² = 0.68 — large effect)."
  4. Conclusion sentence: "The cognitive rehabilitation programme significantly improved both auditory perception (η² = 0.56) and verbal intelligibility (η² = 0.68) in deaf children compared to controls, even after controlling for pre-test baseline scores. Effect sizes indicate large, clinically meaningful programme impact (Cohen, 1988)."
🔗

LMM — Fixed Effects Interpretation

▼

Ref: Snijders TAB, Bosker RJ (2012). Multilevel Analysis, 2nd ed. Sage; Singer JD, Willett JB (2003). Applied Longitudinal Data Analysis. Oxford UP; USM DrPH Notes.

LMM fixed effects = cluster-specific (conditional) effects
β represents the expected change in Y for a 1-unit increase in X, for a cluster with the same random effect value (i.e., within-cluster effect). This is a subject-specific interpretation.
CoefficientTemplate Sentence
Intercept (β₀) "The estimated mean [outcome] was [β₀] [units] when all predictors were zero (or at their reference level), for a cluster with an average random effect (β₀ = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Continuous predictor (βₓ) "Holding all other covariates constant, a 1-unit increase in [X] was associated with a [β] [unit increase/decrease] in [outcome] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Categorical predictor "Compared to [reference group], [comparison group] had a [β] [unit] [higher/lower] mean [outcome], adjusting for other covariates (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Time (longitudinal) "On average, [outcome] [increased/decreased] by [β] [units] per [time unit] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05), indicating [direction of longitudinal trend]."
📐

LMM — Random Effects & ICC Interpretation

▼
ICC (Intraclass Correlation Coefficient):
ICC = τ₀² / (τ₀² + σ²)
where τ₀² = between-cluster variance, σ² = within-cluster (residual) variance
ComponentTemplate Sentence
Null model ICC "The null model revealed an ICC of [value], indicating that [ICC×100]% of the total variance in [outcome] was attributable to between-[cluster] differences. This level of clustering [justifies/suggests] the use of multilevel modelling." (ICC >0.05 typically justifies multilevel; Hox, 2010)
Random intercept variance (τ₀²) "The random intercept variance was τ₀² = [value], reflecting substantial [small/moderate/large] variation in baseline [outcome] across [clusters]."
Residual variance (σ²) "The within-[cluster] (residual) variance was σ² = [value], representing unexplained variation at the individual level."
Model comparison (LRT) "A likelihood ratio test indicated that the random slope model provided a significantly better fit than the random intercept model (χ²([df]) = [value], p [</>] 0.05), justifying the additional complexity."
⚠ SPSS note: In SPSS Mixed (Analyze → Mixed Models → Linear), ICC is not given directly. Compute: ICC = Variance(Intercept) / [Variance(Intercept) + Residual]. Use REML for final model, ML for LRT model comparisons.

Ref: Snijders TAB, Bosker RJ (2012). Multilevel Analysis, 2nd ed. Sage. p. 17–22; Hox JJ, Moerbeek M, van de Schoot R (2017). Multilevel Analysis: Techniques and Applications, 3rd ed. Routledge; Lecturer. USM DrPH Notes, 2022.

🔄

GEE — Population-Averaged Interpretation

▼

Ref: Liang KY, Zeger SL (1986). Longitudinal data analysis using generalised linear models. Biometrika, 73:13–22; Diggle PJ, Heagerty P, Liang KY, Zeger SL (2002). Analysis of Longitudinal Data, 2nd ed. Oxford UP; Fitzmaurice GM, Laird NM, Ware JH (2011). Applied Longitudinal Analysis, 2nd ed. Wiley.

GEE = population-averaged (marginal) effects
β represents the change in the population mean outcome for a 1-unit change in X — averaged across all individuals in the population, regardless of cluster membership. This contrasts with LMM's cluster-specific interpretation.
CoefficientTemplate Sentence (Continuous outcome)
Continuous predictor "In the population, a 1-unit increase in [X] was associated with a mean [increase/decrease] of [|β|] [units] in [outcome] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], Wald χ²([df]) = [value], p [</>] 0.05)."
Categorical predictor "In the population, [comparison group] had a population-averaged [outcome] that was [β] [units] [higher/lower] than [reference group] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], p [</>] 0.05)."
Working correlation structure "An exchangeable working correlation structure was selected based on the lowest QIC value (QIC = [value]). The estimated within-subject correlation was ρ = [value]."
Standard errors "Robust (sandwich) standard errors were used to ensure valid inference regardless of misspecification of the working correlation structure (Liang & Zeger, 1986)."
LMM vs GEE — Which interpretation to use:
  • LMM β: "For a given [patient/school/cluster], a 1-unit increase in X is associated with β change in Y" — cluster-specific
  • GEE β: "In the population, a 1-unit increase in X is associated with β change in average Y" — population-averaged
  • For clinical decisions about individual patients → LMM (subject-specific); for public health policy about populations → GEE (population-averaged). (Fitzmaurice et al., 2011, p. 353)
✓ Full GEE reporting template:
"Generalised estimating equations (GEE) with an exchangeable working correlation structure (QIC = [value]) were used to estimate population-averaged effects on [outcome], accounting for repeated measurements within [clusters/subjects]. Robust standard errors were applied. After adjusting for [covariates], [predictor] was associated with a population-averaged [increase/decrease] of [|β|] [units] in [outcome] (β = [value], 95% CI: [lower]–[upper], Wald χ²(1) = [value], p [</>] 0.05). The estimated within-subject correlation was ρ = [value]."

Ref: Diggle PJ et al. (2002). Analysis of Longitudinal Data, 2nd ed. Oxford UP; Fitzmaurice GM et al. (2011). Applied Longitudinal Analysis, 2nd ed. Wiley; Liang KY, Zeger SL (1986). Biometrika, 73:13–22; USM DrPH Notes.

CFA — Interpreting Confirmatory Factor Analysis

▼

Ref: Lecturer, USM CFA Lecture Notes; Brown TA (2015). Confirmatory Factor Analysis for Applied Research, 2nd ed. Guilford; Hair et al. (2019).

IndexAcceptable ThresholdInterpretation Template
χ²/df (CMIN/DF) < 3.0 (Hair et al., 2019) "The χ²/df ratio was [value], indicating [acceptable/poor] model fit."
CFI / TLI ≥ 0.95 (Hu & Bentler, 1999) "CFI = [value] and TLI = [value], indicating [good/acceptable/poor] incremental fit."
RMSEA < 0.06 (Hu & Bentler, 1999); < 0.08 acceptable "RMSEA = [value] (90% CI: [lower]–[upper]), indicating [close/adequate/poor] approximate fit."
SRMR < 0.08 (Hu & Bentler, 1999) "SRMR = [value], indicating [good/acceptable/poor] absolute fit."
Factor loadings (λ) ≥ 0.50 (Hair et al.); ≥ 0.70 ideal "All standardised loadings were ≥ 0.50, ranging from [min] to [max], indicating acceptable indicator reliability."
AVE (convergent validity) ≥ 0.50 (Fornell & Larcker, 1981) "AVE = [value] (≥0.50), confirming convergent validity — the factor explained more variance in its indicators than error."
CR (construct reliability) ≥ 0.70 "Composite reliability (CR = [value]) exceeded the 0.70 threshold, indicating adequate construct reliability."
Full CFA reporting template:
"A CFA was conducted using lavaan (Rosseel, 2012) with maximum likelihood estimation. Model fit was assessed using multiple indices: χ²/df = [value], CFI = [value], TLI = [value], RMSEA = [value] (90% CI: [lower]–[upper]), SRMR = [value]. The model demonstrated [good/acceptable] fit. All standardised factor loadings were significant (p < 0.001) and ranged from [min] to [max]. AVE ([value]) and CR ([value]) for [construct] exceeded minimum thresholds (Fornell & Larcker, 1981; Hair et al., 2019), supporting convergent validity and construct reliability."

Ref: Brown TA (2015). Confirmatory Factor Analysis for Applied Research, 2nd ed. Guilford; Hu L, Bentler PM (1999). Structural Equation Modeling, 6(1):1–55; Fornell C, Larcker DF (1981). Journal of Marketing Research, 18:39–50; Lecturer, USM Notes.

SEM — Overall Model Fit Interpretation

▼

Ref: Kline RB (2016). Principles and Practice of Structural Equation Modeling, 4th ed. Guilford; Lecturer, USM SEM Lecture Notes.

Full reporting template:
"The hypothesised SEM was evaluated using lavaan (Rosseel, 2012) with maximum likelihood estimation. Model fit indices indicated [good/acceptable] overall fit: χ²([df]) = [value], p = [value]; χ²/df = [value]; CFI = [value]; TLI = [value]; RMSEA = [value] (90% CI: [lower]–[upper]); SRMR = [value]."
Path TypeInterpretation Template
Direct path (X → Y) "[X] had a significant [positive/negative] direct effect on [Y] (β = [value], SE = [value], z = [value], p [</>] 0.05), indicating that a 1-unit increase in [X] was associated with a [β] [unit] [increase/decrease] in [Y], after controlling for other paths."
Indirect effect (mediation) — a×b "The indirect effect of [X] on [Y] through [Mediator] was [significant/non-significant] (indirect effect = [a×b value], 95% BCa bootstrap CI: [lower]–[upper]). [As the CI did not include zero, / As the CI included zero,] the mediation was [supported/not supported]."
Full vs partial mediation "[Full mediation:] The direct path from [X] to [Y] was non-significant after including [M] in the model (β = [value], p = [value]), while the indirect effect remained significant, supporting full mediation. [Partial mediation:] Both the direct and indirect paths remained significant, indicating partial mediation."
Correlational path (~~) "[X1] and [X2] were significantly correlated (r = [value], p [</>] 0.05), indicating a [weak/moderate/strong] [positive/negative] association between the two exogenous variables."
Moderated mediation "The conditional indirect effect of [X] on [Y] through [M] was significant when [Moderator] was high (indirect = [value], 95% BCa CI: [lower]–[upper]) but not when low (indirect = [value], 95% BCa CI: [lower]–[upper]), supporting moderated mediation (Hayes, 2018)."

Ref: Kline RB (2016). Principles and Practice of SEM, 4th ed. Guilford; Hayes AF (2018). Introduction to Mediation, Moderation, and Conditional Process Analysis, 2nd ed. Guilford; Lecturer, USM SEM Notes & wnarifin.github.io/drph.html.

📌 PYQ Worked Example — SEM Partial Mediation: Coping as Mediator of Time→Stress (n=195)
time (X) → approach_coping (M) → stress_score (Y); Estimator: MLR, Bootstrap N=5000 for indirect effect
model <- '
 approach =~ Q1+Q3+Q5+Q7+Q9
 approach ~ a*time
 score ~ b*approach + c_prime*time + sex
 indirect := a*b; total := c_prime + a*b'
PathβSE95% Boot CIpInterpretation
a: time→approach0.420.060.31–0.53<0.001Longer exposure → more approach coping
b: approach→score−0.310.07−0.44 to −0.18<0.001Higher approach coping → lower stress
c': time→score (direct)−1.450.29−2.02 to −0.88<0.001Remaining direct effect after accounting for M
a×b: indirect−0.130.04−0.21 to −0.06sigBootstrap CI excludes 0 → mediation confirmed
Total (c'+a×b)−1.58———Total effect of time on stress

Model fit: CFI=0.941, RMSEA=0.061 (90% CI: 0.041–0.081), SRMR=0.071 — acceptable fit (Hu & Bentler, 1999).
Verdict: c' remains significant while indirect is also significant → partial mediation.
Sentence: "The hypothesised SEM (MLR estimator, n=195) demonstrated acceptable fit (CFI=0.941, RMSEA=0.061, SRMR=0.071). Approach coping partially mediated the effect of time on stress score: indirect effect β=−0.13 (95% bootstrap CI: −0.21 to −0.06), direct effect β=−1.45, p<0.001 (Hayes, 2018; Kline, 2016). The total effect of time on stress was β=−1.58, of which 8.2% was transmitted through approach coping."

Past Year Questions — Model Answers

GET503 Advanced Numerical Data Analysis · DrPH · USM

2023/2024 2020/2021 2018/2019
Q1 · 25 marksMANCOVA — Cognitive Rehabilitation in Deaf Children▾
Scenario: Quasi-experimental study, pre-test/post-test + control group. 24 deaf children (n=12 experimental, n=12 control). DVs: auditory perception + verbal intelligibility. Covariate: pre-test scores.
(a) Rationales & assumptions of MANCOVA [8 marks]

Rationales (4):

  1. Multiple correlated DVs: Auditory perception and verbal intelligibility are two related continuous outcomes. MANCOVA analyses them jointly, preserving their correlation structure and avoiding inflated Type I error from separate ANCOVAs.
  2. Control covariate: Pre-test scores are included to statistically equate groups on baseline differences — critical in quasi-experimental designs without full randomisation.
  3. Reduce Type I error: Simultaneous analysis with one omnibus test controls familywise error rate better than multiple ANCOVAs.
  4. Increase statistical power: Partialling out covariate variance reduces MSE, increasing sensitivity to detect the true group effect.

Assumptions (4):

  1. Multivariate normality of DVs within each group.
  2. Homogeneity of variance-covariance matrices (Box's M test): p > 0.001 acceptable (lenient, Box's M is sensitive to non-normality).
  3. Absence of multicollinearity: DVs should be correlated (r = 0.3–0.8) but not too highly (r < 0.90).
  4. Homogeneity of regression slopes: Interaction IV×covariate must be non-significant (p > 0.05), meaning the covariate-DV relationship is the same across groups.
  5. Linearity between covariate (pre-test) and each DV (post-test) within groups.
  6. No multivariate outliers (assessed via Mahalanobis distance).
(b) Describe the covariate & reasons [5 marks]

Covariate: Pre-test scores of both auditory perception and verbal intelligibility (baseline measurements before the programme).

Reasons:

  1. In a quasi-experimental design, participants are not randomly assigned — pre-existing group differences can confound results. Pre-test scores control for these baseline differences.
  2. Adjusting for pre-test scores reduces unexplained variance in post-test scores, lowering MSE and improving precision.
  3. The covariate should be linearly related to DVs (pre-test and post-test of the same construct are expected to be strongly correlated).
  4. Covariates should be uncorrelated with the grouping variable; high inter-covariate correlation (r > 0.7) indicates multicollinearity.
  5. Residuals from the covariate-adjusted model should be normally distributed and homoscedastic.
(c) Report findings — Wilks' Λ F(2,19)=4.03, p=0.0001 [12 marks]

The experimental group showed a notable increase in auditory perception from pre-test (M=5.63, SD=0.56) to post-test (M=7.29, SD=0.60), while the control group showed minimal change (pre: M=5.67, SD=0.49; post: M=5.72, SD=0.47). Similarly, verbal intelligibility increased markedly in the experimental group (pre: M=2.58, SD=0.23; post: M=4.01, SD=0.23) compared to marginal change in controls (pre: M=2.55, SD=0.23; post: M=2.61, SD=0.19).

The one-way MANCOVA revealed a statistically significant overall difference in combined post-test scores between groups after controlling for pre-test scores [Wilks' Λ = F(2,19) = 4.03, p < 0.001]. Pre-test auditory perception [F(1,19)=4.90, p=0.002] and pre-test verbal intelligibility [F(1,19)=6.05, p<0.001] were both significant covariates.

Follow-up univariate ANCOVA revealed significant group effects on auditory perception [F(1,20)=24.54, p<0.001, η²=0.56] and verbal intelligibility [F(1,20)=27.56, p<0.001, η²=0.68], both large effect sizes.

Conclusion: The cognitive rehabilitation programme significantly improved both auditory perception and verbal intelligibility in the experimental group compared to controls, even after adjusting for baseline scores. Large effect sizes (η²=0.56 and 0.68) indicate clinically meaningful programme impact.

Q2 · 25 marksEFA — Sleep Quality Questionnaire (14 items, n=100, sleep.sav)▾
Task: Explore construct validity & internal consistency of 14-item sleep quality questionnaire, n=100.
(a) Analysis [15 marks]

Step 1 — Assess factorability

R
library(psych); library(haven)
data <- read_sav("sleep.sav")
cor_mx <- cor(data, use="complete.obs")
KMO(cor_mx)              # KMO > 0.60 acceptable; >0.80 meritorious
cortest.bartlett(cor_mx, n=100)  # p < 0.05 needed

SPSS: Analyze → Dimension Reduction → Factor → Descriptives → KMO and Bartlett's test

Step 2 — Determine number of factors

R
scree(cor_mx)            # Scree plot — look for 'elbow'
fa.parallel(data, fm="pa", fa="fa")   # Parallel analysis (most accurate)

Retain factors with eigenvalue >1 (Kaiser) confirmed by parallel analysis.

Step 3 — Extract & rotate

R
efa <- fa(data, nfactors=N, rotate="oblimin", fm="pa")
print(efa, digits=3, cut=0.3, sort=TRUE)
fa.diagram(efa)

SPSS: Extraction: Principal Axis Factoring → Rotation: Direct Oblimin → Options: Suppress < 0.40

Step 4 — Clean items: Retain items loading ≥0.40 on one factor; remove cross-loaders (difference between top-2 loadings <0.20).

Step 5 — Reliability

R
library(psych)
alpha(data[, items_F1])   # Cronbach's alpha per factor; acceptable α ≥0.70

SPSS: Analyze → Scale → Reliability Analysis → Alpha

(b) Interpretation [10 marks]

Q4 · 10 marksStatistical Writing — Comment on article's statistical analysis▾

Structure your critique using the REAAAPP framework (2 marks per point):

  1. Research design appropriateness: Does the statistical method match the study design and research question? Is the chosen test (e.g., t-test, ANOVA, regression) appropriate for the outcome type (continuous/categorical), number of groups, and study objective?
  2. Assumption checks reported: Were normality, homogeneity of variance, independence, and linearity assessed and reported? If violated, was a non-parametric alternative or robust test used?
  3. Adequacy of sample size: Was a power analysis performed a priori? Is the n adequate for the statistical model complexity (e.g., ≥10–20 obs per predictor in regression; ≥30 per group in ANOVA)?
  4. Presentation of results: Are effect sizes (Cohen's d, η², OR, R²) and 95% confidence intervals reported alongside p-values? Are means ± SD provided?
  5. Interpretation accuracy: Does the stated conclusion match the actual statistical output? Is causation incorrectly inferred from correlational data? Is clinical significance distinguished from statistical significance?

  1. Open: File → Open → Data → coping.sav (verify 10 items Q1–Q10, n=195)
  2. Run EFA: Analyze → Dimension Reduction → Factor → select Q1–Q10
      • Descriptives: KMO + Bartlett's, correlation matrix
      • Extraction: Principal Axis Factoring; Eigenvalues over 1; display Scree plot
      • Rotation: Direct Oblimin (oblique — assumes factors may correlate)
      • Options: Sorted by size; Suppress absolute values < 0.40
  3. Reliability: Analyze → Scale → Reliability Analysis → Items per factor → Alpha
  4. Save output: File → Export → Output as coping.docx

Justification of each step:

  • KMO >0.60 + Bartlett's p<0.05: confirms data are factorable
  • PAF preferred over PCA for identifying latent constructs (not data reduction)
  • Oblimin rotation: coping factors are expected to correlate in real-world settings
  • Loading threshold ≥0.40: ensures items have meaningful factor relationships
  • Cronbach's α ≥0.70: establishes internal consistency of each subscale

Conclusion template: EFA on coping.sav (n=195, 10 items) yielded KMO=[value] (adequate/meritorious). Bartlett's test significant (χ²([df])=[value], p<0.001). [N]-factor solution identified explaining [X]% variance. Items loaded ≥0.40 on respective factors; [Y] items retained. All factors showed acceptable reliability (α range: [min–max]). Results support construct validity of the coping skill questionnaire.

Q4 · 4 marksFactor Analysis — 4 Differences: EFA vs CFA▾
DimensionEFACFA
1. PurposeExploratory — discover factor structure with no prior theoryConfirmatory — test a pre-specified hypothesis about factor structure
2. Prior specificationNo prior knowledge; all items free to load on all factorsResearcher specifies which items load on which factors a priori
3. Software/methodPAF/PCA in SPSS or psych package (R)SEM via lavaan (R) / AMOS / LISREL
4. Fit evaluationEigenvalues, scree plot, % variance explained, factor loadingsGlobal fit indices: CFI ≥0.95, TLI ≥0.95, RMSEA ≤0.08, SRMR ≤0.08
Q5 · 7 marksSEM — Attitude → Intention → Exercise Behavior▾
Indicators: Att1, Att2 (Attitude); Int1, Int2 (Intention); Beh1, Beh2 (Behavior). Indirect effect: Attitude → Intention → Behavior.
(a) Draw SEM diagram [5 marks]
  Att1 ──┐                           Int1 ──┐                     Beh1 ──┐
  Att2 ──┤──> ( ATTITUDE ) ──β─> ( INTENTION ) ──β─> ( BEHAVIOR ) <──┤
         └── εAtt                  └── εInt   ↑                  └── Beh2
                                                    εInt2         εBeh

  Legend: ( ) = Latent variable (oval)   [ ] = Observed indicator (rectangle)
          → single-headed = causal path   ε = error/disturbance variance

lavaan code:
  Attitude  =~ Att1 + Att2
  Intention =~ Int1 + Int2
  Behavior  =~ Beh1 + Beh2
  Intention ~ Attitude          # a path
  Behavior  ~ Intention         # b path
  indirect := a * b             # indirect effect (mediation)
(b) Count latent variables and error variances [3 marks — 1 each]
Endogenous LV2 — Intention and Behavior (both receive causal arrows; they are “outcome” LVs)
Exogenous LV1 — Attitude (purely a predictor; no incoming causal path)
Error variances6 — one per observed indicator (Att1, Att2, Int1, Int2, Beh1, Beh2); disturbance terms for endogenous LVs are additional
Q6 · 30 marksMANCOVA — Cognitive Rehabilitation (Full Model Answer from Pursuit of Excellence)▾
(a) Rationales & assumptions [10 marks]

Rationales: The researchers wanted to assess statistical differences in two continuous DVs (auditory perception and verbal intelligibility) based on an independent grouping variable (intervention group), while controlling for pre-test scores. Since the two DVs are likely correlated, MANCOVA is appropriate — it preserves multivariate structure. Including pre-test scores as covariates reduces error variance and enhances power. Simultaneous MANCOVA testing controls familywise Type I error, which would be inflated by separate ANCOVAs.

Assumptions: (1) Multivariate normality of DVs within each group. (2) Homogeneity of variance-covariance matrices across groups (Box's M test, p>0.001). (3) Linear relationship between covariate and each DV. (4) Covariates reliably measured, uncorrelated with the IV. (5) No severe multicollinearity (r<0.90 between DVs). (6) Homogeneity of regression slopes (IV×covariate interaction p>0.05). (7) Homoscedastic residuals (Levene's test per DV).

(b) Describe covariate & reasons [8 marks]

Covariates: Pre-test scores of auditory perception and verbal intelligibility (baseline before the programme).

  1. Covariates must be measured reliably, without significant measurement error.
  2. A linear relationship must exist between pre-test scores and post-test DVs.
  3. Covariates should be uncorrelated with the IV; high inter-covariate correlations (r>0.7) cause multicollinearity.
  4. Residuals from the adjusted model should be normally distributed and homoscedastic.
  5. Homogeneity of regression slopes must hold across groups.
  6. In quasi-experimental design, participants are not randomly assigned — pre-test scores control for pre-existing group differences.
  7. Adjusting for pre-test scores reduces unexplained variance in post-test scores, lowering MSE and increasing the F-ratio.
  8. Controlling baseline differences ensures results validly reflect the programme effect rather than selection bias.
(c) Report findings — Wilks' Λ F(2,19)=4.03, p=0.0001 [12 marks]

The experimental group showed a notable increase in mean auditory perception from pre-test (M=5.63, SD=0.56) to post-test (M=7.29, SD=0.60), while the control group showed minimal change (pre: M=5.67, SD=0.49; post: M=5.72, SD=0.47). For verbal intelligibility, the experimental group improved markedly (pre: M=2.58, SD=0.23; post: M=4.01, SD=0.23) compared to minimal change in controls (pre: M=2.55; post: M=2.61).

The overall one-way MANCOVA revealed a statistically significant difference in combined post-test scores between groups after adjusting for pre-test scores [Wilks' Λ = F(2,19) = 4.03, p < 0.001]. Both pre-test covariates were significant: auditory perception [F(1,19)=4.90, p=0.002] and verbal intelligibility [F(1,19)=6.05, p<0.001].

Follow-up univariate ANCOVAs showed significant group effects on auditory perception [F(1,20)=24.54, p<0.001, η²=0.56] and verbal intelligibility [F(1,20)=27.56, p<0.001, η²=0.68] — both large effects.

Conclusion: The cognitive rehabilitation programme significantly improved both auditory perception (η²=0.56) and verbal intelligibility (η²=0.68) in the experimental group compared to controls, after controlling for baseline differences. The large effect sizes confirm clinically meaningful programme impact.

R
r1_ml <- lmer(score~time+sex+(1|districtid), REML=FALSE, data=data)
r2_ml <- lmer(score~time+sex+(1+time|districtid), REML=FALSE, data=data)
anova(r1_ml, r2_ml)   # Likelihood ratio test (LRT)
AIC(r1_ml, r2_ml); BIC(r1_ml, r2_ml)

Decision: If LRT p<0.05 AND AIC/BIC lower for r2 → adopt RS model (r2): the effect of time varies significantly across districts, and forcing the same slope (r1) under-fits the data. If p≥0.05 → retain RI model (r1) for parsimony: no evidence that slopes differ between districts.

Q2 · 15 marksMachine Learning — Definition and Tasks▾
(a) Define machine learning [3 marks]

Machine learning (ML) is a subfield of artificial intelligence (AI) in which algorithms are designed to learn patterns from data and improve their performance on specific tasks without being explicitly programmed with rules. The algorithm adjusts its internal parameters based on training data, enabling it to generalise to new, unseen data. (1 mark: subfield of AI / learns from data; 1 mark: no explicit programming; 1 mark: generalises/improves with experience.)

(b) Describe different ML tasks with examples [12 marks — 4 marks each]

1. Regression (Supervised — continuous outcome)

Algorithm learns from labelled data to predict a numeric outcome. Used when the dependent variable is continuous.

Example: Predicting patient blood pressure (SBP) from age, BMI, and physical activity level using decision tree regression or linear regression.

R
library(rpart)
model <- rpart(SBP ~ age + BMI + activity, data=train, method="anova")
pred  <- predict(model, newdata=test)
# Evaluate: RMSE, MAE, R-squared

2. Classification (Supervised — categorical outcome)

Algorithm learns to assign observations to categories. Used when the outcome is a class label (binary or multi-class).

Example: Classifying whether a patient has diabetes (Yes/No) based on glucose, BMI, and age using random forest or logistic regression.

R
library(randomForest)
model <- randomForest(diabetes ~ glucose + BMI + age, data=train)
pred  <- predict(model, newdata=test)
# Evaluate: accuracy, sensitivity, specificity, ROC-AUC
library(pROC)
roc(test$diabetes, as.numeric(pred))

3. Clustering (Unsupervised — no labels)

Algorithm discovers hidden groupings or structure in data without labelled outcomes. Used for exploratory pattern discovery.

Example: Grouping patients into clusters based on symptom profiles to identify disease subtypes using k-means clustering.

R
set.seed(123)
km <- kmeans(data[,c("symptom1","symptom2","symptom3")], centers=3)
data$cluster <- km$cluster
table(data$cluster)
Q3 · 25 marksEFA + CFA — Divorce Questionnaire (49 items, divorce1.0.sav)▾
EFA output given: KMO=0.964 (marvellous), 4 factors extracted, 36 items retained (13 removed — low loadings/cross-loadings)
(a) Interpret EFA results [5 marks]

The KMO measure of sampling adequacy was 0.964, indicating marvellous factorability. Bartlett's test of sphericity was statistically significant (p<0.001), confirming non-identity correlation matrix — data are suitable for EFA.

Parallel analysis and scree plot suggested a 4-factor solution. Using PAF with direct oblimin rotation, 4 factors were extracted. Of the original 49 items, 13 were removed due to loadings below 0.40 or cross-loadings (difference <0.20 between highest two loadings), retaining 36 items. Each factor showed adequate to good internal consistency (Cronbach's α to be reported). The 4 factors represent distinct dimensions of divorce-related constructs (factor naming depends on item content).

(b) Proceed with CFA — R code, output, interpret [20 marks]
R
library(lavaan); library(haven)
data <- read_sav("divorce1.0.sav")

# CFA model: 4 factors, items assigned per EFA pattern matrix
cfa_model <- '
  F1 =~ item1 + item2 + item3 + item4 + item5 + item6 + item7 + item8 + item9
  F2 =~ item10 + item11 + item12 + item13 + item14 + item15 + item16 + item17

  F4 =~ item27 + item28 + item29 + item30 + item31 + item32 + item33 + item34 + item35 + item36
'

                   "rmsea.ci.upper","srmr","aic","bic"))

Q4 · 20 marksMANOVA — 3 groups × 3 DVs (manova.sav) — Full Model Answer▾
Dataset: manova.sav | 3 groups | DVs: useful, difficulty, importance | Result: Wilks' Λ=0.526, F(6,56)=3.538, p=0.005, η²=0.275
(a) Perform analysis — save output and interpret each step [16 marks]
R
library(haven); library(car); library(biotools); library(effectsize)
data <- read_sav("manova.sav")
data$group <- factor(data$group)

# Step 1: Assumption checks
# Box's M test (homogeneity of variance-covariance matrices)
boxM(data[,c("useful","difficulty","importance")], data$group)
# p > 0.001 acceptable (Box's M is sensitive to non-normality)

# Levene's test per DV (homogeneity of variance)
leveneTest(useful ~ group, data=data)
leveneTest(difficulty ~ group, data=data)
leveneTest(importance ~ group, data=data)

# Univariate normality per group
by(data$useful, data$group, shapiro.test)

# Step 2: MANOVA
dv <- cbind(data$useful, data$difficulty, data$importance)
manova_fit <- manova(dv ~ group, data=data)
summary(manova_fit, test="Wilks")   # Wilks' Lambda test

# Step 3: Effect size
eta_squared(manova_fit)
# Manual: 1 - Wilks' Lambda = 1 - 0.526 = 0.474 (multivariate eta-squared)

# Step 4: Follow-up univariate ANOVAs
summary.aov(manova_fit)

# Step 5: Post-hoc pairwise comparisons per DV
TukeyHSD(aov(useful ~ group, data=data))
TukeyHSD(aov(difficulty ~ group, data=data))
TukeyHSD(aov(importance ~ group, data=data))

SPSS: Analyze → General Linear Model → Multivariate → DVs: useful, difficulty, importance → Fixed factor: group → Options: Effect size, Observed power, Homogeneity tests → Post Hoc: Tukey → OK

Interpretation:

Box's M test was [non-significant/p=__], [supporting/violating] homogeneity of variance-covariance matrices. The one-way MANOVA revealed a statistically significant overall difference in the combined DVs (usefulness, difficulty, importance) across the three groups [Wilks' Λ=0.526, F(6,56)=3.538, p=0.005, η²=0.275], indicating a large multivariate effect size.

Follow-up univariate ANOVAs revealed significant group effects on [list DVs that were significant; report F, p, η² for each]. Post-hoc Tukey HSD comparisons identified significant pairwise differences between groups [specify which pairs differ on which DV].

(b) Present results in table [4 marks]

M (SD)		Group 2

M (SD)		Fpη²
Useful__ (__)__ (__)__ (__)______
Difficulty__ (__)__ (__)__ (__)______
Importance__ (__)__ (__)__ (__)______
MANOVA (Wilks' Λ=0.526)F(6,56)=3.5380.0050.275

Fill in M (SD) from descriptive statistics. Report univariate η² per DV from summary.aov. Overall multivariate η² = 1 − 0.526 = 0.474.

Dataset: 6150 observations, 2050 participants, 511 districts. time is centred at week 6 (values: −6, 0, +6). Outcome: score (0–100).

2 (a) Model r1 — Random Intercept

Districts vary in baseline stress level; effect of time is fixed across districts.

Code 
r1 <- lmer(score ~ time + sex + (1 | districtid), data = df, REML = TRUE)

2.4 Interpretation

After accounting for the clustering of participants within districts, each one-week increase in time was associated with a statistically significant decrease of 0.621 units in stress score (95% CI: −0.700, −0.541; p < 0.001). Female participants had stress scores 3.71 units higher than male participants on average (95% CI: 2.87, 4.55; p < 0.001), after adjusting for time.

The random intercept standard deviation was 4.49, reflecting moderate between-district heterogeneity in baseline stress levels. The ICC = 0.076 indicates that approximately 7.6% of total variance in stress score is attributable to district-level differences — sufficient to justify LMM over standard linear regression, which would produce biased standard errors by ignoring this clustering (Snijders & Bosker, 2012).

The marginal R² (fixed effects only) was 0.046, while the conditional R² (fixed + random effects) was 0.119, indicating that district-level random intercepts explained an additional 7.3% of variance beyond the fixed effects.

3 (b) Model r2 — Random Intercept + Random Slope

Districts vary in both baseline stress and the rate of change over time.

3.4 Interpretation

Fixed effect estimates were stable relative to r1: time β = −0.649 (95% CI: −0.880, −0.418; p < 0.001) and sex (Female) β = 3.69 (95% CI: 3.23, 4.15; p < 0.001). The wider confidence interval for time compared to r1 correctly reflects the captured between-district variability in slopes.

The random intercept SD increased to 5.93 once the slope was freed, indicating that districts differ substantially in their baseline stress level. The random slope SD = 2.61 confirms that the effect of time on stress score varies meaningfully across districts — some districts show steep declines while others remain stable or increase. The positive intercept-slope correlation (r = 0.357) indicates that districts with higher baseline stress tended to show a slightly faster rate of decline over time.

The ICC = 0.751 in r2 reflects the proportion of variance (conditional on random slopes) attributable to district-level clustering — substantially higher than r1, because the random slope absorbs additional between-district variance. The marginal R² was 0.049 (fixed effects only) and the conditional R² was 0.763, meaning that fixed and random effects together explained 76.3% of the total variance in stress scores.

5 (d) Model Comparison

Code 
# Re-fit with ML for valid LRT
r1_ml <- update(r1, REML = FALSE)
r2_ml <- update(r2, REML = FALSE)

anova(r1_ml, r2_ml)
Data: df
Models:
r1_ml: score ~ time + sex + (1 | districtid)
r2_ml: score ~ time + sex + (1 + time | districtid)
      npar   AIC   BIC logLik -2*log(L)  Chisq Df Pr(>Chisq)    
r1_ml    5 51612 51646 -25801     51602                         
r2_ml    7 45933 45980 -22959     45919 5683.8  2  < 2.2e-16 ***
---
Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1
Code 
AIC(r1_ml, r2_ml)
      df      AIC
r1_ml  5 51612.36
r2_ml  7 45932.59
Code 
BIC(r1_ml, r2_ml)
      df      BIC
r1_ml  5 51645.98
r2_ml  7 45979.66

5.1 Interpretation

The likelihood ratio test strongly favoured r2 over r1 (χ²(2) = 5683.8, p < 2.2 × 10⁻¹⁶). Model r2 achieved a substantially lower AIC (45,933 vs 51,612; ΔAIC = 5,679) and BIC (45,980 vs 51,646; ΔBIC = 5,666), representing an exceptional improvement in fit.

This result confirms that the rate of change of stress score over time is not constant across districts — it varies substantially, as captured by the random slope SD of 2.61. Constraining all districts to the same time slope (as in r1) leads to serious model misspecification. The conditional R² rising from 0.119 (r1) to 0.763 (r2) further underscores the importance of the random slope in explaining variance in stress scores.

Model r2 (random intercept + random slope) is unambiguously the preferred model for these data (Barr et al., 2013; Snijders & Bosker, 2012).
